Something was terribly wrong. Damia Reinhart wasn’t bored.
In fact, she’d forgotten about boredom at all for the last, what, week? She’d lost count of the days since joining Wall Lord Ilvriss’ dig site. That was not to say that things were peachy all the time.
She’d nearly ripped a fingernail off her finger trying to swing a pickaxe the other day. During the first cattle raid, she’d gotten punched for the first time in her life. The Cowpat Plains stank.
—But she wasn’t bored. Or even that unhappy.
What was wrong with her?
 
——
 
Life on average, to Damia, was mediocre at best. You had good days—good moments, but if you took the totality of any given week or month, you were probably bored or restless, and most ‘enjoyments’ were brief sparks of amusement that faded in minutes, often during the act.
She had assumed that was what life was. Assumed that poorer people were just so simple they derived pleasures from equally boring actions, or that someone who wasn’t rich would luxuriate in the things she had plenty thereof.
She was wrong, of course. And now she saw it—Damia was the happiest she’d ever been, working for a Walled City in the middle of rancher no man’s land, the permanent scent of cow dung in the air. She got sweaty, her clothes got soiled, it wasn’t comfortable.
But she was alive.
Mind you, Binevy, Sule, and even Varnie probably didn’t enjoy their stay as much. Varnie was the happiest—mostly because she wasn’t dead—but Binevy seemed practically miserable at times when she slept in the unenchanted tents and had to queue up for a quick shower. She was a Reinhart’s servant and used to luxury.
So was Damia, but she was so busy she didn’t notice. Busy on what? Well, everything.
At first, it was what Ilvriss had wanted: liaising between the new workers and the Salazsarian employees. Damia hadn’t known what to do, but Ilvriss did have his adjutants. Say what you wanted about them—they weren’t as good as [Warriors], [Strategists], [Secretaries] in their specialties, they were hangers on and the south’s version of personal servants.
But they got stuff done.
“Wall Lord Ilvriss will meet at least daily with Clan Leader Plisce. We handle reports from [Forewomen] if an issue requires higher supervision—we shall route some of them to you, Lady Damia Reinhart, and see how you do. Is that acceptable?”
The Drake with the spectacles, Welsca, was the [Adjutant] who’d clearly drawn the short straw of having to deal with Damia. Or maybe she was just the most used to non-Salazsarians. She was not prepared for Damia’s smile.
“Absolutely, Adjutant Welsca, let me know if there’s anything I can do or how you operate.”
“Er, yes. Very good. Why don’t I show you a trial case? Ah, Foreman Lishi. Is it Miner Georgie again…?”
Some Drake in the female mining teams was a known problem to Welsca, who sighed hugely, then showed Damia the system. After that, she left Damia to wander the camps—with some supervision and limits on where she could go, obviously, and she clearly expected the [Lady] to be of little help.
It was not a fun job, managing disputes between species or dealing with the nebulous task of morale. However…
Damia found she was good at it.
 
——
 
“Alright, I have four verifiable complaints of speciesism I find credible, Adjutants. And I do note morale is low—I’ve surveyed sixty of the current [Miners], and they have issues with recreation, the output of other miners—and eight more complaints about this Miner Georgie—and I do have some recommendations for how to fix that. Who should I present my findings to?”
Damia appeared on her second day of work at Ilvriss’ officer tents, and the Drakes there stopped eating breakfast and eyed each other. They turned to Welsca, and the Drake woman picked up a piece of toast, inserted it into her mouth, and went hurrying towards the horses.
“Wall Lord Ilvriss wants me to run some deliveries to Liscor! Terribly sorry, I must be on my way. Lady Damia, why don’t you speak to my colleagues?”
She ran off, and Damia peered around brightly. She didn’t miss the looks the aides gave each other. She knew they thought she was some untrustworthy Reinhart. And when the sapphire-scaled Drake with a monocle and weak smile came over, she read his dismissive tone too.
“I’m [Economic Advisor] Merls, Lady Damia. We do take reports of anti-species behavior seriously. Not that I doubt your work, but four reports seems high.”
He was in his mid-fifties, some kind of career Drake in service to House Gemscale, if one who had even ridden on campaigns with Wall Lord Ilvriss. This was not his happiest hour, here in the camps fighting with Humans, and so he cleared some breakfast space and gave her a rather disinterested expression he tried to cover up.
She smiled at Merls and slid next to him with the paperwork.
“I’m delighted to meet you, Advisor Merls. Is that your rank? Do forgive me if I’m rude and correct me, please!”
“Er—I’d hate to correct a [Lady] of one of the Five Families.”
He blinked. She was sitting very close to him and gave him the kind of interested look that seemed genuine.
“Nonsense! I feel like you outrank me, here. You’ve been with the Wall Lord for a long time, correct?”
“Yes…it’s a Gemscale posting my father held.”
“Were you in the south when he clashed with Zel Shivertail? And met the Goblin Lord, Reiss?”
“I was—”
“Incredible! So your class is a combat one?”
The Drake glanced at the other adjutants, who blinked at him and then at Damia. He adjusted his rumpled suit.
“Well, I, uh, usually stay with the command staff, and we do not enter combat. Miss Welsca is rated for actual combat at need be, but she’s still support. I’ve seen a few engagements, though. The Goblin Lord did require all forces to be fighting. Terrible, terrible—er, let’s look at the reports. Foreman Dritmel? I recognize that. We’ve had a few reports, but nothing substantive. And the rest of these names…are you just taking the allegations at face value?”
He turned to the paperwork, and Damia shook her head.
“Absolutely not, Adjutant. I understand it’s a very difficult allegation to prove, and obviously no Drake—or Gnoll or Human, of course—would admit to it if pressed. Often, the slights are minor and as much as they can get away with, but it leads to a negative work environment.”
“Exactly, and it’s not even about species many times, but just personality, and speciesism is an easy charge to level—”
“Precisely, Merls. May I call you that? Is it too forwards? If you have time, I’d love to get to know you all as long as I’m here. That’s why I had my people blend in or camouflage themselves and personally shadow each individual complaint. I have eyewitness accounts and exact descriptions of further incidents. I witnessed Foreman Dritmel’s actions myself. Here.”
She flipped a page, and the [Adjutant] blinked. Damia went on smoothly.
“Most of it is verbal harassment, and I wasn’t sure how to address it. Do you have a strike policy or is it just a record of actions? I can keep monitoring each individual, though I may need a few more hands to help me.”
She waited as heads turned.
“I would also say morale is an issue if the number of complaints I overheard is any sign of worker productivity. If I’m overstepping or this is a serious matter, I can, of course, speak to Wall Lord Ilvriss at his convenience.”
She smiled, and no matter how hard they eyed her, or even activated Skills—one of the Drakes had [Measure Enthusiasm] as part of a set of headhunter Skills—it still looked like actual sincerity. One of Ilvriss’ [Aides] leaned over as the teal Drake choked and stared at something glowing on their claw.
“What, is she faking it, Ridbnell?”
“No. Her enthusiasm score is—it’s over 90.”
They stared at Damia like she was sick. But that was how it started.
 
——
 
The funny part was that Damia didn’t even really have any charisma Skills of note. Oh, sure, [Lesser Charisma] and [Basic Leadership], but those were literally bottom-rung Skills that just made you able to manage a basic group.
Her real secret was that she was genuinely enthusiastic and even interested.
Over the next few days, Damia not only made friends with Merls, Ridbnell, and the other Drakes, but when Welsca came back from her mini-vacation to Liscor, she found that Damia had settled more complaints than half of Ilvriss’ personal staff combined. And won over her peers!
Not all of it was hard to do. Damia was a Reinhart, so when it came to sneaking up on people or being underhanded, she had a natural advantage. Even when they’d been subdued by Magnolia’s Skill, the family had crept up on each other and weaponized secrets and gossip. By contrast, hiding speaking stones in work sites, having someone blend in to provoke an allegedly bad manager’s ire, all of that was simple. Plus, she had Varnie.
The amused [Gang Leader] and her remaining people who’d decided to follow Damia were able to use hiding Skills to listen in. And eyewitness accounts proved a lot more incidents were occurring than Salazsar’s staff wanted to believe.
Half of it was discomfort between species, though. North and south not liking each other. To that end, Damia had set up communal events where members of multiple groups would stop working, have a refreshing drink of something sweet, snacks, and watch the scrying orb, play some cards, or just chat.
The local [Ranchers] played games with horseshoes—a variation where you had a pole and attached the metal rods at varying heights and lengths instead of a single pole on the ground. Rather to their surprise, some of the Lischelles’ spies saw the game become an actual pastime in camp.
Not only that—several [Cleaners] were dealing with bug infestations in some of the camps, and new linens were being bought due to the current ones being irrevocably torn. Small things. Necessary things. But the attention to detail was spooky.
Also, why were Ilvriss’ staff approving all this? Some [Lady] from the north came swanning in, tried to make changes—Welsca had assumed her co-workers would shut down any unnecessary expenditure of gold.
However, this too had a simple answer: Merls, Ridbnell, and a third of Welsca’s co-workers were somewhat smitten with the [Lady].
“She has to be leading them on.”
Welsca eyed the [Lady] chatting with a very attentive Merls at the breakfast table. Where she apparently had breakfasts. One of her female co-workers, Sasni, stopped writing an approval form for one of Damia’s requests over her breakfast.
“Welsca, this breakfast is to die for. Go back and bring more food from Liscor, please! What about Damia leading Merls on? The only thing she’s doing is pitting him against Ridbnell and Gless. Suits me. I’m tired of Ridbnell and Gless chatting me up nonstop.”
“Merls is more than twice her age. She’s just flirting so he helps her out.”
The [Adjutant] insisted. She wasn’t that protective of her coworkers, having also had the delightful Drake seniority experience with superiors chatting her up and not being able to blow them off. Thankfully, she outranked everyone but actual Wall Lords here. However, Sasni eyed Welsca.
“If she’s doing that, she’s going so far into the act I’d say it reaches right back around to authentic. She had a personal dinner with Merls out on the town last night. Four full hours, and he came back preening over himself.”
“…What?”
Another shrug.
“Apparently, she was all over him, asking about his career with House Gemscale, the Goblin Lord, what he thought about the north, south, everything! It’s eerie how much she seems to like us.”
“Huh. And the paperwork?”
“She does it better than half the new interns. Look.”
Sasni pushed the papers over, and Welsca eyed the neat handwriting and checked boxes in the report forms. She shook her head.
 
——
 
It was true. Damia Reinhart was working hard. And that was not to say her job was fun.
Truthfully, after the third time you busted a [Foreman] for speciesism, you were probably done with any ‘fun’ elements or even rewarding feelings. Same with cleaning tents or reviewing reports of unsanitary conditions and finding someone had crapped in the corner of a worksite and shovelled dirt over it.
Some of the tasks Damia handled were boring. It was just that she didn’t find it entirely boring because she was here.
In Wall Lord Ilvriss’ camp where he was digging up something that was 372% related to The Wandering Inn! Not just that. Damia was all-too-willing to take on more work.
Like…the first thing she approached Welsca with was a proposal.
“Miss Welsca, I have reports in each Dig Site about teams not pulling their weight.”
“Ah, that’s a known issue, Miss Damia. It’s inevitable and accounted for.”
Really? According to both managers and grunts, some teams were working at literally a quarter of the output of other teams! But Welsca barely glanced at the numbers, and Damia’s brain went—
Ping. [Remember That].
Another intelligence Skill. However, Damia pretended not to notice and smiled.
“Even so, Adjutant, that kind of thing hurts morale. Why work if another team can earn the same pay by putting in no effort? See my proposal here.”
She handed over the paper, and Welsca wiped at her spectacles, sighing.
“I’m aware, Lady Damia, but we are not going to reallocate teams or—a contest?”
“Everyone loves a good competition, Welsca. I’m thinking a five-day holiday to Liscor and all the connected cities as the prize. It doesn’t even have to be a high monetary budget. Just say it’s paid time. Let’s say one team wins, this is how much it costs…and imagine how much they’ll race!”
Welsca hesitated.
“We…do need to dig. You know we have an, ah, a monster issue? We still need to dig around them and delve deeper—but a contest would lead to teams cutting corners and potentially put [Advance Miners] in danger.”
“Oh, of course—”
The [Lady] deflated, and Welsca, despite herself, bit her lip and sighed.
“However, we do have an established framework in Salazsar. Teams have to pass safety checks, and if it’s in areas where we’re just widening shafts and it’s the amount of dirt shifted…it’s an intriguing proposal, but it would require a lot of announcements and organization!”
“I’m happy to help, Adjutant. If it’s above our pay grades, I could present the idea to Wall Lord Ilvriss…”
And that was the secret. Damia’s helpful, beaming smile made the [Adjutant] eye her incredulously. She turned, and one of her friends mouthed ‘ninety-four’ at her. Because Damia was genuinely motivated.
If she took on more work and did enough, it was inevitable she’d need to speak to Wall Lord Ilvriss. Which, at this moment, was one of her crowning goals.
Meet the Wall Lord again, get in with The Wandering Inn, and potentially have dinner with him. He seemed quite good at what he did, and if Damia was going to make a list of possible partners…well, she still had to scope out a few other famous people from the inn, but she was quite open to dalliances.
 
——
 
The one silver lining to being a former [Incestuous Lady] was that Damia had zero concern about her maidenly values. If anything, her mother would have probably said any rendezvous besides Wernel could only inflate Damia’s worth in that very simplistic regard.
Plus, Damia had never had people vying for her attention in any way beyond her power or fame herself. She was writing down a list of ideas for morale as she chatted with Binevy and Varnie in their shared tent.
“Let’s see. Can we do a talent competition or—or music one like the Battle of the Bands? We don’t have much instrumentation, but everyone can sing, right? Let me talk to—who’s my contact today? Right, Ridbnell today. Binevy, you were researching worker productivity in other continents. Noelictus does musicians in the fields, and there are storytellers in weaveries in Stitch-folk cities? Anything else you found out?”
Binevy handed her a selection of texts she’d paid to have copied out, and Varnie flipped a knife up and down, eying Damia with much the same expression as the [Head Maid]. Then Damia glanced at her.
“Varnie, if you were going to have a one night stand with Merls, Gless, or Ridbnell, who would you choose?”
The [Gang Leader] missed her timing on the dagger and it nearly ran her through her bared stomach. She rolled out of the hammock she’d set up, swearing.
“Are you serious?”
“Lady Damia, they’re Drakes! Not even well-placed Drakes!”
Binevy appeared horrified. Damia twirled a quill, scowling at Binevy.
“Oh hush, I’d make it clear it was temporary, and what do I have to lose? It’s better than Wernel.”
“Well…yes…”
It was really hard for Binevy to argue with that. Varnie just folded her arms.
“You have to be doing all this for a reason. You think Salazsar’s going to make you that rich, is that it? I’ve been trying to figure out your game, boss lady. Savin’ me, that’s a proper Reinhart move, and I’m grateful. But as far as I can tell, the Wall Lord’s not payin’ you to be running around like a lapdog. What’s this for?”
It was good to have employees who weren’t yes-men. Sule was the most obedient and straightforward of the trio, but Binevy’s glance made Damia chuckle to herself. She knew that despite her Skills, Varnie’s loyalty was no sure thing.
She was a Face, and gratitude wore out. Binevy…
“Are you still reporting to my Aunt Cosoi, Binevy? Or have other members of the family made you offers? Is Aunt Cosoi alive, by the way? I hope she is. You’re allowed to take money to spy on me, just not tell them anything valuable. The same for you, Varnie.”
Binevy jumped and was so visibly rattled Damia congratulated herself.
“M-milady Damia, I—”
Varnie blinked, and Damia smiled. It had come to her when she was dozing one time while using [Think Harder].
“It’s fine. It’s the House Reinhart way. Just remember—I did save Varnie, and I’ve earned a good amount of gold in record time, levelling no less than six times this month and counting. You want to back the right horse? That’s me.”
She blew on the ink, stoppered the inkwell, and turned.
“As for why I’m here, Varnie, you’re right. It’s not about the gold. I want to get in with Wall Lord Ilvriss.”
“In his sheets or good graces?”
“At least the latter. The former if I really like him or it helps me. I’m a Reinhart, Varnie. Why are you giving me that look? Haven’t you traded on everything you could to get to where you were?”
The former leader of the Sorcerous Blades folded her scarred arms. She was a veteran of knife-fights, a very dangerous person with them according to the other [Gang Boss]—just not against heavily-armored Golems.
“Talk to the Sisters of Chell if you want to exchange stories like that. I never slept around to get where I was. It’s a fast way to lose respect.”
“Well then, I shall tactically do so when I feel like it. Or for fun. The point I was making is that Wall Lord Ilvriss has more to offer than gold. He’s connected to The Wandering Inn.”
“Oh Five Families, it’s your inn theory again.”
Binevy groaned. Varnie just listened as Damia explained.
“The Wandering Inn has what we all want. Stop shaking your head, Binevy. You’ve levelled too! It makes people high-level, Varnie. Even Faces! Look at all the guests of the inn and even friends. All of them have levelled over ten times in one year. Octavia Cotton—now Saliss of Lights’ best apprentice and inventor of Occillium. Liska—you saw her yourself! Coach Joseph—and the adventuring teams the inn helped out are approaching Named-rank!”
Varnie squatted down, picked her dagger up off the floor, and began flipping it again, but thoughtfully.
“Or dead. That woman’s left a trail of bodies longer’n most average [Gang Leaders]. You don’t want to mess with the inn, kid.”
“What do you know?”
The [Gang Boss] shrugged. She was technically an [Underworld Leader of Stabbed Corpses]—a very awesome-sounding class. Level 36. A full Face, even if not the biggest one around, come from the north. Her gang had been small but deadly. She had barely fourteen of her people left, but Damia had a real staff in the works with Cosoi’s servants as well. Security and staff.
She needed more money to pay for all their wages, but at least right now she was doing okay with Ilvriss’ money. Actually…that gave Damia an idea, and she wrote something down. Her brain seemed to be getting better at thinking. She could do it longer, and she had more and more creative ideas the more she practiced.
“What do I know about the inn? Just a bit. Names’n faces. You don’t gauge places like that by what they do in the gangs. More like who they leave behind. And she’s got names. Not done in by her, but…there’s Mister Soot. Ripped to shreds in Liscor. Used to run the place. A full squad of the Hatmen and the Sisters wiped out by a single Goblin. The Gentlemen Callers walked up to her front door, and apparently even they got rocked. Several high-level brothers—Crimshaw the Bowler ate it. Or not—there’s a weird rumor around that…oh, and the Bloodfeast Raiders want her dead.”
“Is that last part as impressive? They want everyone dead.”
Varnie grinned mirthlessly.
“They want her dead, and she ain’t. And the inn’s not burned and everyone dead. That’s a warning in itself. So yeah, I buy it. The inn’s got power. But it’ll scorch ya if you can’t harness it. Got a plan?”
“First, I need to find it.”
Damia’s eyes glittered. She grew so excited when she thought of going through the doors of the inn she had to get up and pace.
“I-if I got in, and you know the front hallway’s trapped? If I got in, Varnie, that’s what counts. The inn wipes out its enemies, but I’m not one of them. I could just be useless to them—that’s the second-worst outcome. But so what? I’ll take being Jeweled!”
Neither of the other two got the reference, so Damia had to tell them the Jewel story. Varnie’s eyes rose, and she actually recalled the addendum to the famous quest to find Mershi. She listened attentively, sold on Damia’s ideas.
…But she did stop listening after the fourth inn story.
 
——
 
Damia didn’t spend all her time helping Ilvriss, of course. He had a very brief meeting with her at the end of the first work cycle and personally handed her a contract with her pay sent to the Merchant’s Guild—and a sizable bonus.
“I’ve been exceptionally impressed, Lady Damia. If you’re willing, I’d love for you to continue on in your posting. Welsca and my people had nothing bad to say about you; quite the contrary. I only regret I can’t spend as much time here—I’m always riding back to Liscor, and it is a hectic time.”
For some reason, he had a miner’s helmet on his head. And a pickaxe by his side.
Very…magical items. Varnie had been biting her knuckles bloody all day. She’d told Damia every instinct in her and her people’s bodies had screamed to steal those items.
Damia wore the most pleasant, helpful smile possible.
“I’m delighted to hear it, Wall Lord! If there’s anything I can do to help? Is there a project I could assist with, or the excavation troubles? I’m no [Strategist], but I do have a higher-level fighter under my command in Varnie. If I can do anything—”
Unfortunately, for once, she miscalculated. Ilvriss had been somewhat off, and now he glanced at Welsca, who had a second helmet and miner’s gear on her. His claw stole possessively to the pickaxe, and he cleared his throat.
“That won’t be necessary, Lady Damia. I do appreciate your willingness, but we’re engaged in highly sensitive operations. Major Shellc has it well in hand, and Captain Asrira is also forming a new taskforce—I do appreciate the offer, however. Someone to keep the gears running smoothly is already helpful enough.”
Translation: he didn’t trust her. Damia’s face fell.
“I understand, Wall Lord. But if you had the chance, I’d love to ask a few questions of you? Purely as a student of the south and—Liscor, really. Do you have an evening free?”
That interested him.
“I did want to speak to you and Clan Leader Plisce more in person. Welsca, do I have any openings in the evening?”
Yes, yes, y—Welsca snapped a folder shut.
“I’m afraid that you need to return to The Wandering Inn, Wall Lord. One must dance away to Liscor.”
“Ah, damn. My aching shins. Perhaps after that, Miss Damia?”
“Of…course, Wall Lord.”
Damia deflated and wondered what the hell that meant. It had to be something incredible. Then she smiled and locked eyes with Welsca.
The one member of staff who wasn’t her best friend at the moment eyed Damia. She walked Damia out, and the [Lady] whispered to her as they left the tent.
“I thought we were being friendly, Welsca! What was that about, may I ask?”
The Drake [Adjutant] pointedly glanced Damia up and down. The [Lady] had put on a formal dress, despite it not fitting with the dirty plains and tents; perfume; and styled her hair after bathing.
“Moved on so quickly from Merls, Lady Reinhart?”
“Moving on implies I didn’t intend anything more than a stop on my travels, Miss Welsca.”
“Ah, well then…your affection for Wall Lord Ilvriss is noted. However, he is still mourning Lieutenant Periss.”
“I know that! I wasn’t pushing—”
Welsca leaned over.
“You’re not the only person who is holding a [Light] spell for the Wall Lord, Lady Damia. Sadly—if anyone has a shot, it’s that dratted [Princess] at the inn.”
Damia gasped. Did she mean…? Welsca wore a sour expression.
“Not that I think it’s the most likely in any case, but I know a bad engagement when I see one.”
“But you—? Then why are you sabotaging me and sending him to Liscor?”
The Drake adjusted her spectacles.
“Misery prefers company, Lady Damia. I’m dangling off a cliff, and you’re dancing on the edge. I’d prefer to pull you off so we can hang together.”
That—was a very Drake and Reinhart way of doing things. Damia stuck out her tongue at Welsca, and the Drake did likewise. Then they laughed, and Damia realized she and Welsca might become actual friends.
“You’d make a lovely Reinhart if you were meaner. Fine, you win. I’ll be back.”
Welsca adjusted her helmet.
“Today’s not a good day, anyways. It’s rather—delicate. But you are working hard for House Gemscale, and I am paying attention. If you really are searching for company, may I recommend a brothel? I wouldn’t normally, but we have an employee working there…”
She sighed. Damia snorted at the strange suggestion.
“What? I thought you’d tell me to give Merls a second chance.”
“Me? Ancestors, no, he’s been insufferable all day. I’m sabotaging him too. Ever met a Level 50+ [Courtesan]?”
“A what?”
 
——
 
Damia was a bit let down by the short interaction with Ilvriss despite all her hard work. She told herself he was clearly in the middle of something super interesting and important…she just had to work harder.
But she had done her best, and she was a teensy bit depressed. So she went to a brothel.
Mostly because she really wanted to meet the legend herself, Xesci. The [Courtesan of Change] greeted Damia herself, and she did not disappoint.
“Hello, Lady Damia. I’ll just call you Damia since you clearly want to be my friend. Sadly, I’m not working. Managing is a full-time job. And you’d like…Roubles. A classic farm-lad. Huge shoulders. Very enthusiastic.”
The Drake held up a little book with a headshot and details, and Damia peered at the rather fetching young man, then smiled.
“I, ah, I rather came to meet you, Miss Xesci. I’d love to make your acquaintance.”
Xesci glanced Damia up and down again. Her brows bobbed up and down.
“Yes, you would. Fascinating. Few people feel that way. Unfortunately for you, I don’t like being desired as I am.”
“What? I’m sure I don’t think I was implying something—scandalous.”
Damia hesitated, because she was no stranger to the scandalous acts two women could get up to together, but Xesci was a Drake. However, the [Courtesan]’s smile was brief.
“Yes, you were. Wernel’s lost his appeal now no one cares, hasn’t he? Fair warning, Miss Damia, you’ve been poisoned.”
“How do you know about Wern—tell Wall Lord Ilvriss that’s long behind me, please! Wall Lord Ilvriss employs you? Do you have any interest in working for me?”
Damia was on the backfoot, confused. Who is this woman? Is this a Level 50 person? She’s so amazing. Xesci just walked around Damia, smiling like a serpent rather enjoying playing with a chick who’d wandered onto her coils.
“You’re so funny. I’ve met dozens of Reinharts, and you’re the happiest one.”
Those familiar words. Damia spat back, because she couldn’t help it.
“No one knows Reinharts. My family’s been gone from the world for nearly two decades! You don’t know us!”
Everyone had this image of Reinharts as evil, treacherous snakes, and there was some truth to it. But were they all—? Yet Xesci just raised one eyebrow.
“That wasn’t an attack. The rest was, but I don’t exaggerate. I have known Reinharts. Intimately. I’m older than you think. Listen to what I said. You are the happiest Reinhart. Self-serving, heroic, bitter and cynical, rising, falling—at this moment, you are the happiest one. Your family is more clever than they are amoral, without loyalty to land, passion for creation, love of sea, or infatuation with stone hearts. Cleverness alone is bitter. So you should treasure this, Lady Damia.”
She left the young woman speechless for a second until Xesci winked.
“At least for now. And you are also the one Reinhart who needs my services least. It’s just what I said, Damia—”
She stepped behind a lantern that hid her face for just a second, and Damia saw her form…ripple. When Xesci stepped forwards, a copy of Damia Reinhart stood there.
Perfect. Damia recoiled, gasped, and Damia-Xesci smiled, a bit rueful.
“Poisoned. Few things can poison a Reinhart, but this one might last long.”
“W-what kind of poison…?”
“Affection. Now you’ve tasted genuine respect and affection, paying for the version of it my class provides won’t be nearly as sweet. And it was already wearing thin. Go back to your work, Lady Damia. You can find everything you want without paying for it here.”
Damia Reinhart backed up, genuinely unnerved. She stared at Xesci, and the [Courtesan] made shooing motions. Only when Damia had fled did Xesci pause, then slap her forehead.
“…And I just turned away another customer. Wonderful. Lady Damia?”
She changed back to her form and shouted. The [Lady] whirled, eyes locked on Xesci, and the [Courtesan] shouted.
“Send your [Bodyguard] here—and your [Head Maid]! I’ll give them a discount!”
She gave Damia a thumbs up as the girl gaped, bewildered.
 
——
 
“Damn, Xesci the Changer herself’s in the brothel? I might have to visit.”
Varnie was squatting by the cattle fence as Damia leaned against it. The [Lady] was doing a bit of personal work with Varnie and a sheet of paper. Binevy and Sule were checking out a brothel.
Everyone had just been paid, though Varnie was only flipping a gold coin up and down and not looking impressed. This was not good money for a [Gang Boss], and even Damia wanted more. However…
“Tell me about her later, Varnie. We’re still in super-helpful mode, and since I don’t feel like making friends with Xesci is the right option…”
“You’re right about that. She’s not the violent type, but people who went against her tended to get hurt. By people who like her. Real shapechanger, that one, or so I’ve heard.”
“You don’t know the half of it. Anyways, here’s how much gold I can spend.”
Damia held up her calculations, and Varnie eyed the number.
“Right. That buys you one mid-grade ring or lower-grade weapon. One nice set of steel plate, maybe, and a good amount of plain steel weapons.”
Damia’s heart sank.
“I know magic’s expensive, but—really?”
“Sorry. You want to gear us out like Gold-rankers? You need tens of thousands of gold for a team. Wall Lord Ilvriss don’t pay that much.”
“He barely paid for more than all your salaries! This is all I have from my time in House Reinhart, but you heard what attacked him! Hunter-Killer Terland Golems and super undead?”
The [Miners] talked, even if Damia wasn’t caught up or in the loop. Varnie grimaced.
“Thought I’d never have to fight a Golem again after you sprung me from the noose. I’d rather put my head back in one and have Lord Xitegen hoist the rope than fight House Terland’s assassin-Golems. Every now and then, some [Gang Leader] decides to make a stir in First Landing or Terland lands and not do it smooth and gentle. They make sure you know it was one of their Golems. Mostly, it’s the footprints in what remains of their skulls.”
Damia shuddered.
“Well, brainstorm with me. I need you and your people to arm yourselves for potential engagements with either group.”
Varnie shot Damia an alarmed glance, and Damia waved her hands.
“I won’t throw you away! You’re irreplaceable, Varnie! Reinharts waste nothing, especially people we actually like.”
“You’re all heart. Actually, that makes me feel better than if you were tellin’ me you were my best friend. It’s a big ask, though…”
The woman scratched at her raven hair, and Damia shrugged.
“Well, it’s necessary! Think of it as self-defense. Remember the half-Elf that started this whole mess? Do you really want to be unprotected, even if you and I parted ways?”
“Right. Fuck. I ain’t got a clue what to do with him, but I guess…work up from Terland Golems. Damnit, my granny said I’d hate the new era, and she were right. Okay, the problem’s armor.”
She twirled one of her daggers as Damia nodded, and they conferred.
“I can cut a man’s throat fast. [Free Slash] plus one of these and poison means I end one person before the fight starts. But that doesn’t work on something that ain’t got a throat or has armor on. And anything out of arm’s reach has the drop on me and my people. We like nice, confined spaces. This place is open. And it stinks.”
She spat, and Damia nodded.
“So what would you do if you knew you had to clash with Lord Xitegen…? Give me something to go off of, and I’ll take it from there.”
Varnie thought, sighing. She rubbed at her face.
“If I had to? The biggest damn crossbows I could find. Nets and traps. Slow ‘em down and break the armor. Only, even crossbows won’t pierce Golem armor, not unenchanted. A warhammer and steel wire nets? Not sure that works on undead.”
“You don’t have…some clever underworld tricks?”
A half-glare, then a laugh.
“Tricks? Sure, tons! Mostly baiting it into another gang you don’t like! There’s all kinds of tricks—but all of it’s too expensive, kid. Like there’s this trick where you take twenty wands, put ‘em together in a box, and fire them off. Blasts an entire corridor to slag. But it costs so much and burns out all the wands—once they start firing, you can’t stop ‘em cause of the magical interference. Or you can buy a potion and become an [Expert Swordswoman]—for ten minutes. You can’t afford even the potion.”
Damia saw the problem. She gnawed at her lip.
“Can’t you, I don’t know, use your underworld contacts and call in some favors? We need something, Varnie.”
The [Gang Boss] pulled a face.
“I suppose I’m in this until my debt’s paid…and nearly dancing the hemp jig makes you think you should really call in those favors while you’re alive. Argh, fine. I’ll see what I can do. Give me eight hundred gold pieces, and don’t come complaining to me if I turn up empty-handed.”
Damia wrote Varnie a letter authorizing her to take eight hundred gold pieces out of her account and gave the woman her signet ring of House Reinhart. Varnie promised to ride to Invrisil with six of her people and come back within the week with whatever she had.
The [Lady] saw her off and then was eating lunch when it occurred to her she’d given a [Gang Boss] a huge sum of gold, her signet ring, and sent her to Invrisil without any oversight. Damia told herself that Varnie honored her debts, that she had [Loyalty of the Low], and that if Varnie wanted to betray Damia…now was the perfect moment to ride off with all that stuff.
 
——
 
Wall Lord Ilvriss was just past his Hoarding incident, and he’d decided to put in some actual work around the expedition. His first stop was Lady Damia; he’d wanted to know why she was actually here, and she had been helpful.
He found her at one of the mess tables and began to approach—then halted.
“Stupid Damia! You just had to stop thinking! Stupid, stupid, stupid—”
The aforementioned [Lady] was crying, hitting her head with her fists, and banging it on the table. Wall Lord Ilvriss hesitated, then decided he’d check on her later.
You had to admit, though, she was highly entertaining. Real Erin energy, if not quite the same. A mix of Erin and Ryoka, he supposed. Erin positivity and Ryoka failure and self-recriminations.
 
——
 
Damia stopped beating herself up because she’d given herself a cut on the head from the wooden table. And people were staring. Then she turned, saw Wall Lord Ilvriss walking away, and realized he’d come by to talk to her and seen her.
She was glancing around for the nearest Dig Site to throw herself over the safety railings when it happened. The Wall Lord was walking off nonchalantly, pretending not to have seen a wailing [Lady], when the air—flickered. He half-pivoted, putting a hand on his sword hilt, and Damia’s heart lurched.
“Stealth Golem! Watch out!”
She leapt up, screaming. Damia whirled, but Varnie was gone and Sule was in the brothel! She saw the Rubirel Guard jerk. Damia began to run.
“Evacuate the area! It’s a Golem attack! Protect the Wall L—”
The shimmering air resolved as Ilvriss backed up, and then it materialized into…a little white Gnoll girl, semi-transparent and wearing a kilt, stepped out of the air. Her head whipped around, and she held up a notecard.
Hey, Ilvriss, your stupid anti-scrying rings make this harder than it should be. What’s up?
Her paw blurred as Damia stopped running and froze. Mrsha wrote rapidly and held up a card.
Whoa! Golem attack!? Where? I’ll tell Mom!
Everyone was eyeing Damia Reinhart. The [Lady] halted as Ilvriss stopped spinning around, realized her mistake, and put a hand over his mouth. She covered her face and tried to pretend she didn’t exist.
 
——
 
Damia and the Rubirel Guard had to clarify the situation. She did so, apologizing, beet red, about to cry.
Only one thing kept her anchored and from running to hide under her bed in her tent, and it was the little Gnoll girl standing and talking with Ilvriss. Well, writing in the air with a glowing paw.
Mrsha du Marquin.
The Mrsha du Marquin, the Doombearer who’d faced off against Chieftain Xherw, the one who’d exposed the entire conspiracy, the Gnoll who’d survived Raskghar and the Goblin Lord—
And who’d seen Damia making a complete toad of herself in public. Even so, the [Lady] slunk closer. She lingered around the Wall Lord and Gnoll girl’s position, pretending to be walking, stretching, watching them and trying to listen, but the Rubirel Guard were there. Were they…peeking at her?
 
——
 
Yo, Ilvriss. Forsooth and all that. I actually came by to tell you that Mom wants to know if you’ll be back tomorrow. Apparently, the Players of Celum released some new play, and the Players of Invrisil are putting it on. It’s ‘adult’, so I’m banned. Nanette’s gonna weasel her way in, but you down? How’s the shit digging? Also, who’s the weird [Lady]?
Mrsha the Casual was checking up on Ilvriss, and her writing was a bit less polished than it used to be. But Ilvriss just grinned at her in genuine good humor. He chuckled.
“Isn’t she funny? She’s Damia Reinhart, and she’s decided to help my expedition. I thought it was some kind of Reinhart ploy, but Welsca said she’s been nothing but a credit. I wish you were here, though. Life is quite dull—just complaints and problems.”
Mrsha’s head snapped around, and a [Lady] went walking by and admiring…cowpats or something.
Did you just say Damia Reinhart?
“Oh no, don’t tell me she’s actually a problem. I know she’s Cecille Reinhart’s daughter, but she’s seemed to be—”
You should talk to her. She might be…useful. Or dangerous. But we should talk to her. Why the heck is she here?
The fact that Mrsha knew Damia’s name sounded warning bells in Ilvriss’ head. He bent over.
“Are you sure it’s safe? What should I know? Is it…the [Palace]?”
For a second, Mrsha gave Ilvriss a weary gaze beyond her age, then she shrugged and wrote.
My guy, everything is the palace in the end. In this case? Yes, but nothing I know indicates she’s bad, necessarily. Hmm. Let me talk to her first. I’ll be ultra random and Erin-weird, and then you can be professional.
“Good plan.”
They nodded at each other, and Mrsha cleared her throat. She massaged her face, made a bleh sound in the back of her throat, then got on all fours. She scampered over to Damia, did a somersault, then sat up with the cutest face she had as Ilvriss watched.
It seemed like it hurt Mrsha’s soul to do that, if her momentary grimace was any indication. She wrote in the air with a glowing paw.
Hewwo! I’m Mrsha the Ultimately Dangerous! Salutations and give me all your gold! I need a cookie! Pwease and tanks! Are you a good person? Your Mom eats Creler eggs. Why are you here?
It was like…a parody of the actual Gnoll girl’s antics in the past. It was still probably effective to anyone Mrsha hadn’t met, but it was slightly undercut by Wall Lord Ilvriss.
He had begun wheezing as he read the glowing words. Now he was laughing so hard he couldn’t actually speak, silently dying as he held onto one of the Rubirel Guard’s shoulders. Mrsha glared at him and gave him the finger while Damia read the words.
Her eyes widened. Mrsha waited, her most cutesy expression straining her face, resolving to toss Calescent’s death-spices in Ilvriss’ next meal. But for all her wariness, even she couldn’t predict what Lady Damia did next. The [Lady] finished reading, then her eyes locked on Mrsha, and her face lit up like the sun.
“Miss Mrsha! I’m such a big—c-can I have an autograph? No, wait, this is the [World’s Eye Theatre], isn’t it? I’m seeing it in person! You can have all the gold you—I could write you a voucher if you want gold! I’ll send it to the Merchant’s Guild in Liscor! My mother is terrible, I apologize so much for her! I just want to help with the dig! May I shake your paw?”
She held out a trembling hand to Mrsha, and the Gnoll girl leaned back in the face of the seemingly-genuine delight on Damia’s face. The [Lady] tentatively touched Mrsha’s ghostly paw, dancing around her.
“And you’re using magic! Is anyone else there? I’m so sorry, I know this is inn business. I promise I’m not trying to do anything to the inn! I swear! It—oh my—”
Her eyes rolled up in her head. She put a hand to her head, and then she collapsed on the ground. Ilvriss stopped laughing and began to run over. Mrsha stared at Damia.
Huh. I did not expect that.
 
——
 
Damia came round in moments, and her embarrassment at passing out from sheer delight was only matched by having Mrsha and Ilvriss peering over her.
“I—I’m so sorry, I’m a mess, and I just—this is everything I’ve wanted.”
What, everything you’ve wanted? Meeting the White Rat of Liscor—no relation to the more successful Ice Squirrel—and the Lord of Purple himself?
“Hey.”
Ilvriss fake-nudged Mrsha, but they were watching her as Damia struggled to her feet.
“No, I—I’m just an admirer of the inn, Miss Mrsha. I didn’t know you existed until, what, a few weeks ago? But when I found out what you did—why wouldn’t I be?”
Is she crazy? Mrsha and Ilvriss exchanged a look. Fans of The Wandering Inn existed, but they were rare and usually original citizens of Liscor or people who’d become friends.
“You…found out about The Wandering Inn and liked what you heard, Lady Damia? Really? That’s what led you here?”
This was the first thing Ilvriss couldn’t actually believe. However, Damia insisted.
“You must have tons of adherents, surely! After all the inn has done for Liscor, the region, the world? Miss Erin Solstice invented <Quests>, didn’t she?”
Whoa, she does know us.
Mrsha was vaguely alarmed. She’d heard Damia was intelligent, but not…enthusiastic. But then, her learning about one version of Damia in the Goblin King’s future had been just an aside from Xitegen. Even so, she had to ask.
No one likes the inn. Liscor remembers the good Erin did, and the bad, Miss Damia. And there’s plenty of both. The only ‘fans’ she has are real weirdos like that Gnoll guy who thinks she’s a ‘sexual fiend’. Ever met him, Ilvriss?
“…No. Is that a joke?”
I wish. If you’re a real fan of the inn, uh, prove it! What’s my last name?
“Stone Spears or du Marquin? Or Demas Metal or—I forget your birth mother’s tribe! Argh! But you wouldn’t claim them, so—the first two?”
Damia appeared uncertain and worried, but Mrsha and Ilvriss exchanged another glance. Oh shit, she’s good. Ilvriss tried, growing genuinely fascinated.
“Er, that’s broadly correct. But if you know the inn—you can’t have visited. However, any fan would know the inn had a [Bartender], once.”
“Drassi Tewing, who became Channel 2’s anchor—well, she was Channel 1 with Sir Relz first—after confronting them about the Yellow Rivers disease!”
Damn. Maybe she was legit. Mrsha kept scratching her head.
Alright, you know the inn. But…you actually want to visit? People die there, you know. We’ve got Goblins, Antinium, undead, and maggots. Bird always has one or two in her rooms. She’s actually gross as heck sometimes.
Lumping Goblins and Antinium together with undead and maggots got a shoe thrown at Mrsha’s head by Picker. She took cover as someone hucked a dustrag at her too, and Damia’s face lit up.
“Are they there? Can I meet them? I don’t have a problem with either!”
“Your family participated in the Sacrifice of Roses. At least some did.”
This time, Ilvriss’ skepticism actually grew. He saw Damia Reinhart hesitate, and her eyes flicked to his face, then Mrsha’s, a moment before a smile appeared on her face. Both noticed that.
She’s still a Reinhart. She was thinking of a lie. But then Damia noticed their skeptical faces and drew in a breath.
“I…I was just a child when the Goblin King rampaged, and I didn’t know anyone personally who died. I was born after the Antinium came to Izril. Be it so selfish, but I don’t care about what feels like ancient history to me. Is—that sounds worse than I wish it to be.”
Ilvriss, as someone who’d lived through both Antinium Wars, even if he wasn’t that old, felt a pang at Damia’s comments. It was Mrsha who frowned.
Really? Well, here’s an Antinium. Say hi, Goldbody, Picker.
The Antinium and Goblin warily edged into frame. Instantly, there was a shout of horror; some Salazsarian Drakes had seen the two and freaked out. Goldbody wavered, and Picker eyed Damia. And the Reinhart’s eyes widened—then she held out a hand.
“H-hello. I’m Damia Reinhart. I’m no Goblinfriend…but I could be, possibly? I’d like to meet the Antinium of Liscor too, but I think they’re all afraid of me.”
She held out her hand to Picker, bending over, and then Ilvriss felt his neck spines rising and scales tingling.
Could a [Lady] fake the genuine excitement and nerves like that? Even Cecille Reinhart’s daughter?
He began to believe this was a familiar thing he’d found before at Erin’s inn, but different and welcome. It was Mrsha whose face screwed up with suspicion and worry. She drew herself up and put an arm out.
When she wrote, Damia turned to Mrsha and hesitated. Because the girl said:
No offense, Lady Damia, but I don’t buy it. Say you like the inn. Okay. You’re still a Reinhart, and with respect, I’ve never heard of a Reinhart doing anything that doesn’t help them in some way. Magnolia sealed you away for a reason. Can you swear you don’t have a grudge against her or want something from the inn?
Again, the [Lady] froze, and she seemed genuinely nervous. Afraid…that they wouldn’t like her? The Wall Lord and Gnoll Doombearer studied her, and Damia took a quick breath. She hesitated, then she sank to one knee and looked Mrsha in the eyes.
When she spoke, her voice was aquiver, and it felt like she was confessing something intimate and scary. But this was it. She might not get another chance if Mrsha vanished. And Ilvriss—Damia glanced up at him and then saw an [Innkeeper] with a hat full of flames. A girl waving a white flag.
The stuff of stories. So Damia told them the truth, her only card to play.
“It’s true. I don’t want to visit The Wandering Inn for selfless reasons. Does anyone in this world do anything for others without a reward?”
Yes.
Mrsha didn’t blink. Damia’s heart beat faster, and her throat constricted. Her voice wobbled. Oh no.
“I—I wouldn’t know. I’m a Reinhart. You’re right, I don’t understand…but I wish to visit, Miss Mrsha. Please? Even if not the inn, just—you can use me in some kind of weird Solstice event. I’d love to be part of it. I don’t care if it’s risky. If something is going on, put me in. Please.”
You don’t know what you’re asking for. You don’t want to be part of a real Solstice event.
Mrsha backed up a step, and she appeared tired. Upset. She half-turned, and Damia reached out. Her hand passed through Mrsha’s arm, and she cried out.
“I do! I was so bored in the north. I am angry at Magnolia—but maybe she did me a favor. Not wholly, and I will collect my interest. But I can imagine just staying in the north, being complacent, if I had been myself my entire life. Instead, when I woke up from her Skill, I left my home. That was the right choice, even if it was due to desperation. I would never have been content there. I was so miserable, and nothing really mattered to me. I wanted to be important or just—provocative so badly I slept with my brother.”
Er…that’s too much information—
The girl turned back, and Ilvriss eyed Damia, but the [Lady] was panting now, desperate to get the words back. She was kneeling and scooted closer to Mrsha. Eyes on the girl.
“I mean it. I want to level. I want to do something amazing, even if it means risking my life. Would you take a life of boring peace?”
Yes.
“Yes.”
Ilvriss and Mrsha replied at the same time. Damia’s head swung between them, and her face screwed up.
“It’s meaningless to me. Maybe it’d be different if I had—friends. A place to go. But I’ve nothing to my name. Not class nor levels nor a purpose. I went to Liscor and I started levelling. It felt like I was alive. I want to see it. Everything around me is the product of the inn, shaped by its passage. I long for the real thing, like a moth to the flame. Take me in, please. Let me be a guest. Allow me entrance, and break me apart if you must. Even if I die, I never want to go back and sit in my meaningless home.”
Wall Lord Ilvriss and Mrsha du Marquin stood there as Damia finished and became conscious of her audience. They always watched her, that silly [Lady]. Because she was funny and odd. But this confession struck the people watching her, because it was…honest. Damia’s honesty. It was the inn, the real inn that people tried to pretend wasn’t truly that powerful.
So, the Salazsarian employees were staring at her. Silverfangs turning to each other in shock. Even Liscorians were frankly incredulous, Riverfarm’s folk appalled by the [Lady] who had chosen an [Innkeeper]’s madness over an [Emperor]’s mercy.
And even…Damia’s head turned, and she saw Binevy and Sule standing there. They’d chickened out of using the brothel’s services. The young man was scratching his head, intrigued and confused. But the [Head Maid] met her mistress’ eyes, and it felt like Binevy had finally seen Damia.
Her eyes were wide, unnerved. She gazed upon the kneeling [Lady] like some kind of strange monster. It was the expression every Reinhart servant gave their employer in time. How they came to understand the people they might choose to follow.
But Damia…Damia Reinhart was a unique one, even among her den of vipers.
 
——
 
Lady Cecille Reinhart’s cigarette flared like a cherry in the darkness of her room. She exhaled smoke in a ring and blew a smaller circle through it. Then added a third through the center of both. She watched the faintly crimson smoke vanishing, then took another drag.
Then her eyes fell down to the [Message] scroll, and she brushed some of the ash from the cigarette off it. The transcript of Damia’s confession was still drying on the parchment, as Binevy and a separate spy had recorded. Only a fool trusted one spy.
In that moment, the [Lady] understood her daughter a bit. So that was what freedom had made of her. Cecille exhaled again and spoke.
“Foolish girl. Of course this mansion is dull. We’ll all move out and find our abodes soon. The only joy of gathering here is to match wits among equals. Dull? It is duller outside, when you are the only fresh pair of eyes in a sea of people who walk staring at the shadows on the ground.”
More ash fell, and Cecille’s face twisted further. A bitter scowl that would have told her daughter just as much about her mother, if Damia had seen it. But Cecille had learned to hide it all.
“The pure light is not meant for Reinharts. The shadows we cast are too long. You long for the first spark of authenticity because it looks so tantalizing. There will be more. Do you think you’re so special? So unique?”
She rubbed at her side, just above the left side of her stomach. Her thigh itched. The [Lady] lifted the hem of her dress, exposing a long-faded tattoo whose magic and trust was gone.
“In time…they will disappoint you. Or you will sully them by aiding them. They will not stay so pure. And when they reveal their true colors—”
Cecille whirled. For a second, she stared into a vanity and saw another [Lady] racing off with earnest desires and the belief she was the one Reinhart who saw beyond it all. She spoke, eyes stinging from the smoke.
“When they show you who they are and you come slinking back home in shame, we shall make good use of your levels.”
That was how she said goodbye to her daughter. For a while.
 
——
 
As for Damia? She knelt, trembling as a Gnoll girl stood there and a Wall Lord tried to interject. Steer her from the cliff she wanted to walk off of. But Mrsha just searched Damia’s face, then smiled bleakly. Guiltily.
“I know I’m a Reinhart, Mrsha. But am I allowed to be more than my family’s name?”
The guilty words of a daughter of the Five Families hit Mrsha in the face like Alber’s best punch. She rocked back on her heels, mouth open, and then just looked Damia up and down. Actually scratched at her head, and pulled a hair out with a wince. Then Mrsha wrote, a heavy little paw solemnly writing to Damia. And the girl believed each word mattered. Because she knew what Mrsha had done.
Well, I don’t know how right you are about all that, Damia. We’re not…Gods. Some might say we’re the opposite. But The Wandering Inn is not so full we can turn away friends or allies. I’ll hold a seat for you, if you can help Ilvriss. I hope you don’t regret it. But thank you. For believing in us.
Disbelieving, heart lifting in her chest, Damia rose. Mrsha exhaled, then held out a paw. And it might have contained a [Witch]’s wonders, an [Innkeeper]’s chaos, the power of fate and destiny, a Doombearer’s luck…or nothing at all. But Damia Reinhart took it with trembling hands and mimed shaking the air. And then she was part of it.
It was the happiest day of her life.

——
 
Later that day, Mrsha du Marquin found her mother tending to the plants in the [Garden of Sanctuary]. Sage’s Grass, cocoa trees, and other valuable crops. She was harvesting the pods of slimy seeds and sighing.
“What’s even the point if Oteslia’s growing all this…? Oh, right. The Antinium want their own for their greenhouses. Mrsha, dear, your plants grow a bit too well sometimes.”
Mrsha, who had raised Faerie Flowers until they had grown enough to pierce Skills themselves, had a bit of a green thumb. The Gnoll girl came over to help her mother. After a while, she held up a card.
Hey, Mom?
“Yes, dear?”
I committed a racism today.
Lyonette almost said ‘that’s nice’, until she glanced down and saw Mrsha’s guilty expression. She stood there, mouth open, until Apista buzzed down for hugs. And Mrsha resolved never to do it again. She just hadn’t known you could racism like that. It was a pernicious thing, the racisms.
 
——
 
The effect of Lady Damia Reinhart was more than just one helpful [Lady] making the camp better. It was her darn enthusiasm. And her willingness to spam Skills.
The next day, Ilvriss woke up, and he did all of his paperwork for the week. He emerged from his tents, gnoshing on breakfast—a green apple and packaged Calescent spiced porridge which had no actual heat in it, because it was so much better than camp food—and he saw a group of [Miners] marching toward Dig Site B like thunder.
“Alright, you slack-tailed sloths, let’s get to it! I want those seams dug!”
The [Forewoman] was exhorting her people like they were [Soldiers]. And the [Soldiers]? They were armed and raring to go. Major Shellc was infected with the same energy.
“We are breaching four spots and engaging the undead. I want that growing Dorn Pillar dead. Drinks on me to the entire force if we get it. Captain Asrira, are you sure you’re ready?”
“Ready, sir.”
Captain Osthia was the one person not infected by the energy everyone else shared. She eyed Major Shellc as she and her girls sat—smoking Dreamleaf. Ilvriss half-halted.
“Captain Asrira, what are you doing?”
“Er, building morale, sir?”
She didn’t even hide the contraband drugs as the rest of the Sisters grinned. Osthia eyed Ilvriss.
“What’s with the camp, sir?”
“Camp? I’m just feeling it, Asrira. I must have rolled out of bed after some good sleep or something—ah, Welsca?”
“Wall Lord! We’re ready to work, sir. I’ve gotten ahead on several initiatives, and I’d like to consult with you on the equipment—we’re due in Liscor tomorrow, and I took the liberty of preparing against another [Rancher] assault.”
Welsca and half his staff were in fine fettle. Like Ilvriss, they’d cleared their work queues, and only when Ilvriss saw a sleepy Drake jogging to keep up with Welsca and glancing at her sidelong did he realize they were way too energetic for…for…
The culprit was still at the officer’s mess tables. Damia Reinhart barely peeked up when Ilvriss isolated the originator of the Skill.
“Lady Damia, do we have you to thank for this burst in motivation?”
It was her new Skill, according to Miss Binevy, but the anxious Human was standing by her [Lady], and Lady Damia, who had a new class and Skills and would normally be over the moon about such things…
“Yeah I guess.”
Ilvriss paused. He jerked his head.
“I’d love to talk further, Lady Damia, and I’ve the energy to do a walkabout if you do?”
It took Damia several moments to respond, and she listlessly spooned her food around her plate. Her hair was uncombed, and she was wearing pajamas, he realized. She gave him the most flat gaze he’d ever seen as she raised her head.
“Nah. What’s the point?”
“Er…”
Binevy whispered anxiously.
“She overdid it, Wall Lord! Her [Pass On Enthusiasm] Skill—I think she did it too much!”
Ah. Never let it be said Damia was anything but a hoot. Ilvriss hesitated, then patted Damia on the shoulder.
“I can see you’re in need of rest, Lady Damia. Perhaps ease back on the Skill, but you are an important part of our operations—dead gods, I’d make your position formal since we hired you as a temporary [Recruiter]. Just, er, take the day off. You’ve already earned your pay.”
Actually, if she could do this just for Ilvriss each day, he’d pay her all she was earning and more! The Wall Lord walked off, genuinely eager to clear all the items on his docket and be ahead. He felt great. Just a bit bad for the clearly-spent Damia.
He didn’t see the [Lady] drooping at her table as he marched off. Then…Damia’s head rising slowly, like someone underwater surfacing. Then she burst out.
“Employment? Of course—”
Damia Reinhart returned back to something close to normal after ten minutes. Which was actually terrifying in itself if you thought about it.
 
——
 
Enthusiasm didn’t mean a life without headaches. Ilvriss had two at the moment. Firstly, what to do with the Naamreles gear.
“It’s too good, sir. It should go to House Gemscale’s vaults or arm any Named-ranker or officer we have. This helmet? Sturdiness rated for [Generals]. We could give it to our High Command.”
The simple miner’s helmet sat in front of Ilvriss, and he stared at it. It was so damn insulting to the might of Salazsar that this helmet from days of yore was equivalent to a modern [General]’s helm. But it made sense.
“Everything we have is inferior to this magic, Welsca, truly?”
“Everything except a Relic, sir, or what’s stored in the vaults of Walled Families. Enchantments wear down with use. We were lucky the group that perished in the mines died before using up the charge of the magic. It’s kept them pristine. As has the lack of magicore or other mana-devouring nuisances.”
Magic was not infinite. Only the Heartflame Breastplate could withstand aeons of active use and not break. Salazsar had used all its old gear, hence why Ilvriss had decided to loot this city.
“But it begs the questions, surely—why was Naamreles buried with so much magic intact? Sserys was sure it had intact gear—he apparently spoke to a number of Drake ghosts across Izril.”
“Sir?”
Even knowing what she did Welsca had to give Ilvriss a double-take, but today was a red-letter day, and the Wall Lord wanted to be open. Transparent.
“You heard me, Welsca. You had to have known.”
“I-I did, sir, but—”
“Keep it private from the rest of the staff. It’s the kind of thing that sounds like insanity, but you’ve been to the inn…I swear that’s what happened. I just wish he knew more! But he was shouting all the damn coordinates and information in my earholes, and I didn’t exactly have time to write it down! Actually, I started to the moment he ran off to beat up the Dragonspeaker. Did you see me on my hands and knees writing on the ground with everyone dying around me?”
“No, sir. But Sserys really interrogated other ghosts…?”
“It’s an entire thing. I wish I’d asked Erin more about it. And I do wish he’d been more specific, but it’s hard to ask the [General of Drakes] questions when he’s straddling your chest, screaming orders in your earhole.”
“Sir?”
Ilvriss coughed, reddening at the memory. He’d thought it was an unnecessary position, but Sserys had led with ‘let me have my fun’. Which, when you put it in context with Xesci’s recent comments—
Surely not. But then he’d heard—but that was—
“…That aside, the gear’s too damn good. I don’t know what to do with it. Commit some of it back to Salazsar, I think. I don’t want to look like a Hoarder, and given our reactions…let’s have Enchanter Hedault consult with us just to make sure this is all what we think it is. And maybe offer a piece to Lady Damia. I intend to bring her into our confidence, Welsca.”
“Her? Wall Lord, are you sure? I know she’s been a credit, but—”
Ilvriss just smiled at Welsca.
“You heard her class, Welsca. [Fangirl]? I had no notion—I’m not trusting her with all of it. But would you deny she’s been a breath of fresh air?”
The Drake [Adjutant]’s face screwed up.
“I can’t, Wall Lord. I must point out, however, that she is an ambitious young woman. One with aims on yourself as well.”
“What? Me? Well, I’m sure it’s her attraction to The Wandering Inn. Let, uh, her meet Ishkr or Ser Dalimont and that’ll be that. I’m not in any mood to have a dalliance with someone half my age, Welsca.”
Ilvriss was highly amused, but he shot that one down instantly. He shook his head.
“What kind of degenerate would have a relationship with someone that much younger than he was?”
Welsca nodded rapidly and with great relief. Elsewhere in the world, Archmage Eldavin, Duke Rhisveri, and the Quarass of Germina experienced unexpected sneezing fits. Ilvriss drummed his claws on the desk.
“As for the other problem…alright, let me hear it.”
The other problem was not so grand as magical items of antiquity. The other problem had one name, and it was amazing it had gotten to Ilvriss’ level, but it was, after all…
Georgie.
 
——
 
Ilvriss would admit, he didn’t feel at home with this Turnscale business. He was doing his best, but his best thought was ‘it’s got nothing to do with me’.
Therefore, he’d thought himself quite clever saying that Georgie had been affected by a cursed rune and just letting him work with female [Miner] teams. Because Georgie was…no, wait, he wasn’t, and that was the point…
At some point, someone could use ‘woman’ and ‘Georgie’ in the same sentence and not need to duck. The problem was that Ilvriss was wrong, and how wrong he was needed to be spelt out.
Forewoman Gerra Runefur was that speller. She was a Salazsarian Gnoll—no Plains Gnoll—with the accent of the City of Gems, all the familiarity for the work, and who ran a tight ship. Ilvriss knew her from the Gemscale company; she was forty-two, had one child, had married a member of the corporate staff who worked on Level 11 of his tower—not high level, but a decent [Office Manager] himself, Level 21—and Gerra was a Level 32 [Gemfinder Forewoman]. Nothing special, but she’d never had issues keeping order with her mixed Gnoll-Drake crew of women.
It wasn’t mandatory to have single-gender teams, but sometimes it was preferable. The same gender could bathe and work together. It prevented issues of harassment. It was just an option…and right now, it was not working.
Hence her complaints, which had escalated until they got to Ilvriss. The Forewoman didn’t slam a fist on Ilvriss’ desk. She was very polite as she held her cap behind her back, but her voice was all stress, and Ilvriss recognized it.
“Wall Lord, we cannot have Miner Georgie working with our team.”
“If Georgie’s an issue, Forewoman, I can reassign h—them to another—”
“No, Wall Lord, I am representing all the female mining teams. He’s been moved to six teams, and mine has had him for the last four days. None of them will take him. With great respect, Wall Lord, we will strike and call off work if we have to work with Georgie. I don’t want to make this a huge issue, and I understand there are—problems—around Georgie, but this is not the solution. Sir.”
Ilvriss blinked at Gerra. He sat back in his chair, his enthusiasm for his job ebbing in his veins. He sensed what was coming next.
“Can you elaborate on the issue, Forewoman?”
She took a breath, and he realized he’d walked into that one. Gerra reported with a loud, overly-polite tone of voice that overrode all attempts to shut her down or break in.
“Georgie is…an average [Miner]. No, I take it back, he’s a pain in the tail for even average [Miners] because he’s used to Advance Miner work, but he’s higher-level than I am, so he deserves that, I suppose.”
“I believe it’s she, Forewoman—”
Welsca was drowned out as the Gnoll raised her voice.
“He’s just your average [Miner] in many respects, Wall Lord Ilvriss. Which means that he’s not ruder than most, not more of a lech or hassle—you bark at him and he does his job after complaining. But he’s a [Miner] in my team. Who I understand is cursed or…”
“Terribly cursed, Forewoman.”
“It’s an accidental enchantment caused by—”
“Wall Lord, he’s still a damn Drake, and he looks! He wanders into our changing rooms as we’re undressing, and it’s gotten so damn bad we’re hanging towels up!”
“But Georgie’s—”
“Wall Lord, have you ever seen someone ogle someone else? I’m sure you have, sir—Georgie ogles. He observes. He enjoys watching us nude. If you don’t think I’m being accurate or reasonable, as several of my superiors have, I invite you to strip to your waist and I can show you exactly how that feels.”
She gave Ilvriss a look at his own chest and several parts of his body, including his tail, which he felt was quite inappropriate. Ilvriss crossed his arms over his chest.
“I can understand that, but Georgie, right now, has a woman’s—”
“He’s nearly gotten beaten to death for slapping my [Miners] on the ass. He’s a classic idiot, Wall Lord. Yesterday, we ran into a tough seam. Nothing we couldn’t crack, but he shoved my own lead miner aside and told her, and I quote, ‘stay back and let a real man handle this’. If my team doesn’t murder him by tomorrow, it’ll be because someone’s shoved him into a wall and gotten him stuck there.”
Ilvriss’ mouth opened. But it was Georgie! Who—according to Ilvriss, was a man despite any bodily appearances, but—
“So you’re saying Georgie’s not fitting in, that the team cannot work with him, and for his morale, and yours, he should be reassigned to mixed-gender teams, Forewoman?”
He tried to clarify, and even here was his mistake. Forewoman Gerra gave Ilvriss a dark gaze.
“Oh, no, he was all complaints until he spent an hour on the job, Wall Lord. Now he loves it here. Incidentally, Wall Lord, my superiors have not been markedly understanding of the situation. I hope you are.”
“I am, Forewoman Gerra, and I appreciate your forbearance. Welsca brought this issue to my attention.”
Gerra gave Welsca a nod and half-smile, and both turned to Ilvriss. Gerra gave Ilvriss a very respectful nod, as someone who’d worked her entire career for his company would at the new, relatively young boss who was doing a good job.
“Thank you, Wall Lord. And just so we are clear, if he is not removed from my shift by the end of today, my entire team and I will be submitting sexual harassment claims in accordance with company policy.”
All one hundred and twelve of them. Ilvriss swallowed and decided the enchanted artifacts could wait a second.
 
——
 
Georgie was having a great time, despite knowing that in a broader sense, his life was over. True, most mining teams weren’t filled with Drakes or Gnolls who took the time to do makeup or look their best.
But nevertheless, having that many ladies around was not a bad thing. He took the well-chewed cigar out of his mouth and called out to a female Drake cursing and shoving a wheelbarrow with a slightly-busted front wheel up the tunnel they were enlarging. Some kind of digging project to breach the undead tunnels.
If he never saw another damn undead, he’d be happy. He still had nightmares, but he’d lived. Stupid to make that rookie mistake, but he’d collapsed the cave like he’d heard had once worked and made it out. Now he was still dead because of that damn [Healer], but—
“Hey, Andrasite, you want a hand with that? Just let me know if you want a real man to help you out.”
The mining team had already had enough two days ago. At this, Andrasite dropped the wheelbarrow filled with dirt and stones.
“You want to help out, Georgie? Be my guest.”
To her utter annoyance, Georgie sauntered over, lifted the wheelbarrow with one hand, and began to push it.
“So what’re you doing tonight? Ain’t like there’s much to do in this Human cowturd of a place. Want someone to show you a good time?”
Andrasite rolled her eyes. She tried to stomp ahead, and Georgie shoved the wheelbarrow faster, keeping up with her fast walk. He was, annoyingly, strong and Level 30+—which was double the average level of Gerra’s work team. Most had levelled since coming to the Cowpat Plains, but Level 30 was still Level 30. The Drakes and Gnolls tried to ignore Georgie.
He, for his part, tried to keep the relaxed smile on his face as he heaved the wheelbarrow, which was quite heavy, up the sloping tunnel.
Argh, Ancestors, my arm is going to fall off! But anything to impress the younger Drake. She gave him a sneer.
“I’ve got a boyfriend, Georgie.”
“Who? I haven’t seen him around. Some limp-tailed loser who can’t even pick you up after your shift? C’mon, I’m right here, and I’m Level 36! You ever met someone higher level than me? You won’t wanna go back after that.”
He thought he was onto something with Andrasite until she turned and smirked.
“You and what equipment, Georgie? I heard you were having trouble with that?”
His face went flat. And Georgie’s stomach abruptly twisted as laughter came from around him. He let go of the wheelbarrow and massaged his shoulder.
They knew. Regardless of what that damn Wall Lord had tried to claim—no, it just made it worse, them thinking he’d actually been transformed. Georgie managed after a second—
“All I need is this, Andrasite.”
He waggled two claws at her and stuck his tongue between his claws in a suggestive way. She made a face.
“Ugh. Do you actually think that’s attractive?”
“Bet you’ve never met anyone who even knows how to use their tongue, let alone claws. I’ll have you late for your next shift, babe.”
He leaned against the wheelbarrow to prevent it rolling down. Andrasite hesitated despite the jeers and snorts coming from around her.
“But you literally don’t even have—right?”
“Hey, I’ve got ways. No one’s ever complained after a night with yours truly. How about it?”
He winked at her, and if there was a truth stone on Georgie, it would have probably exploded from that one. The truth was that Georgie had sustained numerous complaints from partners over the years.
Partly, because he’d not known what the hell he was doing and assumed that good sex was proportional to how fast and hard you could move in the act. Which did not win you applause and sometimes got you disqualified before even reaching the finish line.
Second, because Georgie did have some issues in the traditional equipment sense. He didn’t like to think about it. He did have a…phallus. He kept it under his bed in a lockbox, and if anyone ever found it or asked what that was about he’d just kill everyone before explaining.
The problem was, if you brought that out to a lovely lady, she had questions. So Georgie’s move until now had been to find someone who was into him, and then get to the point where both agreed it was time to jump each other. Then get some drinks to smooth over any inconsistencies for both sides.
His second escape during any questioning that arose was to claim that a Creler had once bitten his dick off. And Georgie didn’t want to hear anyone but the ladies of his acquaintanceship complain.
It was his damn life, and he was working with the hand he’d been dealt. He hadn’t been born some rich Wall Lord sitting on a pile of gold in their towers. He was a working man, and now that he’d been found out…
I never should have picked that damn pickaxe up. Idiot, idiot—what was he going to do? He’d already been blacklisted from all the other reputable companies in Salazsar. Leave for a smaller Drake city? What did he do?
Georgie didn’t know, so he flirted. He thought Andrasite might actually bite, despite her mockery, when there was a shout.
“Administrators inspecting! Sharpen up!”
“Ah, shit in my bag of holding. Who is it now?”
Forewoman Gerra was back, that complaining Bagrhaven. Georgie began to haul the wheelbarrow up, swearing, as Andrasite made herself scarce. He was shoving it up the tunnel when the clomp of boots and voices made him peek up.
And there was Wall Lord Ilvriss, his hot secretary, and Forewoman Gerra. Georgie bit down hard on his chewed cigar as Ilvriss glanced at him.
“Miner Georgie, I’d like a word, if I may?”
“Busy working, Wall Lord.”
Georgie tried, but Welsca snapped.
“You’re reporting to the Wall Lord, Miner! You’re off-shift until he’s finished. Forewoman Gerra?”
“Thank you, Adjutant. Alright, everyone, back to work! No more distractions—I want [126% Effort!]”
Georgie’s heart sank as the shaft echoed with cheers and shouts. He straightened and gave the female [Miners] around him a betrayed look.
“Ancestors damnit—you’d think they’d be grateful for having a fellow around doin’ all the hard work. I’ve been humping dirt and taking on the hardest jobs and not a single tail raised even an inch off the ground. Women, am I right, Wall Lord?”
He turned to get confirmation or a grunt from the Wall Lord, and Ilvriss stared at Georgie with the most confused expression on his face—Georgie hesitated, then averted his gaze. So the Wall Lord didn’t get what he was, but he wasn’t having Georgie strung up or run out. Was that something?
Damnit. Georgie didn’t know what he was, but it wasn’t his fault. He just wanted to be—a regular fellow. Unfortunately, fate had other plans for him, and so did the Wall Lord. He seemed to think Georgie was his pet project.
 
——
 
It was weird to Ilvriss because Georgie was not a…okay, he was a pain in the tail. He was rude, ready to brawl, lazy when he knew he could get away with it, and he’d tried to steal the first chance he got.
Ilvriss had sort of thought he’d be different than…well, a slightly-below-average [Miner]. He was a woman. In body? Form alone? At birth but had been subjected to a gender-transformation potion?
Ancestors, it hurt Ilvriss’ head, but he tried! He pulled Georgie aside and had a word with the Drake.
“Miner Georgie, you make Forewoman Gerra’s team uncomfortable.”
“What? I’m extra respectful to them, Wall Lord. I don’t throw fists or nothin’.”
“You’ve been leering, according to Forewoman Gerra and the complaints I’ve gotten.”
“Well, what kind of fellow doesn’t leer when they’re changing around you? Am I blind?”
In a sense, it was Ilvriss’ fault. He had prefaced Georgie’s predicament by claiming he was now a woman. And it seemed that he’d been trusted on that. Georgie kept staring at something only he could see.
“Can’t them [Mages] do magic pictures of memories? I swear, it was worth getting buried alive and nearly having my head ripped off. All that jiggling…”
His claws made grasping motions, and Ilvriss resisted the urge to smack Georgie on the head. He was a victim of his own greed and unfortunate circumstances…
“I’m going to assign you to another team, Georgie, and given the, ah, issues, I’ll have you report to me for any further problems. It should hopefully not be arduous, but voice your complaints to me, and any interpersonal dilemmas. Understand?”
At this, Georgie stopped leering and glanced at Ilvriss and Welsca. Then he lost his smile.
“You think it’s all gonna get swept under the rug, Wall Lord? I’d rather you paid out my contract.”
“You’re a witness to events I’d like to keep private, Georgie. And if I did pay off your contact, would that end the matter?”
Georgie peered around the dig site as he put his hands in his pockets. Then his head lowered, and he stared at his boots.
“No.”
“Then let me try to help. Agreed?”
The [Miner] said nothing. But that was how Ilvriss began the odd conversations he had with Miner Georgie. He rather hoped it would be a small issue. Unfortunately, he was beginning to know Georgie.
 
——
 
On the first day of being reassigned, within two hours of reporting to Dig Team Oxynite, Ilvriss decided to monitor Georgie’s break. Not everyone knew about his situation, just that he was an Advance Miner. The Wall Lord activated his Ring of Invisibility only to find Georgie had integrated with the team.
At least some of them. The mixed-gender mining team was your old rough-and-tumble group. Veteran [Miners] who ranged from late twenties to fifties, ready to swing a fist at anyone who said the wrong thing if they were working.
And—right now—teaching a lesson to some of Riverfarm’s Humans, who they didn’t care for. Because they were Humans? Or northerners? Or just rookies?
“Hold ‘em still! You don’t pull your weight, you get your skin dyed!”
A laughing group of nine, all Drakes and Gnolls, were about to dunk a young man headfirst in a bucket of charcoal water. It’d color your skin or scales like ink, and a wet Gnoll with blackened fur and another Drake and Human half-glaring at the group were signs of the hazing ritual.
Georgie was laughing and holding onto a kicking leg when Wall Lord Ilvriss appeared, arms folded. The Advance Miner stopped, took his cigar out of his mouth, and let go of the young man as the rest of the [Miners] turned. Ilvriss sighed.
 
——
 
Incident two occurred as Georgie’s mining team was coming off shift. That same day. The tradition was for the [Forepeople] of each team to report their take.
It wasn’t all about who’d dug up what. Only a fool thought one team digging more gemstones up mattered; if you were assigned to a bad seam, what could you do?
