It was instead about how much you’d dug. How many tough pockets you’d had to clear—if you’d run into a monster nest, all kinds of stuff. And it wasn’t all competition, but there was some satisfaction to Foreman Rolx of Oxynite strolling over and dumping a sample of ore down in a pile.
“Oxynite, 84 buckets of loose stone deposit. 122 feet straight down and nothing.”
Not much, really. Loose stone deposit was all bits and tiny gemstones or dust that someone would sift through for valuables. Barely worth the effort. It was a shit take, and Georgie would have been nervous if he was in a traditional company in Salazsar. Too many days like that and your entire mining project might get canned for lack of value.
However, here it was fine. They were digging down, still searching for something else besides the teams assigned to mining around the undead. Georgie was tired and sweat-soaked. It was a hot damn day of work, and he wanted to use the new showers he’d heard they’d installed.
And toilets—the ladies had refused to let him use more than buckets from their damn cubicles. As if he’d steal their underwear. You got that as a trophy, and you tucked it into your belt so the lads knew you’d scored a motherlode the next day. Like a proper Drake.
Anyways, the point was the other teams were reporting numbers.
“80 feet. Hard clay.”
That came from a Human to a few jeers—from Georgie especially loudly—but he shut up when Rolx glared at him. The bastard was not shy about throwing a fist around, but the new team was Humans. From Liscor! Or Invrisil or something—
It wasn’t that bad for newcomers a few days on the job, actually. If the clay sucked it sucked. But Georgie kept jeering until a huge damn Silverfang appeared.
“Miner Thamert, of Silverfangs. We report 214 feet dug. Mostly limestone with some coal.”
He casually dumped a black bucket on the pile, proof of said coal. Georgie nearly inhaled his cigar.
That deep on top of stone and other deposits they had to harvest? He began to mutter.
“Gotta be bullshit. I reckon they’re just trying to look good. How do a bunch of Plains Gnolls know how to dig better’n us?”
He got several nods from other Salazsarian [Miners], but someone hissed.
“That’s an Ekhtouch Gnoll. You see him swinging that pickaxe? I heard he cracked the entire limestone seam after winding up for twenty minutes! Just hit it and it all turned into gravel!”
Ancestors, really? The Silverfang marched back, and Georgie swallowed another comment. Then came a team he knew well.
“Rolx. 33 feet. Nothing to report.”
A broad-chested Drake walked forwards, dumped no bucket down, and seemed very disinterested in showing off. He nodded to the other [Foremen], who said not a word, and Georgie ground his cigar down in his teeth. No one said anything—until he did.
“Thirty-three feet? What were they doing, yanking their tails in one hand and pissing with the other? Why do we have to share shift-pay with this lot?”
He called out, and the Drake who was walking back into line turned his head. He and his team didn’t appear as dirty as Georgie’s group. Nor had they dug even half of what Georgie’s team had!
But they were sort of big, which is why no one had commented hither-to, and the administrators didn’t care, even if they could see the results. Georgie pointed.
“It’s always Rolx and Oblest and Canera—lazy bastards. You don’t get anywhere while we’re doing all the digging!”
“Hey, someone’s got to sort through all the shit your team misses!”
One of the Gnolls fired back, but one of the [Administrators] barked.
“That’s enough! Let’s muster out and get back to camp. Eyes on a swivel for those damn [Ranchers]!”
Each mining team’s leader began to shout orders, but Georgie was fed up, and from the muttering, he wasn’t the only one.
“Just so long as they get half-pay. I’m not busting my tail to let my bonus slip away to some weak-armed Hatchlings who can’t even lift a shovel!”
At that, there were a lot of loud murmurs in agreement. Everyone from the Silverfangs to the Humans to the all-female teams—you got paid for exemplary work, and these idiots didn’t work. Georgie saw the leader of Rolx’s team square his shoulders, then bark back.
“That’s enough. Muster out!”
“Eager to get to your bed? You didn’t earn it!”
That came from Andrasite, Georgie swore. Someone tossed a clod of dirt, and it hit one of the lazy miners in the helmet. There was a growl.
“Break it up! Everyone get moving!”
The [Administrator] saw the trouble and tried to head it off, but it was too late. The angry, annoyed miners began shoving upwards, out of the dig site, but one got too close to the Rolx team. They shoved past the angry miners and nearly sent a group of four sprawling. When the enraged quartet shoved the lazy [Miner] so hard he crashed into his buddies, there were shouts, people falling—then someone nailed someone else with a stone.
More objects flew, and the mining team captains began to shout and use Skills—then saw fists raise. They backed up. Then the first person threw a punch. Georgie wasn’t that person.
He threw the sixth punch and clocked the leader of the Rolx team in the jaw—and broke his jaw. Georgie was swearing and holding his fist, which felt like it was broken itself, when the enraged Drake rose, covering his face, and George realized the other Drake had a head on the slim [Miner]—and over a hundred pounds.
Ancestors, he’s ripped for a shitass min—
 
——
 
Wall Lord Ilvriss was standing over Georgie when the Drake woke up and stared cross-eyed at the ceiling. He had his head in his hands.
“How many injuries?”
“Er…less than if the [Ranchers] attacked, Wall Lord?”
“How many teams are off rotation tomorrow?”
“Only—six, sir?”
Uh oh. Georgie tried to pretend to be asleep, but Wall Lord Ilvriss had noticed him waking up. The Wall Lord leaned over.
“Miner Georgie, do you have anything to say for yourself? I’m told you broke one of my Foremen’s jaws.”
“They started it, sir.”
Georgie lied like he breathed. Ilvriss stared down at him. He straightened.
“Your pay will be docked pending a full investigation, Miner Georgie. Along with everyone else who instigated the fights. Welsca, note the incident down on file. Don’t list it as a serious incident, but I want accountability.”
Shit. Georgie sagged as Ilvriss spun on his heel and turned. It wouldn’t be the worst incident on his record, but that kind of thing added up, and he had a history—
Who was he kidding, he wasn’t working for Gemscale after this. And he probably had to leave—he heard the Wall Lord departing after some more talking. The man whose jaw Georgie had broken was lying in a cot at the far end of the [Healer]’s tent, and that did not make Georgie feel better.
Bastard had laid him out in three punches. If he wanted vengeance—Georgie decided he’d be careful when taking a piss or eating. Though he was always careful about some things. He tried to get some sleep as he lay back in bed.
Just another day in the life of Miner Georgie, who didn’t really have many friends, prospects, or a future in this world. He closed his eyes.
Georgie was nodding off when he felt the pillow go over his face. But contrary to what people might believe, a pillow didn’t exactly choke you if applied to your face, just made it harder to breathe. Plus—he tore it off.
“Hey, who the h—”
That was when his unknown assailant dropped the pillow, put two claws around Georgie’s throat, and began to strangle him. He kicked, thrashing, but the attacker had the advantage of height and surprise and—
 
——
 
It was late, and Damia’s Skill had worn off on Ilvriss. He was nursing his own bruises; another [Rancher] clan had paid him a visit that evening, and while he’d clocked someone leading the lot hard in the face, he’d eaten a full kick that had torn his scales up.
Miner Georgie had been a pain in his scales three times today, and so his name was the last one Ilvriss wanted to hear. When he heard it again, he actually hissed.
“If that idiot’s molesting the [Healer], I want him fired and shipped back to Salazsar in a—”
“Sir, someone tried to kill him just now.”
Ilvriss stopped hissing. He rose to his feet.
“You’re sure?”
 
——
 
The Drake who’d saved Georgie from being asphyxiated was Miner Team Leader Maiss. Of Team Rolx. A Drake whom Ilvriss knew, though that was not the issue at hand here.
His jaw was still broken and on the mend, and Ilvriss winced; the [Healer] had done her best to set it, but it might not have gone completely right without a healing potion; it appeared distinctly swollen or like his chin was a bit too forwards. Maybe he should assign a healing potion, but they weren’t supposed to have them, despite the inn gifting Ilvriss some for his officers…no, wait, those healing scrolls. He needed to buy some but pretend he hated Antinium. Next time he was in Liscor.
“Team Leader Maiss, report?”
Maiss struggled to talk.
“Saw someone try to put a pillow over his face, sir. Thought it was just annoyance until he started strangling. Stopped it.”
“How?”
“Broke his arm, sir, and gave him an introduction to my knee.”
That…would do it. Maiss was a big Drake, and Ilvriss was impressed. No ‘mysterious assailant’ running off here.
“Good work, Team Leader Maiss. Get some rest.”
“Sir.”
He saluted Ilvriss, and the Wall Lord turned.
“Who tried to kill Georgie? One of the teams he insulted?”
Welsca checked her notes, and she was uncomfortable, now.
“No, Wall Lord. It’s a Junior Mining Inspector Lacambt, sir. One of Dramm’s subordinates. When questioned, he just stated that he was doing the right thing, Wall Lord.”
Ilvriss’ heart sank. He felt a shadow pass over him, despite the late hour, and saw Xesci’s gaze in the reflection from Welsca’s spectacles.
“Wall Lord Dramm’s apologized for his subordinate’s assault and requested leniency, sir.”
“I imagine he has.”
“So have five other leaders, from [Foremen] to administrators.”
A longer silence, then. Ilvriss inhaled, then spoke.
“…[Miners] brawl all the time, Welsca. This was an unfortunate brawl, but we’ve had idiots start multiple and kept them, albeit with discipline. Murdering another [Miner]? Unless it’s some kind of jilted lover, someone getting the other person killed, or feud, it doesn’t happen. We are the Gemscale Company, and we tolerate no speciesism, no hostile work environment, no pettiness.”
“Yes, Wall Lord. With respect, Wall Lord, that’s not the issue at hand.”
He knew that. But Welsca was changing too, because she pointed it out so frankly. Ilvriss met her eyes, then glanced down at his table.
“Put Georgie in a separate tent and post a guard. Have Lacambt on trial for attempted murder.”
“Are you sure, sir?”
“He tried to strangle Georgie.”
Was that not obvious? Ilvriss’ incensed gaze at Welca didn’t provoke an expression of shame, because she reminded him of something he’d recently learned.
“Yes, Wall Lord. I’m not denying that. I’m just pointing out that Lacambt may have not been acting solely on his own, Wall Lord.”
A longer silence, then, as Ilvriss met her eyes, and he didn’t need anything else spelled out. He rubbed at his face again.
“I wish I had a drink. Don’t get me one. Just…begin asking around, discreetly. Make sure that guard is secure. And—and move Miner Georgie off his current team. Have him assigned to Advance Miner work again once he recovers. But report him to me and Major Shellc for the anti-undead operations.”
“Yes, sir.”
Fortunately or unfortunately, being strangled by an amateur did less damage than getting punched out in a brawl. Georgie was fit for work tomorrow morning, and that was how Ilvriss found himself in the company of a Turnscale, which was one thing.
But also one of the most annoying Advance Miners he’d ever met, which Ilvriss decided mattered a lot more.
 
——
 
When you got down to it, what was a Wall Lord? Useful? For his Skills, certainly. For the money he provided, of course, a necessity given Salazsar’s commercial systems. But when the hammers and pickaxes were going, where should he be?
In a tower, ‘leading’ people and waiting for the profits to come in? Most of Ilvriss’ peers operated that way. They set huge initiatives in concurrence with their trusted [Administrators] and staff, used their Skills, and just…raked in the gold.
When you got down to it, the truth was—Ilvriss knew he was like the nobles of the north in many ways. He was not the one crushing stones. What good was he at this dig site? The fact that he had left so often to visit the inn was proof he wasn’t essential. However, that morning, he took a walk around the town of Thirthoof, his dig site, and just—was the Lord of the Walls of the Gemscale company.
With Georgie and Damia. The former because it was necessary for Georgie’s safety and because Ilvriss had to do something—the latter because she had earned it and you had to have fun in life.
But Georgie…he seemed subdued after his near-death experience. Shaken, pale as he strode over in a grimy, sleeveless undershirt and trousers. Ilvriss saw Welsca glaring, but he leaned over.
“That’s how most [Miners] attire themselves, Welsca. I’d like some privacy today, please. It’s going to be just inspections.”
That meant only his [Bodyguards] and fewer staff. Given his company, Welsca nodded. She followed of course, and Georgie actually threw a salute. A bad one, but he touched his helmet.
“What’s my job today, Wall Lord? I’m ready to go.”
“For now, you’re accompanying me around the dig sites, Georgie. We need to talk. Plus, Major Shellc is still trying to corral the undead that attacked you. We need those damned mines clear.”
Georgie’s eyes flickered as he nodded and took a breath.
“Bastards are tunneling around a lot, right? I hate that. That’s real Advance Miner sh—stuff. Chasing them down and not getting close before the monsters jump you. And the [Soldiers] are always too damn slow.”
See, that interested Ilvriss and Lady Damia, who had come out in a full dress with a low cut blouse—he glanced sideways as Georgie stopped and stared at Damia’s chest.
“Not appropriate, Lady Damia.”
“I, uh—I’m so sorry—”
“We might be entering the mines. Protective clothing on everyone, please. I should have mentioned.”
He had on his regular clothing, but he had rings, and they were good enough to fight in. Damia dashed off and came back two minutes later, in an amazing display of speed-dressing, to Ilvriss and Georgie…talking.
“You actually had to participate in an anti-Creler operation? The Razzmetal mines? I heard about that. Forty days, right?”
Georgie grimaced around his worn cigar.
“Thirty-nine, and they only called me in for eighteen. Little bastards kept digging.”
“Didn’t it set a record for most Advance Miner injuries this century? I thought it was incompetence, but Alrric speculated it was just…nasty.”
“Yup. See, the thing all those stupid administrators don’t get is that even if we’ve got Rubirel Guard ‘at our tails’, they’re always standing up the mine shaft. Can’t get too close in case we cave in. They ain’t got the reflexes to dodge, and we need space to move. I don’t care if one of ‘em’s got [Lightning Interception]. They’re still slow. When the monster decides to come at you, it just takes one swing. Then you dodge or it’s got you.”
“Did you run into the Crelers?”
Georgie flexed a bicep.
“Sure did. We got to the point where we’d just go fifteen feet, listen, and go fifteen feet again. Then we drew lots, and one bugger had to keep minin’.”
“Why? Ah—”
Damia leapt into the conversation, though her eager voice became subdued.
“Wait, isn’t that because the Crelers would flee a large number of bodies? So if they thought they could kill one and they were hungry—oh.”
The Advance Miner wore the same expression as soldiers. That too-calm expression of a man who’d seen death. He spat on the ground.
“Yep. No fancy spells, no high-grade magic. Just a rope to haul them up, healing potions if the Crelers were on them, and waiting. We had to do it or they’d keep digging. That’s why we’re Advance Miners. And that’s why you haven’t caught the undead, Wall Lord.”
Ilvriss’ head turned sharply.
“The [Miners] and Shellc have been coordinating and cutting off some of them—”
“But they’re always letting the other ones get away, right? It’s ‘cause the dig teams are scared.”
That was Georgie’s theory, and Ilvriss’ head snapped forward.
“If they’re hesitating to breach or dig fast because of monsters—to Dig Site B, then.”
Damia eyed Georgie sidelong. She knew Georgie as well—mostly from the complaints about him. But her instincts told her he was significant in some way. She’d had Binevy ask around, and no one had wanted to say outright what they thought the problem with Georgie was. Rumors about a magical artifact? Everyone knew something, but what?
Ilvriss himself was reminded of something he’d forgotten: Georgie was an Advance Miner, and a veteran one. Crudeness and Turnscale issues aside, he knew this job. The Wall Lord had high hopes for the day, which diminished after he heard a faint thwap and a shout.
He turned just in time to see Georgie turn and give Damia a tail-slap on her rear end. She turned and slapped him—and Ilvriss exhaled.
Okay then.
 
——
 
As he knew he was a dead Drake walking, now, Georgie had been hoping the Wall Lord would just send him back to Salazsar with a terminated clause. Even if he was fired, that would give him some lead time unless they sent him to the sanatorium.
So yes, the butt-slap on the [Lady] had been calculated. The [Adjutant] was about to chew him out and write Georgie up when the Wall Lord did something unexpected.
“Give me five minutes. Lady Damia, I apologize—Georgie, one side.”
He stepped to the side with Georgie, not actually out of earshot, and the [Miner] braced. He could see the other Drakes and Humans accompanying Ilvriss watching him and pretending not to. This would be the moment when the Wall Lord threw a punch or the [Miner Leader] sorted you out. Then you got back to work.
“Sir?”
He braced, trembling a bit because he knew he couldn’t punch the Wall Lord—that was another kind of death sentence. But Ilvriss just exhaled hard.
“That kind of conduct is unacceptable in the Gemscale corporation, Advance Miner Georgie. You know that.”
Instantly, Georgie relaxed and felt a bit contemptuous. No punch, which meant the Wall Lord was one of those soft ‘lead from behind’ types who sat in their towers all day. The ones who just lectured you. He glanced at Damia.
“Sure thing, Wall Lord. Won’t happen again. She’s just a H—”
Ilvriss reached out and grabbed Georgie’s shoulder. Hard. Georgie jerked, and Ilvriss dragged him over.
“Finish that sentence, Georgie, or do that again in my presence and I will ship you back to Salazsar and blacklist you across the entire Walled City. I am trying to help you. Why did you do that?”
His aura was pressing down on Georgie hard, like heavy gemstones, and Georgie hesitated. Swallowed as he glanced down at his boots and shoved his hands in his pockets.
“I—look, it’s just the kind of thing you do, Wall Lord.”
“Harass women?”
“I—sure? It’s just how miners do things.”
“Not all [Miners], and not in my company, Georgie. Your conduct has been inexcusable and deliberate. You know treating other Drakes and Gnolls like that is inappropriate. What I want to know is why. I’ve read your record. Plenty of indictments for insubordination, fighting—and you know what? It’s pretty standard for an Advance Miner.”
“I’m s—what?”
That threw the Drake. He finally looked up, and Ilvriss raised his brows.
“All Advance Miners fight with their supervisors and each other. And brawl. It’s not the standard we hold regular [Miners] to, but you risk your lives. It’s like Gold-rankers. We have an internal system in the Gemscale company where we weigh your negative records against how many times you’ve been in a life-or-death encounter and how you performed there. Alrric does it; it’s some kind of mathematical calculation. You passed it. Until recently, you weren’t exactly ideal, and your attempted theft of company property was a problem, but the sexual harassment claims weren’t high at all. Three over an entire lifetime of work. Something changed. What?”
Georgie squirmed as he glanced at Ilvriss’ boots, then up at the Drake. Fuck, this wasn’t his usual dressing down. It felt more like a conversation. He snapped back.
“What’s there to say, Wall Lord? It’s not like I’ve got any future now that I’m cursed. You had me on theft—then you tossed me in a mining team full of women!”
“And?”
“And I’m a man, not someone who’ll wear a female uniform or be one of the girls. I reminded them of that, didn’t I?”
Georgie hissed back, and now Ilvriss blinked. He peered at Georgie, then glanced at Welsca. Backed down his aura and exhaled.
“Is that what this is about? Are you trying to get fired?”
“I’m already fired. Someone gets drubbed down for theft and they’re already on the way out.”
Ilvriss scratched at his chin-scales.
“You’re an Advance Miner who breached a high-level monster site. And the artifacts you found have been nearly stolen by multiple members of my company. It even triggered Hoarding impulses in me. I wouldn’t hold that against you entirely, though I would have you watched.”
He was being too reasonable. Georgie was mad and trembling with it. He chewed on his cigar.
“Then the other reason’s enough.”
Again, Ilvriss hesitated, and Georgie stared at him. He knew Georgie was a Turnsc—that thing. Georgie didn’t even like thinking the word himself. It was bad. Terrible. So why was the Wall Lord so off?
They said he employed a [Courtesan], that he had an affair with a Human in the north and tried to marry her. They used to say he was one of Salazsar’s glittering boys, an exemplar Wall Lord. But Gnolls always liked him because he employed them and then—
Who was this Drake? Ilvriss was thinking, as if asking himself that very question.
“That…matter is one of great concern, Georgie. I do not want you to be put in further danger, and I understand if I let you go, there will be concerns.”
I’ll have to flee the city fast as I can. The companies’ll probably have me blacklisted, and I bet someone’ll call into the Watch to haul me to the sanatorium. You don’t get out of there. Georgie took a shuddering breath.
“Could be. But hey, if you paid me out my contract, I could, uh, just go. Plenty of work for a [Miner] in the north, right?”
The Wall Lord looked him over again.
“You’d rather walk into Human territory—this territory with the [Ranchers] about—than go back to Salazsar?”
“Well, yeah.”
This time, Georgie met Ilvriss’ topaz colored eyes, and he thought the Wall Lord understood. A bit more, and he’d already been close to the truth. But like a man chipping away at a glittering gemstone buried in stone, another piece fell away. Ilvriss exhaled.
“I see. If we can’t resolve this, I’ll arrange for you to be paid out and dropped off in Invrisil.”
That—might actually work. There were Drakes there, and if he used his papers, he might get through Pallass or just go to Liscor. The sense of doom hanging over Georgie’s head lightened. He actually smiled.
—Right before Ilvriss took him by the shoulder.
“Now, back to your behavior. No more of that.”
“Oh, come on, Wall Lord. That’s how the ladies like it.”
The Wall Lord’s look made Georgie splutter.
“I’m not joking! You know how it is. Sure, half the [Miners]’ll belt you in the face, especially if they’ve got someone, and the other half ignores you. But that leaves, like…a fifth who’ll listen. They go for a man who talks loud or who does what we’re all thinking.”
“Your math is as atrocious as a certain [Witch]’s fashion sense, Georgie. That is not how you establish a romantic relationship with people.”
The two Drakes argued, and Georgie snorted. He hadn’t actually had a conversation with another Drake or Gnoll about, well, romance. It felt odd. So, almost eagerly, he challenged the Wall Lord, jutting out his chin.
“Okay, how does it work for you? I bet having gold and being a Wall Lord makes it so much easier.”
For a moment, he saw Ilvriss drawing breath, flushing slightly under his scales, and then the Wall Lord hesitated. Exhaled.
“I won’t deny…I had the same attitude as a younger man, Georgie. Showing off, boasting, it does have its appeal, and it does sometimes work. But the woman I fell in love with didn’t get to know me because of any of that. Actually, she requested a transfer from my command after—for every person you win over, you’ll alienate ten more from your behavior. And, with due respect to said individuals, the women who appreciate being nonconsensually slapped on the rear are not the partners I’d choose for life.”
To this fairly reasonable statement, Georgie shrugged.
“I know that. I’m just down for a quick—y’know. I’ll settle down with someone who’s actually decent later. Someone who appreciates a good fellow and doesn’t want me acting out.”
That was just a dream. Just a dream, but he talked like it was a reality. Like he was any other fellow—but Ilvriss was simply frowning at Georgie.
“There’s an error in your logic, Georgie.”
“Just one?”
“Hah. Listen, I get that mentality. Be young, fool around. Some people never leave it. I have a friend, Wall Lord Brilm—he’s married and has a kid, and he’s been divorced because he keeps doing what you’re doing. Let’s say you can quit, though. Here’s a question P—someone asked me once. What happens to the women you’re talking about? The ones who’d never make good life partners?”
“…Huh?”
Ilvriss was walking and talking with Georgie now, towards Dig Site B since it was a ways.
“I’ve had to deal with female employees who have been accused of being licentious as well. Not in those exact words. It’s a bad idea to enforce a no-relationships policy in the company, but the flipside is all these issues. But you know, I’ve heard the exact same things come out of their mouths. They’re just having fun—in ten years, they’ll find a fellow who doesn’t mouth off or cast eyes at every passing tail. In time, it seems no one thinks that kind of behavior is ideal in a life partner. Brilm aside.”
Georgie had no idea where this conversation was going, so he shrugged.
“So? Seems like everyone agrees, then. We’re having fun.”
“Only, your ‘having fun’ involves quite a lot of people who don’t want to participate in your behavior.”
“Yeah, well…I overdid it because the ladies were full of snark. It’s just me and the boys doing what’s normal, usually.”
“Faking Gnollish howls whenever someone you fancy walks by?”
The Advance Miner had the grace not to deny he did that. Ilvriss wore the pained expression of a Drake who’d done just that.
“Let me put it like this, Georgie. I can’t enforce social behavior outside of the Gemscale company, but I can within my work environment, and that’s why we have codes of conduct. But even outside of it—we don’t have to act like that. We can, but it makes other people uncomfortable. It turns you into someone who’s competing with every other man around you for attention and that often leads to bravado. Fights. It’s not that much fun, especially if you’re not the most handsome or strongest man in the room.”
“…So you want me to not say a word and be one of those respectful, sexless losers who holds doors open for women and hopes they eye him sideways?”
The Wall Lord exhaled.
“No, I’m just saying tone it down and the entire room takes it down. Let me give you an example. There’s always someone like that in a group of friends. Brilm. It’s always Brilm walking over, asking someone what color their underwear is, making comments that are wholly inappropriate. And if you think you’re bad, you’ve never seen a drunk Wall Lord.”
“I just slapped that [Lady] on the rear. How bad could—”
“You’ve never tried to strip a [Waitress]’ skirt off in a restaurant.”
Ah. Georgie hesitated, and Ilvriss stared past him.
“At that point, you do drop-kick your friend. Or let the Watch sort it out. But you know, it doesn’t have to get there. If you’re in that moment, and he’s acting out, you just have to pull him aside and say, ‘Brilm, this isn’t funny, knock it off’.”
“Right, I’m sure that works.”
“Mhm. And when he shoves your other friend, Tasilt, off and calls him a limp-tailed loser, you pull him aside and say the same thing. When three people say it, or everyone tells him to shut up or go home? He does that. And he can go off and find other friends or change. It’s called peer pressure, Georgie. It is the act of being made to be uncomfortable when a dozen people around you address the thing you shouldn’t do and you stop.”
“Normally, a [Foreman] just tells me to knock it off or throws a fist.”
Ilvriss walked forwards, shaking his head.
“Right. That’s miner culture, and I don’t do that. Alrric’s part of that old mentality of smacking heads, and I’m not. My father was. I try to address things without needing to punch heads because it doesn’t actually change things.”
Georgie was shifting from foot to foot.
“Look—I know it was wrong. I’ll apologize to the [Lady], Wall Lord. I’ll stop. I’ll even apologize to the women in the mining teams. But you’re not going to change things with this, I dunno, social talking to people thing. That’s not how it works.”
If you had fifteen of the lads all shouting comments and you pulled what Ilvriss was doing? It only worked because he was the Wall Lord. Otherwise, they’d go tell you to get stuffed, and if you insisted, they’d come at you with fists. But Ilvriss just glanced at Georgie, amused.
“Maybe it only works for me, Georgie, but I’d say it has an effect on people.”
“How do you figure that? I haven’t ever been peer-social-pressured or whatever.”
“No? I’m doing it to you right now. Most young men, Gnolls, Drakes, listen when I have this conversation with them. If they keep acting up, it’s a shame and we let them go. We had to hire a lot of new workers because this isn’t a regular operation, but I think I’ll have a few more conversations like this today. I’d like you to be part of them. Or at least, observe.”
Georgie came to a halt, mouth open. Ilvriss strode onwards, and he didn’t insist that Georgie apologize to Damia or hover over his shoulder. But he was watching, Georgie realized.
 
——
 
Wall Lord Ilvriss was not amazing at combat, strategy, or even leadership. He was just…good at all these things. And good enough to know when someone was better than him, like Major Shellc.
The Rhir veteran rebutted Georgie’s claim in some respects. After his conversation with Georgie, Ilvriss caught everyone, including Damia and her people, up to speed on the undead issue. Which had Damia agog.
“A Dorn Pillar?”
“A growing one. It’s mobile. When it gets too big, apparently it turns into said pillar, which is mostly stationary. Bone with holes in it that extrude undead-themed monsters. And it can hypnotize, cast magic—it’s one of the nastiest undead down there because it thinks. Pursuit has been hard, but it’s not just the [Miners], Wall Lord.”
Shellc was pointing to a three-dimensional model of the dig. The mining tunnels that Georgie had found were zig-zagging upwards. Long, almost geometric lines compared to the exploratory shapes dug down. But then it became an anthill of confused tunnels—attempts to dig and attack the undead and keep them from tunnelling outwards.
A damn mess in short. Shellc appeared annoyed, but grimly determined.
“I have no doubt our teams are slow due to fear, Wall Lord, Advance Miner. But the undead are actively avoiding us after we destroyed several of their high-grade members. The moment they hear us, they retreat.”
“Ah. Small wonder they’re eluding us. The Creler problem all over, but I am not minded to send in a sacrificial lamb. Thoughts, anyone? Advance Miner?”
Damia was prodding the side of her head, muttering. Georgie just frowned.
“Are the bastards listening to us? ‘Cause there are Advance Miners with silence Skills.”
“Probably detecting our hearts or just lifeforce. Still, an Advance Miner with stealth Skills…not a bad idea. I’m embarrassed to say that I don’t know much about mining, Wall Lord. I didn’t know that was a thing.”
Shellc frowned. Ilvriss assured him it was, if rare.
“Advance Miners need to avoid monster nests or even buried magics, Major. Let’s say we get the jump on them—”
“We have, three times. The Dorn Pillar will screen itself and run. It’s not fast, but the bastard can send a Crypt Lord in front of us, and getting past it slows us down. By the time we hack apart its escort, it’s gone.”
Hence the problem. Damia lifted a hand.
“What if—what if we put down traps ahead of the Dorn Pillar so it runs into them?”
“You mean we flank it then lay traps? It’s hard enough getting the drop on it once, Lady Damia. No offense, but if I manage to get close to it, I’m ambushing because a flanking maneuver takes too much time, and they are actively mobile. A good thought, but we don’t have dedicated trapsetting experts.”
“We have some auxiliaries…”
“None with dedicated trapping Skills, Wall Lord. Unless the Silverfangs have any, I’d put this as ‘nice, but impractical’.”
The two Drakes exchanged glances as Damia’s head swung between the two, and Georgie frowned. Ilvriss sighed.
“Damnation. I never was a good commander with a varied army. We have some of my staff who can [Deploy Fortification], but it takes fifteen minutes and a few dozen hands, even with the Skill. Battlefield-ready, not for ambushes like an adventurer. We can do magic—”
“The Dorn Pillar senses mana, so it runs the moment a [Mage] gets within two hundred feet of it. There are too many tunnels, Wall Lord, and the more we dig, the easier it is for the undead to slip around us in the passages.”
“You’ve been bracing them, right? The stuff in the weird mining tunnels won’t collapse, but if the tunnels are not braced, you’ll risk a cave-in and everyone dying.”
Georgie put in, and the Rubirel Guard shifted nervously behind him. They could survive even underwater thanks to their helmets, but being buried alive? Their helmets would run out of oxygen, and that was a horror story. Shellc was grimacing further.
“I’ve had numerous [Foremen] shouting that very thing at me, Advance Miner. We’re trying, but as I said, now we have to tunnel, brace it, while the undead are—”
“What if you collapsed the tunnels on purpose?”
A quiet voice, then. Damia Reinhart’s eyes lit up as her thoughts manifested an idea. All the Drakes turned, and Shellc blinked.
“On the Dorn Pillar?”
“It would slow it down, wouldn’t it? Can you perform a controlled collapse? [Geomancers] have trouble, I know, but if you knew where it was running—”
Bury it and get in place while it tries to get out. Shellc’s eyes lit up. Ilvriss spun.
“Excellent idea, Lady Damia! Who has collapsing Skills? Stealth Skills? Welsca, get a list—”
Georgie raised one claw. He had both. And he felt that familiar flutter of fear in his chest, but if Ilvriss meant what he said…
“I could do it. For a big payout. Combat pay and more.”
That might give me a chance to buy my way across the north. He had a way out of here that didn’t get him killed. He just had to risk his life.
Easy. What a relief. He actually grinned, a cocksure smile of someone who was good at his job as the room turned to him. Appraising, nodding, even a hint of respect from Ilvriss.
Lady Damia smiled sweetly at Georgie. Then she grabbed his tail and gave it a squeeze near the base.
He nearly leapt on her before Shellc and Ilvriss dragged him back.
 
——
 
Damia Reinhart held grudges. And she had the counterpoint to Ilvriss’ reasonable conversation about respect and behavior.
If you do it to me, I’ll do it to you. Georgie was furious—but he simmered down once Damia apologized and Ilvriss called in a mediator.
Xesci was always highly amused whenever she was called in to help. She turned to Lady Damia, then Georgie, then to Ilvriss.
“You want me to deal with sexual harassment?”
“I know that’s ironic—”
“No, no, I’m great at it. Do you have any more offending [Miners]? I have a very simple way to deal with it.”
“What is it?”
Georgie was eying Xesci, who was not in her more lascivious forms, with some curiosity. So was Damia. Xesci smirked.
“Your speech on behavior is nice, Ilvriss. I don’t have time for it, so if one of my [Bouncers] thinks he can treat a girl like that—or vice versa—I just remind them it can go all the way around. Lady Damia had the right idea. I’ll just start groping whoever’s doing it.”
“…That seems like it would have the opposite effect on a number of young men, Xesci.”
She gave Ilvriss an amused gaze.
“Would it? If it’s what you’re imagining and I’m being nice and caressing them, it would, Ilvriss. Do you think that’s how it feels to the opposite sex? A slap on the rear when you’re not expecting it isn’t fun. Neither is being fondled. Or being treated like you’re a toy. If I did that to you in front of an entire group and, say, called you a cute little thing while putting a claw on your butthole, what would you do?”
Ilvriss’ face said he regretted asking Xesci instantly. But Georgie jerked back.
“Punch your face in? They’ll kill you.”
“Not if I have leverage. No one’s strong enough when there are six people on you. Well, Named-rankers, but even they find it hard in the right joint-lock. Want me to demonstrate? If one of them harasses my girls, I’ll have them stripped and walked naked back to their tents. And believe me, there will be comments.”
Xesci could be vicious, and Ilvriss winced to imagine how much she could comment on your personal appearance. She turned to Georgie.
“I’m sure you don’t want me to get handsy with you, Advance Miner? Or Lady Damia?”
The look in Georgie’s eyes went murderous in a moment, and Xesci lifted her claws.
“There we go. Don’t lash out at me. Also, you’re copying the wrong thing. You think that’s how most Drake men should act. It’s just the loudest ones.”
Anger turned to uncertainty, then terror. Georgie backed up as Xesci smiled at him, and she could see straight through him. Straight through everyone. She turned to Damia, then, and the [Lady] lost her eager, inquisitive smile.
“I’m delighted you’re being useful and having fun, Lady Damia. Just remember—you haven’t reached the inn yet. There’s always time to mess it up, just like you always have.”
She stepped back, then, and gave Ilvriss a thumbs up.
“You see? So much faster than your speech. Want me to visit your mines?”
He hesitated and eyed the two mildly-traumatized employees.
“Can you…be a bit less intense, Xesci?”
She sighed.
“I could try.”
 
——
 
Okay…one issue of Georgie’s behavior had been stomped flat. He was so subdued that besides asking who Xesci was, he seemed rather beaten down.
Ilvriss was reminded that the fellow had survived two near-death experiences. He tried to think of the future. And be encouraging. That was what he was doing as Major Shellc began his Operation Cave-In.
Building morale. It was a stupid phrase, but that was what he was trying. How good he was at it, Ilvriss never knew. He thought it had some effect, but…
“How is Team Oxynite doing? I know morale’s been low recently, but between the reinforcements and a certain [Lady], I understand things are better. Still…how much should I be putting in as a bonus?”
It was a bad joke, but the Drake and Gnoll [Miners] laughed awkwardly as they stood around. Ilvriss’ scales were itching. They were digging towards the undead, but this group was taking a break, and there were [Soldiers] all about. Rolx’s team was digging another tunnel—poorly. Georgie was lending a claw, and they were still half of what Oxynite was doing.
[Foreman] Rolx was eying Georgie, but he didn’t comment on the oddities, like Damia or—well, it was all oddity. He just listened as one of the Drakes, a fellow with a scar on his cheek from a falling stalactite, laughed.
“We’ll take a big one, Wall Lord! This has been one of the most sh—er, interesting jobs so far.”
“You’re allowed to say shit, Miner Filss. We are in the Cowpat Plains. We’re walking on it to work.”
More laughter, but Ilvriss didn’t react to it. He didn’t need to be humored. He just stood there, drinking from a water flask as they did likewise. No free mugs of beer or officials going around shaking hands. And the [Miners] get a bit less raucous as they realized he was just…
“Any major complaints? I know you’re all on combat pay, and I assure you, every measure has been taken to avoid miner-deaths. But we are working around multiple high-tier undead. They have to die. If we pack up and haul off, these lands will be hit by a scourge in a decade’s time like an Adult Creler’s nest.”
“Not like the Humans don’t deserve it, Wall Lord. They come riding in, break our skulls, and ride off, and we have to clean up the shit living in their tunnels?”
Another silence to see what the Wall Lord said, but Ilvriss nodded at the Gnoll.
“Fair enough, Miner…?”
“Rulf.”
“Thank you, Miner Rulf, I agree with a lot of that. However, it’s their lands—and ours. These undead? They’re all Drakes. Well, I heard a few Gnolls were among them, but it’s Drakes. Even if most have mutated. It’s…our problem.”
Now they were all staring at him, and someone coughed.
“As in—we were the ones mining down here? What’re we, uh, what’re we digging up, Wall Lord? There’s rumors it’s—we just heard it was some big dig.”
Ilvriss took another sip of water, thoughtful.
“Whatever I can tell you would be a problem, Miner Arassa. You know we’ve got corporate spies—with how many there are, I think we might have more than [Miners]. Which I apologize about, but believe me, when bonuses are delivered, I am paying attention to who’s actually digging.”
He gestured with his free claw, ever-so-slightly, to Rolx’s team to the side, and the [Miners] stirred. Oh, was that how…? Ilvriss went on, glancing around.
“If you have safety issues, or I can make this more tolerable, that’s my concern right now. Are you missing anything? The water here’s…brackish.”
He grimaced at the water flask. Rolx hurried forward to assure Ilvriss he could get the Wall Lord something better, but Ilvriss just took another swig.
“No, we had it purity-tested. So it’s potable. But maybe we enchant this with a flavor? We can do that—I actually heard one of the Horns of Hammerad had a bunch of water flasks that did the same thing. Fascinating stuff. There’s an Enchanter Hedault coming by for a consultation in a bit. Say I do that. What flavor?”
“Mint?”
“Not mint—something like a juice?”
“You want to drink that nonstop? Milk?”
“I’m going to be sick, Wall Lord, anything but mint or milk—”
He grinned as he took suggestions, then asked about any other amenities.
“At least we’ve got damn bidets. Nice toilets, Wall Lord. You know, we had Humans asking about them?”
The Drake Wall Lord was amused as Georgie came out for a break, chewing on his cigar, and listened.
“Keep this secret, but I heard the Lischelle family was asking about our [Plumber] installing his toilets in their houses.”
“Hah! And they can squat over a hole while we’ve got real plumbing, eh, Wall Lord?”
“No, I gave Master Oidhol the go-ahead. We’re trying to be friendly here.”
The [Miners] were comfortable enough to grouse, now, and Ilvriss held up his claws.
“Look, I’m not happy about the raids either. I’m paying for all these damn cattle, and that Marvus punched me so hard I swear I felt like I was fourteen again the other day. But we can’t let this go to blood. There’s a lot more of them than us.”
Plus, he’d rotated squads like Rolx’s so they ended up being off-duty when the [Ranchers] came by in the evenings, leaving the real [Miners] to rest up. The Humans didn’t get nearly as far as they’d like, despite all their Skills and levels. The [Miners] continued to grouse.
“D’you think you can do anything about the heat, Wall Lord? It’s getting hot down here.”
“Cooling spells? Don’t you have any?”
“Sure—it’s a bubble, but we’re spread out.”
“Wind spells, then. I’ll put in an order and see if Master Hedault can do some more enchanting. Now, I’ve got to keep moving—I’ll be here to oversee Operation Cave-In for a bit, but I have to keep going. We’re doing important work here.”
The [Miners] nodded, and one of them offered a hand to shake after tugging a glove off his paws.
“Just as you say, Wall Lord. It’s good pay, and the Gemscale Company does right by us. Whatever we’re digging up—it’ll be good for Salazsar, right?”
“It’ll be good for everyone, I hope, Miner Rulf.”
Ilvriss met the Gnoll’s eyes, and the Gnoll in his late thirties smiled. He wasn’t a copy of Georgie; he had two children, and he was a company man who’d signed up for a lot more pay. Ilvriss’ stomach tightened; Rulf might be hurt in the operation or times to come.
He was doing it for the gold. It was like Osthia’s new group. Men and women coming all this way, enduring privation, lack of bidets, angry Humans, undead, all for just gold.
Because they weren’t him. Ilvriss met his eyes.
“Trust me on that.”
Ilvriss thought they appreciated the visit and break if nothing else, but he felt that incongruity. Just for a moment.
The Wall Lord…thought about that.
 
——
 
Advance Miner Georgie and Ilvriss watched Operation Cave-In going down. Both were ready for action, and Georgie had actually done some of the digging to get to the baby Dorn Pillar. However, as luck had it, he was resting when the breach was made and the [Soldiers] went in.
Rubirel Guard and the regular soldiers that Ilvriss had taken with him to the north. Shellc knew his business; he screened the undead with heavily-armored Drakes and Gnolls and let lower-leveled [Soldiers] gain experience in supporting roles.
The Major even went down himself as Ilvriss listened to the reports.
“It’s running, it’s running—straight up Tunnel Beta! [Geomancers], target from the surface for long-range precision quakes—[Miners], activate your [Controlled Collapse] Skills and prepare to breach!”
The [Geomancers] shifted the earth, trying to trigger a controlled collapse of the tunnels. One in an adjoining tunnel actually created a [Stone Wall] spell and pressed it into the earth, forcing the dirt around it to move, and then—
“Collapse! Collapse in Tunnel Beta! It’s not buried! It’s trapped against one wall! It’s screaming, Major—”
“Rubirel Guard, advance alone. All squads, fall back and fortify your zones and prepare for another counter! I’m heading in.”
Major Shellc strode out of the command room, and Ilvriss waited, ready to lead a second squad down. Captain Osthia was in the wings—
Then they heard a report. Major Shellc’s voice.
“Dorn Pillar down. This is for the record—you go through the damn holes in its body, just like the Monster Bestiary says. Eugh, there are Children everywhere.”
“Major Shellc, say again?”
“Sorry, the monster, not actual children, Wall Lord. Always wondered where the buggers came from. The unborn ones do not look cute.”
And that was it. Ilvriss didn’t believe it was that simple, and neither did Georgie. As reports came in of other undead moving towards the soldiers—then away at speed—they realized Shellc had done it. When he hauled the ‘corpse’ of the Dorn Pillar up and they saw the white flesh oozing out the holes in its body, like a mutilated piece of bone with holes drilled in it leaking flesh like a slug—Ilvriss gagged.
“Major, you took that out?”
A huge cut had split some of the holes open, and by the scorch marks, the Rubirel Guard had thrust their weapons through the gap and exploded the Dorn Pillar from the inside out. The [Major] seemed slightly hurt; he was rubbing at his earholes.
“Sorry, my ears are still ringing from it screaming. I’ve seen worse in Rhir. Remind me to tell you about an ever-splitting monster some time…there are more undead down there. We just took out a leader. Nice work on the digging. Lady Damia has my compliments.”
And what did you say next? Ilvriss thought about it.
“Two days paid leave to every [Miner] and [Soldier] who participated. Let’s send to Thirthoof for some good food. Keep at it, Major Shellc.”
“Absolutely, Wall Lord.”
Ilvriss walked away, smiling, and shook claws with people as the cheering started and the dead Dorn Pillar was paraded around for the other teams. We did it.
He caught Damia celebrating with the [Miners] and saw Georgie standing there, eying the other undead being brought up for dissection or disposal.
“How was that, Advance Miner? Anything we’ve done wrong?”
“You’re asking me? It looked tidy. Never had Rubirel Guard for escort on anything but Crelers, Wall Lord. Yeah. It’s a good company you’ve got there.”
What else do you want from me? Ilvriss frowned. He appreciated the honesty since he doubted Georgie lied well, or cared to about matters like this. But the [Miner] was just…kicking dried cow pats around.
“You did solid work today. Do you think you’d want to stay on? Assuming we clear the issues around you, of course.”
Georgie’s head snapped up. Ilvriss consulted his [Mental Notes].
“You’re working pro tem for this expedition, but anyone who works well we could keep on with the Gemscale company. Given the counterlevelling going on here, I think that’ll be a lot of good people. How about it?”
“If you clear my issues—hah!”
The Drake managed a shaky laugh. He shook his head, and Ilvriss pressed him.
“Let’s assume we did. How about it?”
Georgie’s face twisted up, then he spat the next words out.
“Even if you did the impossible—why would I? It’s a nice paying job, and if you deliver on tasty water and breeze spells, you’re better’n most damn companies, but so what? This is a shitass job, and I only took it for the gold. Don’t expect me to stick around if I find a better offer.”
That was—fair. Ilvriss gazed down at the [Miners] who were waving up at him. He waved back; someone must have told them they were getting time off. All it took for them to cheer his name was that. And he felt it again.
“I…do appreciate all the [Miners] are doing here, Georgie. This has been a hard dig, and I’m not the one digging.”
“I’m sure a fat bonus does all that, Wall Lord. It’ll last you as long as it does for them. But this is your dig. Whatever we dug up is going straight to the Gemscale Company. Lots of those pickaxes and stuff?”
Ilvriss hesitated before nodding. Georgie stuck his claws in his pockets.
“I only stole it for a second, but I could tell what it did. That’s like a Relic-class pickaxe, right?”
“…Pending Master Hedault’s personal confirmation.”
“There you go. D’you know how it feels when you dig up the biggest gemstone you’ve ever seen, or a piece of Adamantium, and know it’s going straight to the company?”
“There are bounties on—I see your point.”
“Yeah. Besides. You can’t save my life. If you want to organize me getting paid out, I’ll go to Invrisil tomorrow. It’ll spare all of us a lot of problems.”
Georgie turned away abruptly. Ilvriss stared at his back. He cleared his throat.
“Georgie, the attack on your life was unacceptable. The Drake’s being charged, and it won’t happen again.”
The Advance Miner was staring into the hole in the ground that was Dig Site B like he was considering jumping down there.
“It’ll happen. Everyone knows what I am, even if you claim I’ve been cursed. That doesn’t matter. Cursed or not, now I’m a—a—”
“Georgie, I’ve had hard conversations these last few days. You’re not an easy person to work with, but I respect your actual capabilities quite a bit. If we can make this work—”
The Advance Miner turned in a flash and grabbed Ilvriss’ suit.
“There’s no way to fix this, Wall Lord! Everyone knows! What am I supposed to do? They’re going to kill me—”
There was genuine terror in his eyes. And Ilvriss saw it again.
“I won’t let it happen, Georgie.”
The Drake didn’t believe him. He let go fast as people turned, then stepped back, staring at the ground.
“Just let me leave with some gold, Wall Lord. I’m one Advance Miner among hundreds you could find at the drop of a hat. Even if I am the best—why bother?”
Why indeed? Ilvriss stood there, and he remembered Erin’s words to him. But more than wanting to do the [Innkeeper]’s bidding, more than curiosity or—he just regarded Georgie.
“Well, what’s in your future if you quit?”
“Another mine? Another job.”
“What about retirement?”
“You don’t get retirement as a freelancer.”
“You do in my company.”
Another flash of rage. Georgie turned abruptly.
“Yeah, well. I wish I was so lucky as to fit in nice and square into the company hole, Wall Lord. But I wasn’t lucky enough to even be born a Gnoll in a Drake’s Walled City.”
He walked off, and Ilvriss stood there a while. Before going to meet Master Hedault.
 
——
 
The [Enchanter] was always on time. He did not enjoy the Cowpat Plains, but given the sensitivity of this job, he had come in person to give Ilvriss a second report on the items they’d dug up. He had nose plugs in his nostrils, which was really distracting as Ilvriss sat with him. The nose plugs were orange, like Hedault’s receding hairline.
“They are closer to Relic-class than not, Wall Lord.”
“They’re not cursed in any way? They triggered my Hoarding—”
Hedault peered at the cup of water Ilvriss offered him and pushed it back, shaking his head.
“Likely a subconscious instinct, Wall Lord. Especially among Oldblood Drakes—there is an innate ability of some Drakes to appraise the worth of items they hold and covet them.”
“R-really?”
“It is a known ability, though I believe not formally recognized. I have several colleagues who have documented the phenomenon. Just like the ‘sixth sense’ of Humans or Gnolls claiming their fur can detect weather changing, like a [Sailor] and winds. You could say the items are cursed in one sense of the word.”
“How so?”
Hedault rubbed at one arm, then tapped his finger on the desk between them, quite miffed.
“The items are cursed in that I have fought off Grimalkin, Salamani, Valeterisa—all of whom exhibited signs of Hoarding themselves. All of whom wanted these items. I suggest you take care with who is entrusted with the artifacts, and I mention this as due prudence in my role as consultant. Beyond that? They are merely valuable beyond belief. Enchantments of an age bygone, preserved due to a near-perfect containment environment and the Monster Preservation Effect.”
“You’ve lost me.”
Hedault raised a finger, smiling.
“A simple phenomenon known to [Enchanters]. Artifacts degrade over time. Mana seeps even from peerless enchantments such as these. However, the presence of monsters that generate magic, even undead, reduces that decay to almost nothing.”
“So that’s why artifacts and monster nests go together—”
“Precisely. Many monsters tolerate the presence of magical artifacts because the seepage of mana empowers them. A fascinating biological adaptation to magic that has left you with artifacts that are in the top ten items for net worth I have ever appraised.”
Hedault took the cup then, and Ilvriss realized that the [Enchanter] might be Human, but even he was eying the pickaxe placed in front of him with great desire.
“But you didn’t take the items, Enchanter Hedault. I quite appreciate that.”
The man just shrugged.
“They are not mine. I do not break the laws I personally hold to be upstanding. They are not mine…and that is that.”
He turned his head away, hard, averting his gaze from the item. Ilvriss knew that copying the enchantments on the pickaxe would make Hedault level and rich—if he managed it. The Wall Lord was reminded, again, that the Erin-effect was in this.
Any other [Enchanter] might have claimed the items were less valuable, or that one had broken in the process of analyzing them or just sold the information. Not Hedault.
“How are your projects coming along, Master Hedault? I was distressed to learn you had been forced out of your home in Invrisil. I have properties in Liscor if you need residence.”
Tap-tap went Hedault’s finger on his wrist. He nodded.
“A kind offer, Wall Lord. However, I have been residing in Esthelm with lodging provided by Solar Cycles, and I am spending an inordinate amount of time in the company of other [Mages]. I hope to have permanent lodgings soon. I have also been engaged in personal research, which has kept me from earning income, but this job was quite worth my time in remuneration and knowledge. Thank you.”
His green eyes glittered with something, and Ilvriss thought he recalled Ryoka visiting the [Enchanter] a few times during the long winter. But whatever Hedault was up to—Ilvriss picked up the pickaxe.
A fortune in magic in his hands. A weapon a Named-ranker might take up. And yet—it was a damn pickaxe.
“Who can use a pickaxe in combat?”
“Two Named-rankers in this age, I believe, Wall Lord, are former [Miners], though I do not know if they are retired.”
That was Hedault for you. Ilvriss had meant more…who’d use it in Salazsar? He had been studying sword techniques, and they did not work with the pickaxe. He thought of Georgie’s comments, and in hindsight, the answer was obvious.
But it was damn hard. Ilvriss admired his reflection on the faintly-glowing metal and thought of his Ancestors. A shame he’d never gotten to really talk to Teriarch. But what would he have said?
Generations upon generations separated the two. They were, in the end, different species. They had to be. So, he slowly held the pickaxe out.
“Master Hedault, I would like you to take this and every item with a unique enchantment among the gear we’ve collected.”
The [Enchanter] had been checking a personal timepiece. He went completely still. Then his head snapped up.
“In exchange for how much work, Wall Lord?”
“What? None. That is to say—I don’t think you’d like to put a number on it. Consider it an investment.”
Ilvriss was having a hard time holding the pickaxe out. When Hedault stared at him, he shook it.
“Take it, please.”
The [Enchanter] did not, so Ilvriss put it down on the table.
“I do not understand, Wall Lord.”
Ilvriss tore his eyes off the pickaxe. Meeting Hedault’s gaze made it easier.
“What’s there not to understand? You’re a friend of the inn.”
Hedault took his time with a response, jaw working as he also gazed at the pickaxe, then at his fingers.
“I am—most assuredly not, Wall Lord. I have not socialized there in any meaningful way. I have friends I have made through the inn, such as the late Kevin—but I—”
“You’ve helped Erin. You know what she’s like. You’ve met Goblins and Antinium. You are, for lack of a better word, a good person in a world distressingly short of them at times. Or people who are willing to be good when it’s hard. Take it.”
Ilvriss felt like a hatchling, saying it all out loud like that. But the [Enchanter] blinked, and Ilvriss shoved the pickaxe forwards.
“Don’t look at it as a gift. An investment, I said. If you manage to copy this enchantment, any of them, or whatever project you’re working on—I want it. I have an eye for good business, and you are that. Take it, Master Hedault. I’d hate to quibble. I have a lot to do today, suddenly.”
He stood, hoping to avoid further argument. But Master Hedault, in his curious way, was just as quick. He picked up the pickaxe, made it vanish into his bag of holding, and then stood as well.
“I will take the most-atrophied items, then, with your permission?”
“I’ll walk you to our Chest of Holding myself, given that I doubt anyone will trust your word even with a note.”
The [Enchanter] nodded. Then he held out his hand.
“Wall Lord, thank you. I will remember this.”
That was all he said. Ilvriss shook Hedault’s hand and was surprised by the strength the man had. He wondered—did Hedault ever shake hands? But when the [Enchanter] smiled, Ilvriss looked him in the eye and thought he’d done himself a great service.
In time.
Then he walked Hedault over to the guarded armory and did the hardest thing in the world—again. Which was the second-hardest thing in his life.
Well, far lower than some things when you got to it. It just felt bad. After the third artifact, it got easier. And then—you know what?
It got a lot easier.
 
——
 
Foreman Rolx didn’t get to go to Invrisil on leave just because of the cruel arithmetic of numbers. Ilvriss had promised time off, and everyone got it—but staggered. Pulling that many mining teams off the dig sites was too much even with the new [Miners], and so the staff had organized things.
In three days, Rolx would get a four-day weekend, which was excellent. Normally, you might work six days out of the week, then take a two-day weekend, and get a three-day weekend once a month. Given you had eight days in the week, working six days in a row was punishing, and he was really looking forward to hitting Invrisil, Liscor, or Pallass.
He had been triumphant about killing the horrific Dorn Pillar, but he was tired, and he wanted to hit the showers and rest. So being called over during the last hour of his shift had him scowling.
He tried to smile at the Wall Lord, but the Drake had done a lot of hanging out, and Rolx’s tongue hurt from not shouting curses and playing nice. He liked Wall Lord Ilvriss, he did. Good manager who hired Gnolls—but like Georgie, Rolx would have been honest in private and said it was just about the gold.
He was surprised, then, to find Ilvriss appearing rather harried himself. And with a Drake trying to put him in a choke hold; he finally threw Advisor Merls off of him.
“Someone drag him away and put a Calming Tonic on him.”
“He’s gone mad! Stop the Wall Lord! Welsca, Damia—help!”
Lady Damia, her [Bodyguard], and two other Drakes had to drag off the screaming man, and the others were also looking at Ilvriss as if he’d gone insane. Rolx hesitated.
“Er, trouble, sir?”
“My staff are objecting to some of my decisions, Rolx. It’ll pass. I had to fistfight three of them—Ancestors. It really is a damn curse.”
Administrative work was harder than he’d thought if that was how it looked internally. Rolx wondered if he’d been too hard on his boy for wanting an office job.
“How can I, uh, help you, Wall Lord? We appreciate all the rewards for our job, but we’re all set to finish the day.”
If you’ll let us just get to it, we’ll go and eat and sleep, thank you. But the Wall Lord smiled at Rolx. He was no idiot, the Gnoll realized.
“It occurs to me, Foreman Rolx, that you’ve been working for the Gemscale company for twenty-two years.”
“Yes, Wall Lord.”
Thirty and he’d be eligible for part pension. Forty, and he’d have tons of benefits rolling in…the companies got you when you were a teen in apprentice-jobs. As young as twelve sometimes, and then you just worked for them.
Gemscale was a good company. Rolx stood there, wondering if he’d get a year added to his work record. That would be worth it. But the Wall Lord only spoke.
“All this work, digging up a Walled City. I thought it was obvious it would benefit everyone.”
“W-Wall Lord?”
Had he just said…? Lady Damia whirled, and Ilvriss just watched the setting sun.
“But it does only benefit from the top down, doesn’t it? If I’m ‘fair’, you get paid well, but that’s it. That’s why it’s so damn hard. You want to keep everything. Because there is no economic sense in giving you anything close to a share of Gemscale’s profits. To succeed, the company should take everything it can and reinvest in itself. Not the people.”
That sounded like real corporate talk. Rolx felt like it was the kind of thing he’d gripe about with other [Foremen] in private. And he’d heard agitators back home talking about that. Followers of the late Sellme. He’d heard Sellme was dead, and that was a bit of a shame, even with the painting thing—but Ilvriss just regarded Rolx.
“Maybe that’s how it works ideally, but you know, the real world isn’t made of such things as numbers. There’s plenty of that, but I’ve known business deals to fail or go through just based on people not liking each other. And one of my favorite people is the most suboptimal, inefficient Human in the world. Nothing she does makes good business sense. But the value she extracts always seems to be beyond whatever any logic dictates.”
“Yes, Wall Lord.”
That seemed like a safe bet. The Gnoll was waiting for Ilvriss to finish whatever philosophical musings he was about, but the Drake was done. He just handed something over to Rolx.
“That’s a pithy, longwinded way of explaining this, Foreman. It’s only one per [Foreman] and the best [Miners]. Here.”
He handed Rolx…a pickaxe. The Gnoll blinked at it. He recognized it, of course. They’d recovered them from the mining tunnels, and they’d been run off to administration.
“Sir? I already have a very good pickaxe. Top of the line. Foreman’s Pick—”
“Take it, Foreman Rolx.”
Hesitantly, the Gnoll lifted the pickaxe. It was so light that he gasped. When he swung it at a stone wall—his paw kept moving, and he stared as he completed the swing without resistance. Then all the fur rose on his body, and he turned.
“Wall Lord—”
Did he know what that was? He had to. The Wall Lord stood there, and it didn’t even seem hard for him to have given the pickaxe away. Welsca, behind him, looked like she was trying to keep herself from launching at Rolx.
“Yes. It’s not temporary. It’s the real thing. I’m going to have you put it in the armory, of course, at night. And we’ll have a system to withdraw them. Tags on the handles or something simple.”
“Yes, Wall Lord—”
So they were letting him use this? Did it swing like that every time? Dead gods, he’d triple the output of the team himself. Foreman Rolx felt like a cub suddenly. He wanted to run back to work. Then he wondered how much gear like this they had. Ancestors, the Gemscale Corporation was rich. And he felt that envy, the sense of forlorn envy that Georgie and the others felt.
That’s when he saw Ilvriss gazing at him.
“You understand, there are considerable risks to owning a piece of gear like that, Foreman Rolx. Every [Thief] in the world will be after it.”
“Of course, Wall Lord, but I’ll be a credit to the company. Thank you for trusting me with it. I’ll be as secure as can be.”
He might try to steal it? The idea was just an intrusive thought, and Rolx shoved it away. No, he’d never get away with it. Using it would be enough. But Ilvriss just kept going.
“I think I need to insist on…a two year clause. We’re working it up. I can’t just give it away.”
“Of course not, Wall L…give it away?”
“Indeed not. It’ll be a reward. And we have numerous employees in the Gemscale Company who deserve one. Alrric, for one. But if we find as many as I think—yes. Give me two years, Foreman Rolx. Two years, and I know you are risking your life and health on this job. It is still, likely, not a fair trade for those who will be injured and die. But that is the unfairness of a company. This?”
Ilvriss pointed at the pickaxe as Rolx understood what he meant, and his mind went blank.
“This is your share.”
“Mine…?”
Now the Gnoll realized why the other Drake had tackled Ilvriss. Why Welsca was trembling. Ilvriss just turned and grinned.
“Welsca, stop that.”
“Wall Lord, your father might murder you. Genuinely.”
“Well, you’ll stop him. Once we find some armor in those tunnels. Or a ring. It’s miner’s gear, Welsca. Neither you nor I look good with those damn helmets on. So if we find that, once we find that…”
Ilvriss turned. He began walking off, and Rolx’s paws were sweaty now. This had to be a joke. They were going to laugh at him or—
“W-Wall Lord! You can’t just give this—why me?”
He hadn’t worked here as long as some! He was a Gnoll. But Ilvriss just turned back. He looked lighter, and he pulled a lantern out of his bag of holding. He was giving them all away, Rolx realized.
“Make no mistake, Foreman Rolx. In two year’s time, you can quit the Gemscale company. I won’t lock you in here forever. You can go and sell that, or use it and start your own company.”
“Are you insane? That makes no sense, Wall Lord.”
“Well, I expect I’ll have more to give away. I sure hope so. If this is all we ever find, I’ll look mighty stupid, and my father will kill me. My mother, sister, and uncle too. But if I’m right, Rolx…we’re digging up a Walled City’s treasures. They belong to their descendants. I am Wall Lord Ilvriss Gemscale of Salazsar. Son of the walls. Aren’t you as well, Rolx?”
The question hit the Gnoll harder than any speech or [Inspiring Moment] anyone in the Gemscale company had ever used on him.
Aren’t you? For one moment, he saw the Archmage of Izril’s final moments in Fissival on scrying orb. Where she left the Scholarium and her city because they had never wanted her. He saw Ilvriss turn and walk off, and the Wall Lord appeared carefree. Laughing as he began to run—Advisor Merls was back and tried to tackle Ilvriss but went over a safety-railing.
“Thanks.”
That was all the Gnoll said. Then he walked back down into the mining shaft, past laughing [Miners] who asked what the Wall Lord wanted now. Rolx stood in front of a wall of dirt. Gazing at it until they got worried he wasn’t well.
Then he swung the pickaxe. And later, he heard a voice.
 
[Tough Foreman Level 27!]
[Class Change: Tough Foreman → Miner of the Magic Pickaxe Class!]
[Miner of the Magic Pickaxe Level 20!]
[Skill – Bound Item: Pickaxe of Naamreles Assigned!]
[Skill – Understand Enchantment Obtained!]
[Skill – Mighty Swing Obtained!]
[Skill – Disguise of Mundanity (Item) Obtained!]
 
[Rank: Son of Salazsar Rank 1 Achieved.]
[Skill – Dirtdweller’s Friend Obtained!]
 
——
 
The Wall Lord had to admit, he was lightheaded after all that. He had given everything out except for two pickaxes. One for Alrric. And one for Georgie.
The Advance Miner had heard what was going on. How could you not? Rumors were spreading, even if those who had been gifted one of the items of Naamreles were keeping their lips shut.
Most of the staff had understood, after the Hoarding had left them. A few had been put on leave and assigned to [Thought Healers] in Pallass—
The Advance Miner had been avoiding Ilvriss all day. He stood at the edge of camp, lighting the worn cigar. Blowing on it. Hacking.
It turned out Georgie didn’t like smoking it. The Drake broke the silence before Ilvriss could.
“I’ve been thinking.”
“About?”
“The mine I found. Who mines like that?”
“…With the struts, you mean?”
Georgie shook his head. Ilvriss thought he meant the enchanted support struts he definitely wanted to pull out once they could replace them.
“Not that. We never cleared it ‘cause of the undead, but we sent down sensor spells and scout Skills. It has no exit. Not up nor down. But they were digging up. The down…is collapsed. Looks like they collapsed it. Why?”
“Accident?”
“With their gear? Fat chance. They collapsed it and started digging up. Only…that’s a slow death. Not a fast one. They had bags of holding. Maybe spells. You can create free air or store it. I’ve heard of cave-ins where the bastards inside last months. Even years if they had food. But you die slow. And they just kept…digging. Guess which way?”
“Up.”
Ilvriss felt his scales crawl, and Georgie nodded.
“Almost like they were trying to run from something. Once the mine’s cleared of undead, we’re going down. If those undead had popped out, a bunch of [Miners] armed with that stuff could have cleared all of them.”
“Probably. It might get harder after that. Georgie—”
“I tried to steal that pickaxe. Maybe not that one. You don’t want to give it to me.”
The Drake Wall Lord squatted down next to Georgie as the Advance Miner sat, cross-legged. Georgie stared across the plains, and the cigar smoke made it smell better at night. Ilvriss sat, pickaxe across his legs.
“I meant it when I said that I wanted to help you, Georgie. This would be proof of that. You’re not allowed to run off with it. Three years is probably what we’re asking.”
“You don’t know what you’re getting yourself into. You give me that pickaxe and anyone who hates my guts will want a piece of me—on top of what I am.”
Georgie stared ahead. Ilvriss murmured.
“Well, we can address it.”
“How?”
“I don’t know. I keep saying it because I must. Listen, you’re not my first pick either, Georgie. You haven’t worked here as long. You did try to steal this. But I have a feeling that if I pay you out, and let you vanish, you’ll just keep doing this. And your luck will run out eventually. Won’t it?”
“Yeah.”
That soft comment confirmed the other thing Ilvriss understood. He sat there.
“You’re not great, Georgie. But I’ve known worse.”
“—Thanks?”
Ilvriss went on doggedly.
“I’ve met Tessa, who is a lot of things, and not all of it was her fault. The same with Captain Todi. I’ve looked in a mirror. Sometimes, you need someone to throw a frying pan at your head and give you a flaming cup filled with glory. We do give people second chances in the Gemscale Company. I’d wager you don’t get many first chances. Today, after I talked with you, you were the exemplar of an Advance Miner. If I give you the opportunity to prove yourself, in an environment that gives you that trust—can you deliver?”
“You can’t…change what’s wrong with me, Wall Lord. Everyone knows.”
Georgie mumbled. Ilvriss exhaled.
“I can try to talk to the instigators. I know who was behind the attempt on your life. And it seems to me that I’ve been putting it off. If you can give me something, I’ll give you this. Call it a probationary period.”
He held the pickaxe out, handle first. Georgie sat there. The Wall Lord was planning his next move, formulating a plan within the world he understood, which was trying to include Georgie. It was not that hard to include the Advance Miner in there. But for one thing, Ilvriss would have treated the Drake like anyone else. He had already forgotten, most of the time, what lay under Georgie’s clothes because Ilvriss didn’t go around imagining anyone nude.
There was another voice, like the old one that wanted him to take every magical item and hide it away, that understood, though. It spoke a word in the back of Ilvriss’ mind.
Turnscale. He ignored it. He had bested the instincts of his species that told him to Hoard. But…he didn’t understand. Georgie did.
The Advance Miner’s head rose. He gazed at the handle of the magical pickaxe. Then Ilvriss.
“Okay. Okay, I…I can’t turn down that. I’m not gonna make it much longer as an Advance Miner. Not as an old man. My reflexes are slowing down, and if I don’t have good gear and take too many risky jobs—three years for that? I’d be a fool not to take it.”
He was barely past thirty. Ilvriss stifled a comment. They both knew Georgie was pushing it for his profession. The [Miner] went on, voice suddenly raspy.
“I can—I can do it. I just have to fit in, right?”
“If we can avoid someone killing you or starting a fight, yes. The cover story about the curse doesn’t appear to have worked. Master Hedault agreed to write a false report, but I rather think the issue isn’t helped.”
If Georgie had been an ordinary fellow transformed by a gender-swap curse, he’d be a Turnscale. Sort of ironic that the excuse resulted in the same…Georgie nodded.
“Got it. In that case, Wall Lord, d’you have a…a dress?”
“Huh?”
“I can probably get one that fits, and—I mean, there’s women’s gear, but it looks the same, mostly. I’ll just—wear that. And, uh, speak differently. Like this.”
Georgie’s slightly raspy voice changed and raised a full octave. Ilvriss jumped. Advance Miner Georgie just stared at the pickaxe. Played with the handle.
“That’d do it.”
“You—want to put on a dress and pretend to be—I mean, that is to say—”
“If I dress up and apologize, that’ll work, right? Better than getting killed. I’ve done it a few times to get out of trouble. I’ll just cry a bit or something and—then you can say I came to my senses. After a while, it’ll simmer down.”
There was experience in the way Georgie was talking, but his voice was—shaking. Ilvriss sat there in the fading light, watching the way Georgie’s tail was curling in on itself, more than he’d ever seen, and he feared the bones might break.
“You’re sure?”
“Definitely. I’ll get some comments and stuff, but that does it. And—and if you had someone from Miss Xesci’s brothel or one of your people? Someone who didn’t mind, y’know, saying it. They could tell people they’d—that we’d had sex—and put me in my place.”
His voice shrank. Ilvriss sat there, and Georgie shakily took the cigar out of his mouth. Stubbed it against one knee.
“How’s—how’s that?”
“Does that…cover story work?”
“I mean, that’s what happens. Sometimes.”
A cough, hoarse in the night, and Ilvriss listened to the silence for a moment. He sat there, staring ahead at the empty plains, and then spoke.
“Georgie. I don’t mean to insult you in any way. So please take this as objectively as possible. But you and dresses don’t go together. You’d look terrible in them, and I have been in the company of a young Human girl who tries her best to be objectionable—they don’t suit a fellow like you.”
The shivering Advance Miner didn’t appear to hear Ilvriss at first. The Wall Lord went on, feeling his way forwards. Towards the faint light he understood.
“We just had a conversation this morning about making people feel uncomfortable, Advance Miner Georgie. In all good conscience, I cannot make you do something that makes you this visibly distressed. If that’s not who you are, I will not have you dress or behave that way. We’ll find another solution. Not that.”
He watched Georgie’s tail slightly uncurl. The Drake hesitated.
“But if we don’t do that—”
“It would be wrong to do. I am going to address the perpetrators, Georgie. I will find a team you can work with, and I will institute policies to keep you safe. I cannot change people’s minds or obfuscate the information. But I can ensure the people you work with are at least of my disposition. Even if that is not ideal.”
That was the only thing he could think of. Ilvriss wondered—
That’s sometimes how it happened? His scales itched. He had questions, suddenly, for Xesci. Deeply uncomfortable ones he was not going to ask now. Not with Georgie visibly tense. And the Advance Miner looked at Ilvriss and snapped—
“You think that’s just going to work?”
“I very much doubt it will. I’ll keep monitoring the situation, and we’ll have you guarded—”
“You—it’s going to blow up in your face. You think I trust that? What—do you need a loyal Advance Miner that badly? D’you want me to go into whatever’s below the mines? What’s your angle?”
Suddenly, Georgie was on his feet again, suspicious, even afraid. Ilvriss rose.
“Georgie, I’m trying to help—”
“You? You? There’s no Wall Lord or Lady in the city that would help someone like me. What’s with the change? What’s—what do you want from me?”
Paranoia had replaced agony.
“I had an employee, Tessa, who I wanted to help, but I didn’t know how. I failed her, Georgie. I have been rather blind to Turnscales, but I have been told there is an issue. Now I’m seeing it. I’d like to help.”
“You’re mad. Or trying to show off. Are you trying to sleep with that Human?”
Ilvriss twitched.
“The woman I love is dead, Georgie. I mean it.”
“Yeah, well, so do I. I don’t trust this. I don’t trust you. You don’t know me—us. What’s with all of this? There’s no Turnscale in your company; what’s with making me the [Hero] you’re doing everything for?”
“There’s been one in my company at least as long as you’ve been working, Georgie. And while you weren’t ideal, I didn’t have a problem with your conduct aside from the, well, thieving.”
Ilvriss countered. He was worried Georgie was about to literally flee into the distance. The Drake seemed ready to bolt. And this time there might be no Lyonette to march on Tenbault.
“Why me? Just because someone told you to?”
Ilvriss’ face was shadowed as he stood there. Georgie was backing up slowly. The Wall Lord called out.
“I admit, that’s why I listened. I don’t have a good reason, Georgie. It’s simply that when I realized how much unfairness was coming your way, I disliked that. That’s all. I like to believe in fairness, even when it doesn’t truly exist, and you are being treated unfairly. I’d rather have garnished your wages for a month and written you up for trying to steal that pickaxe.”
It spun into the dirt at Ilvriss’ feet and cleaved so deep only the handle jutted out of the soil. Both Drakes stared at it, then Georgie pointed a finger at it.
