“You can’t buy my trust. You don’t know any Turnscales. I don’t want to be your pet project.”
“I don’t want another pet project. I just want to offer a decent Advance Miner a job and not see him dead or miserable. Truly, Georgie, I’d like to not think or worry about it.”
“Well that’s all you’ll have to deal with if you keep me on. You don’t get it, Wall Lord, they won’t stop. Maybe you can deal with this camp. Maybe. When I get back to Salazsar, they’ll put me in the sanatorium. This will keep going on.”
“Then I’ll address it as it comes up. Or we can find you a different posting or send you off. But speak with me, Georgie. Please.”
“How do I trust you? You don’t know any Turnscales. You’re just pretending—”
The two were getting angry. Ilvriss at Georgie for all he was trying to be reasonable, and the Advance Miner’s fists were balled up. Before one of them threw a punch, someone rose from the grass.
Like a shadow. Like an [Assassin].
Or just like Xesci could do when she wanted to be stealthy. Ilvriss and Georgie recoiled. Well, the Advance Miner threw a punch, and Xesci caught it, then threw him over her shoulder. He landed, and she spoke.
“Yes, he does. Me.”
Georgie lay, gasping up at Xesci like a fish. The [Courtesan of Change]’s face shifted to that of a male Drake. A copy of Ilvriss stared down at Georgie, then she shifted again. Her features morphed like putty, and Ilvriss’ scales chilled.
She never showed—
Then a slightly overweight Human man pulled Georgie up. He recoiled. Xesci changed back to the ordinary Drake woman, face resigned.
“It scares people. But I am that, if anyone is. I think I was a Drake woman, once. Now I’m everything. I was listening because I could tell Georgie was about to run for it.”
She turned to Ilvriss. He just stared at her. Then Georgie burst out.
“You—don’t count! I don’t know what you are, but you’re not me. You’re too high-level. You could be anything. Anyone. Why don’t you just…leave?”
His voice broke, and he stared at Xesci, fists balled up, envy in his tone. The [Courtesan]’s smile was gentle and a bit sad.
“I could be that, Georgie, and I know so many people who want to be me, like you. The difference is, you want to be someone else. Just one person. Who you are now doesn’t fit, and it itches and hurts. I? I fit no body. I will never fit again.”
She grew in height, adding two feet, and became a Minotauress. Shrank until she was a Lizardgirl. Twisted around into a Lamia—both men backed away, and Xesci’s voice was a whisper.
“I am only comfortable in change. That is what it means to be eaten by your class. I am a new being. Unique, even if there are those like me. That is what it means to be Level 50. If I wore a face and tried to adopt one of my many guises, there would come a day when I would tear my flesh apart and flee, because it would grate on me until I drove myself mad. Believe me. I’ve tried.”
The [Courtesan of Change] confessed it all simply, then became her ordinary self again.
“But we’re not talking about me. Just you and Ilvriss, Georgie. I try to tell no secrets. But I like gossiping, and you are wrong. Wall Lord, you asked me not to tell you anything that would lower your opinion of the Tidebreaker. Yesterday, I could not tell you this. Today, I will, because I think he would have wanted me to. He is dead and the knowledge out there, so I cannot hurt him. You did know a Turnscale you held in high regard. He just never told you.”
It was Ilvriss’ turn to recoil. To stand there in shock, just like Georgie.
“Zel?”
“The Tidebreaker was…?”
“Oh, he just liked men. Not like Georgie. He and Sserys were both Turnscales. And he was actually quite private—he avoided Turnscale bars. I think he knew how dangerous association was with him, but it left him quite miserable. I met him while soliciting important clients, but he refused to hire me, even when I explained my abilities. I think I unnerved him.”
Xesci kept talking when Ilvriss would have really preferred to sit down or have a long walk. But then she met his eyes.
“It’s not just Zel. There are others. I am not a perfect judge, but I am very good, Wall Lord, and you hired me to find out who was who. It has never colored your judgement before. So now you know. Does it change who Zel Shivertail was?”
Ilvriss saw the Drake getting in Magnolia Reinhart’s carriage, alone. A huge back, heading north to fight the Goblin Lord when no one else would. That disappointed expression he favored Ilvriss with so much.
Tired, alone—the Wall Lord whispered.
“Yes, it does.”
Georgie and Xesci gazed at Ilvriss, and the [Courtesan] searched around for a rock to brain Ilvriss with. The Wall Lord spoke.
“I wish I’d been able to go back in time and help him. I didn’t even know his struggles. We just stood in his shadow until he was gone, and now the water’s rushed in and I’m drowning. Turnscale? We let our hero die just for that?”
He sank to his knees, then. Just fell down, and some of the Rubirel Guard standing in the distance saw and rushed over. Georgie and Xesci went to help Ilvriss up. But all he said was.
“Damn Drakes. Damn us. We’re our own worst enemies sometimes.”
Georgie saw Xesci smile. He looked around, found the stub of a cigar, and put it in his mouth, grass stains or not. He stood there awkwardly as the Drake got up.
“I still think you’re taking too much of a risk on me.”
“Well, consider it the luck of the draw.”
For being a Turnscale? It was the first good turn the word had ever given him. Georgie laughed at that. Then he shrugged.
“Show me, then. I want this. If it’s worth the effort.”
“It’s not, for you. You’re not the best Advance Miner, Georgie.”
Ilvriss assured the Drake, and Georgie glared because he disagreed with that. But Ilvriss just looked past him at Xesci.
“Here’s a selfish way to put it. If there were another Zel Shivertail out there? This is so we don’t lose him. That’s a purely enterprise-driven reason. Good enough? Selfish enough?”
Xesci chuckled.
“There is another Zel. And more will always be rising.”
Both Georgie and Ilvriss gazed at her, and then the Wall Lord brushed at his suit.
“Then I have work to do.”
 
——
 
Wall Lord Dramm was uncomfortable. He felt like he’d been ill-at-ease for days. Ever since he’d found out one of them was in the camp.
Turnscales.
Oh, sure, he was glad the undead were being wiped out. He’d heard about some kind of artifacts being passed around in camp and been trying to get anyone to tell him about them, but he was a junior Wall Lord, even if he was noble, and it was frustrating.
He’d been snapping at his juniors to figure it out, but mostly—it was that Turnscale. Georgie.
Someone had to do something! Why wasn’t Ilvriss—? Was it because Georgie knew something about the artifacts? And they were putting Lacambt on trial for it! They had to know…
When he was summoned to Ilvriss’ tent, Dramm felt his stomach lurch, but he was determined. He’d talk to Ilvriss, Drake to Drake, and sort this out. His father was Brilm, and if Ilvriss were crazy, Brilm would…
It was to Dramm’s surprise and mild outrage when he found himself part of a gathering of Drakes and Gnolls, even. [Foremen] and administrators. A lot of command staff!
“Wall Lord Dramm reporting, sir!”
“Dramm, come in. I think you’re the Drake to speak to. We’re going to have a conversation, Dramm. About Lacambt and Miner Georgie. Have a seat.”
Everyone else was standing. They appeared—uncomfortable. As uncomfortable as Dramm, who spotted some of his subordinates here. Not all. He hesitated.
“Is this a formal hearing, sir?”
“No, Dramm, just a conversation.”
So what’s with all the underlings? Dramm sat.
“Perhaps we should have this conversation in private, sir? It’s a delicate matter.”
Ilvriss looked tired. He poured a cup of disappointing water. Dramm wanted a drink, but Ilvriss didn’t, and the Wall Lord seemed to know it.
“Usually, I’d have conversations like this at a bar, but there aren’t any good ones in Thirthoof and I don’t drink anymore, Dramm. It’s cultural. Funny, I didn’t realize it was. I thought everyone was used to having conversations at a bar, but apparently that’s not where you take work.”
“Really, sir?”
“Humans don’t do it as much. Us? It’s an entire routine. You’d offer to pay for my drinks, and I’d insist because I’m the superior, and then you’d pour us a drink and select my favorite one from the menu.”
That…was how it worked. Dramm felt uncomfortable with Ilvriss laying it out like that. He couldn’t say why. He glanced and caught the eyes of Foreman Rolx, then shifted.
“Well, with respect, sir, sometimes it’s important to be private. If I could speak to you about Lacambt, sir? It’s a terrible incident, violence in camp.”
“It is. Attempted murder. By rights, I should have him shipped back to Salazsar for forced conscription labor or just the local jail for imprisonment for life. Or simply executed. This can be considered a military operation, given Advance Miner Georgie’s efforts against serious monsters.”
Dramm’s blood ran cold.
“Sir, that’s—if I may explain the situation—”
Ilvriss sipped from the shot glass and grimaced—he didn’t have better cups, so he kept pouring and drinking.
“I know I’m not giving you a chance, Dramm. Once we’d had a few drinks and chatted about your father or something light, you’d then have the opportunity to tell me, privately, why Lacambt should be released with a light slap on the tail, if anything. Because he was trying to murder Georgie, whom you believe to be a Turnscale, and he did it on your authority.”
The tent had not exactly been noisy, but people had been coughing, shifting, as confused as Dramm. Suddenly it went silent, and Dramm felt his scales crawling.
“S-sir?”
“It’s not an accusation, Wall Lord Dramm.”
“Oh, phew. I—”
“I know it was you.”
Ilvriss filled the cup again, and Dramm’s stomach was in freefall. The Wall Lord forestalled Dramm’s excuses.
“I don’t have proof, but I’m a Drake of Salazsar. I know how it works. Someone made the suggestion, and you approved it. I very much doubt Lacambt disagreed or he’d have broken a leg or found an excuse. Even put a word in Welsca’s ear so it got to me. We have ways.”
Lacambt had volunteered. Dramm’s mouth was dry, but he forced words out.
“Well, sir, if you understand that, then you understand my position.”
Ilvriss stood slowly.
“Wall Lord Dramm. You had one of your people try to murder one of our [Miners] in his sleep.”
“Yes…sir. A Turnscale.”
Was he being arrested? Was Ilvriss insane? But Dramm straightened his spine because he had witnesses, and they could testify—Ilvriss glanced around.
“I can see the word provokes a lot of reactions. Tell me, what makes Georgie a Turnscale?”
“The fact that she’s—you know, Wall Lord!”
“No, I don’t. Explain it.”
“She’s a woman pretending to be a man!”
Dramm exploded, and his scales writhed. He stood up, slamming his claws on the desk.
“It’s not right! Someone should have put her in the sanatorium or—and she’s walking around and—what’s wrong with you, Wall Lord Ilvriss? Are you mad? My father says you might have hit your head fighting Zel Shivertail! First you live in Liscor, now this? Any decent Drake would do what I did!”
Ilvriss saw Dramm lean over the desk and spoke.
“Sit down, Wall Lord Dramm.”
“I prefer to—”
“Sit.”
The aura knocked Dramm down hard, and he gasped. His father couldn’t use his aura to do more than warm up a bit. But Ilvriss just spoke.
“Dramm, I know your father sent you to spy on me.”
“I—sir?”
Suddenly, Dramm was worried again, and he felt his anger waver. Ilvriss snorted as he poured more water into the cup.
“Brilm’s not exactly subtle. He’s terrible at politics but excellent at fads. You think you’re the only spy? Calistoca, Eschowar—everyone’s got a spy. I’ll wager a decent number of employees in this tent are being hired to report.”
A few shifts of posture, guilty faces. Ilvriss glanced around and shrugged.
“If it’s not confidential, I understand. We are Drakes. Did you ever figure out what I was digging for, Dramm?”
“Not in so many words, sir.”
“Too busy having parties and complaining about the insects. That’s disappointing, Dramm. Anyone with a map and a history book on the region’s figured it out. Maybe Brilm was testing you.”
Now, the young Drake was flushed, hot and cold with worry that Ilvriss was right. The Wall Lord sighed.
“Or maybe he knew from the start and he wanted you to get a bit of that glory. Brilm is my friend, and sometimes I think he likes me more than I like him, which I feel bad about. Maybe he just wanted his son to get a piece of the achievement.”
“Sir?”
Ilvriss took the situation and dumped Dramm on his head. The Wall Lord sipped more of his water.
“You should have asked him what I’m doing, Dramm, when you reported in. He’s your father. Not everything is a test—you’re aware of the magnitude of artifacts I’ve found?”
“Yessir. What does this have to do with…?”
“The Gemscale Company is poised to become Salazsar’s top company within the year if we leverage these assets. Since I’m giving them away, we won’t have that monopoly in the same stored value, but we’ll still probably be the best around. Unless I share the wealth.”
Ilvriss dropped that calmly into the tent, and every eye locked on him. He continued speaking.
“That’s if we packed up tomorrow. If we find what I think we’re going to find, we are going to change everything. Now, do you understand how important this venture is, Dramm?”
“I do, sir. And I’ve done my best—I’m sorry about Liuss.”
The Drake who’d died trying to steal from the damn Humans. Dramm still had nightmares about it. Ilvriss peered down at Dramm.
“Good. It’s no consolation to Liuss or his family, but you have to confront your guilt. He went in that pen, and you could have stopped him. You were the senior Drake there.”
It was true, but Dramm—he saw Ilvriss put his claws on the desk.
“Now, we have a second crisis. A Turnscale in our camps. Something we understand is culturally wrong. But I have indicated, if not in so many words until now, that Miner Georgie is working with me. He found the undead. Nearly died doing his job. But I am clearly aware of Miner Georgie. So why did you take matters into your own claws, Dramm?”
“I was trying to save you the trouble, sir.”
“Trouble.”
“Of dealing with the matter. I mean, publicly.”
That was it. Ilvriss just gave Dramm a flat look. He pushed Dramm’s cup over, and the young man took a drink.
“Sir, don’t you get—”
“Dramm, I’m about to dig up a Walled City.”
Ilvriss cut the room’s oxygen out again. He glanced around, and Dramm was reminded of his audience. The Wall Lord rasped, coughed, and drank water.
“I’m about to dig up a Walled City. And you’re trying to murder a man for being—”
“Not a man, sir—”
“Shut up, Dramm!”
Ilvriss slapped the table, and Dramm jumped. The Wall Lord snapped.
“Let me spell this out from a very basic perspective, Dramm. I’m not going to argue with you about Turnscales. I don’t think I can change your mind. I’m telling you this, to your face. I am about to dig up the City of Purity, and you are trying to murder people and wasting your time on killing a man. If there were Dragons flying overhead, would you be concerned whether or not you had on matching socks?”
But she was a Turnscale! Dramm opened his mouth as he got Ilvriss’ argument.
“Sir, I—”
Ilvriss sat down with a huge sigh.
“I know you think you did the right thing. That’s the problem. I’m telling you you’re wrong, Dramm. And you don’t have to like that. You don’t have to agree. But I am going to find [Foremen] and Mine Leaders and [Miners] and assemble an entire shift who can work with Miner Georgie. Volunteers. It’ll be tedious. It’ll be a waste of time, asking each damn person if they want to work with one [Miner]. If you refuse, that’ll be that. Everyone will continue digging for Naamreles, which will change everything. And your response would be?”
Take me away from that damn Drake, then, if I have to, Wall Lord. Dramm didn’t get what was making Ilvriss insane, but he saw the point. A Walled City? What the hell was ‘Naamreles’? He could see the bigger puzzle. He thought that was Ilvriss’ lesson, but Ilvriss was no [Teacher]. And he spoke.
“If you tell me you can’t work with Georgie no matter what, Dramm, I’ll accept it. I’ll find you work you can do. Then, once your contract is done, or before, if I discover you’re that unable to function, I’ll let you go from our employment.”
“—Sir?”
“You’re only working temporarily for the Gemscale Company. That’s how it works, Dramm.”
“I know, but—”
Is this a threat? Ilvriss read Dramm’s expression.
“No, it’s not a threat, Dramm. I’m just informing you what will happen. The threat is when I say that after I release your contract, and anyone else who hassles Miner Georgie or interferes with my operation over this matter, I will instruct Administrator Alrric to blacklist your name. We share a blacklist with several companies, and you can access them.”
Blacklist him? Dramm’s eyes widened.
“Sir, he’s a—”
Ilvriss spoke over him. His aura pressed Dramm’s jaw shut, and now he was looking around the room.
“You will not work for anyone the Gemscale Company is affiliated with. Any of you. I’m sure you can find work in Salazsar. Plenty of people would applaud you for your position and hire you. That’s their choice. But my company will not. When I divide the objects taken from the City of Purity, I will review my employees and refrain from giving any items to someone I am releasing. And I will not be offering references.”
He threw that last one at the young Wall Lord. Dramm rocked in his chair, then leapt up.
“Wall Lord! I’ve been working for you loyally for months! My father’s your best friend! All this for—”
Ilvriss grabbed Dramm and dragged him over the desk. And there the boy flinched, and Ilvriss stared down at him, then gazed around. Their audience watched.
“This is a meeting where we disagree, Dramm. My father, and his grandfather, wouldn’t do it like this. They’d just call a problem into their office and punch the offender out. Beat them down and kick them out on their heads.”
Dramm was trembling, but Ilvriss let him go.
“I don’t do that. It doesn’t change minds. I’ve laid out my position. Not once have you asked me why. You just repeat that one word like it’s enough to get me to change my mind. As if I’m deaf. That’s disappointing. You do remind me of my father. Brilm used to call Tasilt a traitor. A Furlicker. Now they’re friends, and Tasilt’s a bigger man than Brilm, because he never brings it up. I don’t know if I could.”
His father said that? Dramm opened his mouth, and Ilvriss went on.
“Back when Tasilt married Rhelika, it was a scandal all over the Walled City. People threatened to murder him. Other Wall Lords. There were assassination attempts. Someone poisoned her food, Tasilt said. Now? He’s still a bit of a pariah, but it’s not insane to hear of a young Wall Lord or Lady having a partner who’s a Gnoll. A Human would be insanity, I suspect. But things change. I’m no expert. I was as ignorant as you were yesterday, Dramm. But I am trying to listen and judge objectively. And I read a book when I was confused. It was a book on Salazsarian law. Some parts I disagreed with, but I didn’t see a clause about murdering someone in their sleep.”
“Sir…why does this matter so much?”
“It shouldn’t. I’m just disgusted by murder. You’ve never killed anyone in your life, Dramm. It must be so much easier to countenance, to you. Get out of my office, and if you can show me you’ve changed, I’ll take that. If not, you didn’t work out.”
Ilvriss sat down at his desk and looked around the tent at the various men and women. Some didn’t meet his eyes. Others stood there, as stunned as Dramm. Ilvriss nodded.
“Thank you for your time, everyone. There’s no need to file this; a fifteen minute overtime bonus will be added to your next pay stub. Welsca? Give me three minutes to pee and send in the next wave.”
He waved a claw, sighing. He hoped Dramm would take some of that to heart. It wasn’t really about Dramm; Ilvriss had used the lad for a few reasons.
Firstly, well, he was Brilm’s son, and Brilm was no perfect fellow, but if he could change a bit, Ilvriss owed it to his son to try. Hard as it was. Second, Dramm, like it or not, was a young Wall Lord, and his opinions spread. Hit the critical juncture and you might move the entire seam—or bring it down on your head.
Give people a chance. Ilvriss chuckled.
“It’s still kinder than a frying pan to the face.”
Then he waved at his sweaty chest with a claw and sighed.
“It’s hot as magma out here. I should have gotten Hedault to enchant my tent.”
He checked a timepiece. One group down, at least four more to go. But he suspected he’d sleep well tonight.
And he did.
 
[Wall Lord of Corporate Opulence Level 39!]
 
——
 
Rumors spread from Ilvriss’ dig, of course. Nothing verifiable—yet. But anyone with detection Skills or who could tell how markets moved had noticed what he was doing. And there was already attention there, just not a willingness to go in fully.
Though Deilan wished he had. But he was a busy man and not in possession of all the facts.
“What’s going on in the Cowpat Plains? Is that a real name?”
Lord Deilan El was made aware of the mass of Terlands and Golems moving southwards a bit late. It was causing a stir. But then, he wasn’t his predecessor, Maviola El.
She’d always been good with politics, if not money. She’d known everyone, been able to call on favors, and often needed to in order to keep their impoverished House afloat. She’d been sociable, fiery, charismatic.
He was an artisan at heart. A craftsman who could not fight, but who could improve on designs and run their business better than Maviola could. Deilan dared even say he had a head for advertising; he’d revolutionized the Kaalblades from a failed project into a hot commodity.
Despite them still having tons of flaws. Needing mana stones for fuel, arguably not being that effective on enchanted armor—but he’d done it.
The key was Earthers. Earth. Deilan El didn’t have an Earther, of course. He’d just…interviewed one. A young man named Christopher. The House of El had found him, had a sit-down talk, received a lot of troubling information, and then sent him on his way. He’d accepted gold for the interview, talked about maybe becoming an adventurer, perhaps seeking out another famous Earther—but he hadn’t known where Erin Solstice or Ryoka Griffin were, and he was leery about Liscor after Deilan had offered him a full report on the attacks.
A nice young man, much taken with El creations. Deilan had even gifted him a crossbow and Kaalblade. The [Lord of Splendid Creations] hadn’t insisted Christopher stay with the House of El. He’d been mildly shocked when, later, one of the Wellfar Admiralty had shouted at him about the matter.
“He didn’t want to stay. What should I have done, kidnapped him?”
That…was who Deilan El was. It had confused the [Lady] into silence, because it was an answer she hadn’t expected. In that sense, Deilan El supposed he might have thrown potential profits away. Just like Maviola El, Fulviolo, and his predecessors.
Well and so, he’d answer to her face if they ever met again.
‘Some things matter more than coin.’ It was one of the House of El’s mottos, which people took to be a saying about their creations. Deilan El had just always considered that if he compromised El’s name entirely, Maviola El would arise, and her ghost would make a fireplace incinerate him.
Anyways, the point was he had heard about Earth, thank you, and without Ryoka Griffin’s erratic help, he’d done the logical thing: he’d made the Kaalblades extend and retract without a guide-rod. He’d also colored them so they resembled more of a light beam than crackling magical energy.
This was to make it look like a certain energy-based weapon from what he understood was a very popular mythos from Earth. A bit of work with the handles and a highly impractical Kaalblade with blades coming out from top and bottom, and every nation with Earthers had ordered every Kaalblade he could make.
That, in turn, funded the House of El’s projects in other areas, gave them the wiggle-room they’d always needed to invest in different projects that would hopefully be more lucrative and perpetuate the cycle. The Kaalblades would fund at least half a year of the House of El comfortably, Deilan had projected, and they were saving up coin. If they ran out after that, well, it’d be because they’d tried everything.
So he felt like he’d done a decent job his first year in office, and the family seemed to agree. The one area Deilan knew he was bad at was politics. As a head of one of the Five Families, he had a lot of sway. He hadn’t curtailed Lord Tyrion or opposed Magnolia Reinhart. He’d gone against the Circle of Thorns, but he wasn’t…confident there.
Ulva Terland was the last of the old greats. He didn’t want to oppose her, but knowing what she was doing…Deilan cleared his throat.
“Zelonica? What do we know?”
The half-Elf standing by his desk appeared to be in her late thirties, which meant she was quite a lot older than that. She read from her notes, shrugging.
“Some kind of incident, Lord. I haven’t heard much, and my contacts in the Terland family just told me there was a kind of attack on Ulva Terland. They heard she was injured and rushed back to the Mansion of Stone, then she was on her feet. The next thing anyone knows, she was headed south.”
Zelonica, the half-Elf [Mistress of Accounting], was not her father. Zedalien had been a consummate right-hand man to Maviola and previous generations. Less of a paperwork fellow and more the one who stepped in to make things happen.
But just like Deilan, Zelonica was firmly in the business and was not so much Deilan’s assistant as the head of a bunch of projects. He trusted her. She was the highest-ranked non-El in the family, and he had it in his mind to promote her further. Which was not how things worked normally; you had to be part of the El family.
Well, they could induct her or something. New business practices for a new era. That’s what Christopher had talked about! Less hereditary nepotism…Deilan peered at Zelonica.
“Your contacts. You have them?”
“Oh, yes, Lord Deilan. Friends from when they were younger. Now they’re quite a bit older, but we keep up. Ever since I took the new post, I have tried to establish more of a network. My father had the same, but he complained all his old contacts had passed away due to age. It’s part of why he left.”
“I see. Well, if we don’t know what’s going on—er, try to find out.”
“Yes, Lord Deilan.”
He drummed his fingers on the desk.
“Isn’t Desinee in the south? See if she can make contact. That’s perfect, actually. If Ulva Terland’s going to pull another Tyrion and make war on someone—that’s bad. Just have her keep us apprised. Do we know what she’s doing?”
“Trying to sell to a client in the Cowpat Plains as well. A coincidence, Lord Deilan?”
He paused in grave thought.
“…Is the Cowpat Plains really what it’s called?”
Zelonica laughed faintly as he colored.
“It’s the name everyone else calls it, Lord Deilan. Now, the Terlands aside, we do have a very important client interested in our Kaalblades.”
“Oho. Another Earth-nation?”
“Apparently not, Lord Deilan! Independent and just—taken with the concept. He’s been waiting in our demonstration rooms, and given his rank, I understand you should speak to him yourself. He’s Prince Dafranail of House Taima, of Taimaguros. It’s very important you address him as Prince of Taima.”
“I see, I see. I heard they were visiting. Something of a stir in First Landing?”
Deilan never visited. He was no good with socializing or flirting—and everyone flirted with you when you were head of House El. All the family wanted to know who he’d choose as his wife, and were hinting at him not to ‘pull a Maviola’ and never get married.
Deilan found that highly intrusive into his life. He had no one in mind. All the eligible candidates in the other Five Families were—weird in their own way, was how he’d charitably phrase it—and anyone who wasn’t them was just too different to marry. He didn’t know any Terandrians, let alone nobles or royals in Chandrar and Baleros.
Adventurers or Couriers were common if not the nobility. But they got weird, or so Deilan had been told, and the one eligible bachelorette he had known, if briefly, was taken.
That was Lord Tyrion for you, a menace to civil conversation and your own love life. Deilan changed clothing into something less grease-stained and more presentable, then went down with a fake smile.
“Your Highness of Taima! The House of El greets you warmly. How can we create wonders to suit your needs?”
It wasn’t fancy, but the Prince of Taima offered him a huge, flourishing bow with his cape, and his [Knights of Taima] were impressive, armored in red and gold. It was some kind of color-theme, as was the [Prince]’s huge fur collar despite the heat.
Deilan had done a quick read-through, and Gura apparently liked to theme themselves after the coast and sharks, while Taima occupied the inland. They were rivals despite being one huge dominion…and he really hoped he didn’t need more to close this sale.
“Taima greets you, and Gura grovels to do the same, doubtless, Lord of El. Ah, but I should represent my kingdom equally, for all one part clearly stands above the other. I am most taken with your weaponry, these magic swords, and even the crossbows. I had heard tell they were works of art that Noelictus bought, but I would have not thought them fit for a true warrior. Yet to see them is to wonder: could my [Knights of Taima] not use them? It is doubly refreshing to have a warm welcome. You Izrilians can be so—rude.”
Well, he was a bunch of personality in a bottle. Deilan listened to the little warning bells in his head and was careful as he smiled.
“I apologize in advance for any rudeness, Prince of Taima, Dafranail. My cousins in the Five Families are passing odd to Terandrian sensibilities. It is why we left. I am an [Artisan] as much as a [Lord]. Creation is my desire. We do sell to Veteran Hunters of Noelictus’ Hunter’s Guild. But our weapons have gone to Gold-rank adventurers and our finest works to Named-rankers. We are capable of making such weapons. If the price can be paid.”
“Ah, a man who knows his worth. Then I am doubly interested. I was told by one of your peers—he does not dress to his rank!—that I might see and try the weapons?”
The [Prince of Taima] was a young enough man to be delighted by the chance to try the auto-reloading crossbows and rather impressive target range. It was no expenditure unless he used an enchanted bolt, and the cost of a mana stone to let him hack apart dummies…Deilan thought he might have a sale, and he smiled as Zelonica smoothly stepped forwards.
“Prince Dafranail, I am [Mistress of Accounts] Zelonica, in service to the House of El. Do you have a preference which weapon you’d like to start with, crossbows or Kaalblades? We have a range of weapons, but you are free to test them all as we inform you of their features. Right or left-handed?”
She had two Kaalblades ready, and the [Prince] turned, blinked at Zelonica, then held up a finger.
“Ah, I have but one question. When you say ‘in service to the House of El’, I take this, then, to mean that she is not a noble as you are? As she is a half-Elf, and I doubt one would be married to the blood of Izril, let alone considered such.”
He turned to Deilan, and the [Lord] frowned at the rude question, but he nodded.
“Zelonica is a longstanding employee and trusted advisor, Prince.”
“I see. Thank you.”
So saying, the [Prince] picked up the left-handed Kaalblade from Zelonica’s hand, then, with the same motion, backhanded her across the face. She fell down with a cry, and Deilan blinked down at her. The [Prince of Taima] rubbed his hand ostentatiously.
“Commoners. Addressing me as an equal. I have had no less than ten incidents in First Landing. Doubtless, this is just because she is a half-Elf, and her kind is known to speak out of turn, Lord El. I do not hold it against you. Now, where are these targets?”
He turned as his [Knights of Taima] shifted. Because they had seen how well this encounter ran the last ten times it had occurred in First Landing. All with non-nobles, which had meant the [Prince] tended to get away with it like everywhere else.
Except with the angry [Guildmaster]. However, Lord Deilan El just cocked his head like an owl and peered down his spectacles.
“This way, Prince. We like to calibrate our Kaalblades to each user before use.”
He pointed away from the testing ground, and the [Prince] beamed.
“Ah, this is the House of El’s specialty, as I have heard! Lead the way!”
He strode past Zelonica, who rose shakily.
“I—I apologize, Lord Deilan.”
She spoke to him, carefully not looking at the [Prince] as she held her cheek. The [Lord] led the way into one of the workshops. Several surprised artisans, including members of the House of El who were so gifted, glanced up.
“This is where we assemble the Kaalblades and calibrate them, Prince. This is Irve of the House of El.”
“Er, hello.”
The man was working on a Kaalblade’s runes, giving it the finishing touches as he calibrated it. He offered a gemstone-stained glove to the [Prince], who hesitated, then shook it.
“And what is my blade to be calibrated to? Length? Color, of course, I fancy a red—”
“One second, Prince.”
Deilan took the [Prince]’s Kaalblade and handed it over to Irve, who stared at it nonplussed, as if he had no idea what to do with it.
“Do you want me to calibrate it, Deilan? I can—oh, what happened to Zelonica?”
He focused on Zelonica, who was holding her red cheek. She tried to wave it off, and Deilan smiled. He led the [Prince] over to a huge machine where rune tracings could be printed on the objects via spools that dripped liquid gemstone.
“He struck Zelonica for being a commoner.”
“Hm? For—oh, I see.”
The [Prince] heard the comment and turned his head. Dafranail spoke with a jerk of the chin.
“Standards must be maintained for the Kingdom of Twin Beasts. My cousins in Izril treat commonfolk with soft hands and allow them excesses; but I lead by example. [See It My Way: Noblesse Domine].”
Lord Deilan El’s spectacles flashed as he nodded his head, and the other members of the House of El blinked. Zelonica’s head bowed further. The [Prince] smiled as Deilan motioned.
“Naturally, I see your position, Prince Dafranail. So does the House of El. We are nothing if not proud. Do excuse the clutter. The adjustment machine is right here.”
They did remember, didn’t they? Lord Irve adjusted his oil-stained gloves and stepped back to give the [Prince] room as they edged past the assembly line. Somewhere along the long and winding way through the workshop floor, the [Prince]’s bodyguards slowed to avoid the workers who were getting up. Nobles and craftspeople. The [Prince] was frowning at the contraption.
“If it takes too long, I will use a simple Kaalblade, Lord Deilan. After all, one cannot rush such things, and I desire to test them.”
“Not at all, Prince. If you’d care to look forwards? We need to get a definite read on your features.”
“Fascinating. That my likeness might activate the Kaalblade and none other? Where do I look?”
“Straight at the side of the metal, please. Right there—thank you.”
The [Prince] stared ahead obligingly, then turned his head to ask what he was gazing at, because he just thought it was a side of the metal contraption. That was when he realized Lord Deilan had gotten on a workbench. Then the [Lord] kicked the [Prince]’s head into the side of the metal rune-printer.
He wasn’t good at fighting, and Maviola had always said that if he was going to start a fight like that, he’d better have the advantage of surprise, height, something hard and painful, or ideally all three. He leapt down as the [Knights] began to shout, then the crack-hiss of a dozen Kaalblades igniting made them go silent.
Lord Deilan landed on the [Prince], who made a hublagh sound. Then he just improvised. And he thought—well, he was no Maviola El. But it was amazing how many moves you could think of when you were mad. Here he’d thought he was so even-tempered. But even the [Lord] of House El could produce a flying drop kick when needed.
Anyways, the consequences of that incident and the other ones with Taimaguros were another story.
 
——
 
A number of events were taking place over the course of Wall Lord Ilvriss’ long dig. Seemingly unconnected as Izril’s north changed.
The war between Assassin’s Guilds in the north.
Wing Captain Osthia’s new command and Skill.
A [Princess] making use of her Skills and new inn to dance.
Diplomat Nerul Umbral Throning his way to the respect of [Lifters] and Golems.
Miner Georgie finding himself agreeing to a three month extension on his contract with options for permanent employment if he was interested.
Lady Damia signing the same version of the contract eagerly, then asking if Georgie was doing anything for dinner. He eyed her. She was very curious.
Let alone the elections and more.
But the result was that the world was changing very quickly, and for once, Ulva Terland felt it.
She was on the road, sitting in a carriage pulled by Golem Horses, a rare pair of bluemetal horses whose eyes glowed yellow and stormed down the roads. It wasn’t a fast carriage, and she was upset.
…Mostly because Wistram’s Magical CarriageTM kept passing her conveyance on the road. She was so annoyed that the third time they began blaring their obnoxious advertisements, she snapped.
“—discount on any bulk travel for members of the nobility! Apply at your local Mage’s Guild for—”
“Destroy the carriage. Don’t harm the passengers.”
Obligingly, one of the two Golem horses turned its head and projected a [Ray of Force] at the Wistram Carriage. One wheel exploded, and the magical carriage crashed down and began disintegrating.
“WARNING! ANY ASSAULT ON WISTRAM PROPERTY WILL—”
“Take out that one too.”
There was a scream, and the second carriage swerved away too late. Ulva listened to the explosion and sat back.
She was Ulva Terland, and despite politics, despite anything else, she was a Terland of the Five Families. She could play nice, but no one insulted a member of the Five Families on their lands. That was why some of the news didn’t strike her as bad.
“So Deilan El broke a [Prince]’s jaw. So what? Who’s censuring him?”
“Several of the family and other—”
“Golems, identify each member of House Terland who condemned the House of El and paddle their behinds ten times. That Terandrian brat backhanded one of his people. We are the Five Families. Clearly, the kingdoms of Terandria have forgotten what that means.”
Everyone had. That was abundantly clear; House Terland hadn’t gone south like this in an age. And their slower speed was still fast—but the reason they weren’t already at Invrisil was simple.
The Golem-Carriages were pulling a second trailer behind the first. People turned and gawked at the carriage that Ulva Terland was sitting in—and then the long, rolling platform with Golems standing on it.
Golems locked into place but active, ready to break free of their restraints as need be. Not just one ‘trailer’ of Golems. No less than six carriages were following Ulva Terland, with their own nobility and Golems.
She had brought an army. Not one in great numbers, but no less than sixty Golems herself—and her family, the meddling flies, had followed her with three times that many.
That was enough Golems to win a war if you had [Soldiers]. And some of the Golems…were ones who had won said wars.
The carriage that Ulva sat in had been designed to let her converse with Golems, so it was huge, reinforced, and had a back door that led to the trailer—a Golem was standing in a holding area behind Ulva’s seats. There was a faint click-whirr coming from its chest.
He was a Timekeeper Golem with a Golem’s Heart that was made up of little gears moving around an inner power source. The reason for all the machinery instead of a full magical Golem’s heart?
All his limbs were likewise connected by the gears instead of just magically animated. The overdesigned Golem came from antiquity so old that Ulva Terland suspected it predated the Long Night. It was highly efficient due to the gears and far stronger than a regular Golem because it could leverage the gear-system to augment its arms, rather than relying solely on magic for strength. She had seen it once push the Pride of the Wellfar out to sea when it had run aground.
Alone.
But this Golem was not invincible. One of its kind had been with Petria when she died. The sister-Golem who had served House Terland for ages…this Golem was male, at least in shape, with a carved face that looked like an automaton’s, a cylindrical head with basic features and a faded cap on his head. They called him Timekeeper, her companion of old.
Ulva didn’t talk to Timekeeper. She addressed a nervous pair of younger Terlands and one of the serving-Golems in the carriage for lesser orders like spanking the Terlands.
“As for this Assassin’s Guild, I don’t know Calidus Reinhart. Honest, is he?”
“He’s a wastrel, a pervert, and a gambling drunk, Lady Terland.”
She snorted.
“Good. If he was pretending to be honest, I’d not trust him. Cooperate with Lord Etril Wellfar or the Veltras family and put Hunter-Killers on the Assassin’s Guild. Don’t lose them. If any of our family are still with this Circle of Thorns, tell them to knock it off or I’ll strip them of their Golems.”
Awake. She was awake and exasperated. The family just jumped and stared at her, as if they’d forgotten…
No, it had been over ten years. They had forgotten what she was like before this, and so had she. Just like the Reinharts, Ulva had come back to life.
But she was still damn grumpy.
I am going to feed that Drake his own tail. As for Emperor Godart, he is less objectionable, annoyingly. He took charge when everyone else was fleeing or incompetent. The Summer Solstice…but there is tolerating him and then there is allowing him to do what all empires do. I will remind him of his place if needs must.
Small wonder Xitegen had gone south if he’d taken notice. No one else had. Ulva snapped.
“Send a [Message] ahead once we’re within range of Invrisil. We’ll give that Drake a chance to explain himself. I want to inspect this magic door. Is the inn around there?”
“Possibly, Lady Ulva, but it might be north or south of there—”
“It connects to Pallass and Invrisil. Around Liscor sounds right.”
Ulva raised her brows. That was basic geography. But the blank looks from her family—a voice interrupted her.
“La-dy Ter-land. Your fa-mi-ly is under the effects of an ob-fu-sca-tion Skill. Widespread…effect.”
Timekeeper’s voice had a staccato quality, and it sometimes slowed down, a sign of his age. Ulva got annoyed instantly.
“[Awaken, Hearts of Stone]. Timekeeper, your heart. Beat. Give me a walker’s pace.”
Tic-whirr.
Tic-tic-tic-whirr.
Tic-tic-tic-whirr.
She saw his dull, bronze eyes with their faded green cores light and her family sat back. Awed. Ulva just smiled.
“Now, speak.”
“As thou wilt it, Lady Ulva Terland, so awaken we shall. This Golem estimates a widespread Skill effect at Level 50 intensity or higher. Worldwide.”
“Really?”
“The detection-matrices have expired, but in company, Golem X-1ED detects consistent readings with past effects.”
“Exled? We brought that one out of storage? That Golem’s only good for pervert children spying through walls. Who okayed that?”
Ulva frowned until Timekeeper spoke.
“This Golem was direct-ed to assemble a force capable of countering Drake supremacy and covering contingencies at ‘jogging pace heartbeat’.”
“Hmpf. The wisdom of Golems. Thoughts?”
“Ma-ny, Lady Terland. It is good to think. We grieve your sister’s death.”
One of the [Ladies], Eldertuin’s wife, Alorelle, was choking. She must not have been in the Mansion of Stone around Petria and Ulva as a girl. She was young…and their inherited Skill was not oft-used. It taxed Golem Hearts, obviously. But it had effects no Golem-lover could but dream of. Ulva was too experienced to be in awe; she snapped back.
“Don’t lie to me. You don’t have the ability to mourn with Petria dead. Timekeeper…did we salvage her?”
“There was no possibility of such.”
“Well. I’m sorry.”
“Thank-you, Lady Ulva. Another thought. Are we to deploy for combat?”
“That depends on the Drake. I wish to visit the inn as well, and there are—things I want to check out. That Drake who took Petria’s face will answer to me. Keep an eye out and tell me if there are shenanigans afoot.”
Shenanigans. Petria would have laughed at the word, but Timekeeper just nodded.
“It shall be done, Lady Ulva. Shall the other Golems’ hearts beat? It would im-prove efficacy.”
“…Give me a single pulse per minute. Beat yours harder if you must, but I don’t intend to lose Golems.”
“As thou will, Lady Ulva. Fear not. I was made for your Skill.”
Then he remained still, and she sensed the other Golems stirring. Ulva turned back, and Alorelle burst out.
“Great Aunt UIva, you can bring the Golems to life? I heard—”
“Silence.”
“But I have—”
“I am not entertaining questions. Get me something to eat. Ask your seniors what it means.”
“But Aunt—”
“Eject her from the carriage.”
There was a scream, then protests, frantic orders—Timekeeper got up, lifted Alorelle up gently, and carried her out onto the trailer compartment. Which had no seatbelts or anywhere to sit, just the wind rushing past as they moved at considerable speed—
Ulva waited until the door closed and the screaming stopped. Alorelle wouldn’t die. Spoiled, the lot.
She took a pair of chopsticks and salivated at some Drathian sushi. Then wrinkled her nose.
“Why is this in a bowl and arranged so? It’s not rolled.”
“It’s a new invention from Liscor, Lady Terland. A ‘Poke bowl?’.”
“Hmph. Damned children from another world or whatever nonsense Xitegen keeps screaming about. Tasty. I like the concept.”
She had taste again and more of a lust for life. All her old fear…Ulva kept eating in good humor until the carriage slowed slightly. Then she heard shouting and sighed.
“That brat.”
Speak of the Demons. Lord Xitegen Terland himself had come north to intercept his Aunt. And by the looks of it, run the entire damn way. He pulled himself into the carriage, sweating like a pig, and she snapped.
“Out!”
“Come now, Great Aunt. Primera, cleaning routine. I came all this way—are you eating those weird bowls of su-shi? I’m sick of seafood. There’s too much of it in Liscor. I’ll have two.”
Primera had swung herself into the carriage. The huge Golem sat and began speaking.
“At once, Lord Xitegen. [Cleanse]. [Cleanse].”
That was…new. Not in Primera’s construction reports. Ulva eyed Xitegen.
“What is that? New Skill?”
“Reconstruction and upgrading Golems, Great Aunt.”
Her mood went back to cheerful, and she let him stay. She’d been about to have Timekeeper eject him just to teach the boy manners.
“Hah! You are a fit heir for the House of Terland. We used to let only someone with the ability to upgrade or maintain our Golems make family head. Stupid rule.”
“Logical, Great Aunt, in its way. Do we still love logic in the family or is it all for fits of rage?”
He settled back, took a spoon, and began devouring his bowl. Ulva clacked her chopsticks at him. When he raised his brows, she spoke.
“Primera, toss the spoon out a window.”
“Lady Ulva, I am Lord Xitegen’s—”
“[Awaken, Primera]. Toss the damn spoon.”
Primera froze—and then her heart began to pulse. She snatched the spoon from Xitegen’s hand, tossed it out the window, and sat back. Staring at him. At Timekeeper, who nodded to her. And Xitegen himself went still.
He’d been but a boy when the Goblin King rampaged. Eleven? Ulva didn’t remember. That had aged him, but she knew he didn’t remember this Skill. But he was better than Alorelle. He fumbled the chopsticks, cursed, then spoke.
“I haven’t heard you use that Skill in a decade, Great Aunt. May I ask about it?”
“Since you’re to inherit, go ahead. You can play around with it once I’m dead.”
His eyes flickered.
“Ah. It only passes to each new head?”
“In the case of Petria and I, both. It’s not as fun as it looks.”
“…So less fun than the most amazing Skill in existence half the family would murder for, Great Aunt?”
She snorted as she reached out.
“Water.”
It was handed to her, and she drank, grumbling as the carriage bounced and she got water on her dress. Primera reached out to wipe it off, and Ulva slapped her hand down.
“Don’t attend me.”
The Golem recoiled, and Xitegen stared at her. Ulva smiled, not entirely nicely.
“See how she moves? She’s thinking. It can turn even low-grade Autonomous Golems into Sentient-class, though it wears out Golem Hearts proportionally to how well they were made. Low-grade will be destroyed in a single battle at full roar. Higher-grade Sentient-class or Sentient-capable like Timekeeper here turn fully Cognizant.”
“Lord Xitegen. Heir. Greet-ings.”
Timekeeper spoke, and Xitegen nodded to him.
“So we refrain from the Skill with greatest effort because of the damage to our Golems, Great Aunt?”
“Double hah!”
Double hah? Xitegen exchanged a glance with Primera, then stopped, delighting in how she was expressing awareness. Thinking. And Ulva snorted.
“I can see you thinking you’ll make Primera and Seconda awaken nonstop. You won’t. Primera, state your feelings. Any you’d like.”
The Golem woman, porcelain made to be a statue, albeit with more functional limbs than most, hesitated. Then she blurted out, too fast—
“I do not believe my recreational functionalities are best employed with intercourse, Lady Ulva.”
The [Lady] smiled once as Xitegen went still. He dropped his chopsticks, and she spoke.
“Now that she can think, she can tell you how she feels. Don’t think of it as rape. The family doesn’t like to; she couldn’t think before this.”
“Great Aunt, are you trying to hurt me more or…?”
“I’m teaching you the lesson the family learns, boy. I keep Timekeeper around because he’s one of the smartest. And he likes me for whatever that means. They don’t have feelings, but logic which can mimic emotions and everything else.”
Primera avoided Xitegen’s gaze. Ulva amended her statement.
“Well, she might if you upgraded her. You’ll find which ones work best with you. You think this is bad? Some Golems we have can be awoken. The first and only thing they do is beg for freedom. Timekeeper does not, hence why we work together. And that’s a choice, isn’t it, Timekeeper?”
“They are in-correct in their desires, Lady Ulva.”
That was all the Golem said, and Ulva watched Xitegen and his Golems. Some of the color returned to Xitegen’s face.
“I see. May I have a conversation with Primera?”
“Later. This is what it is to be head of House Terland, boy. You wonder why Nouzcrat didn’t jump at it? Golems are meant to serve, and any notion to the contrary will be addressed when you ask some what they want to do. Or just read a history book. Now, tell me about Liscor.”
Xitegen truly was a [Warrior] by blood; despite her hitting him with all these shocks, he was back to civil conversation in minutes. Though he stopped eating, and he moved away from Primera.
“If you’re going to make war—”
“Not on the inn. But I remember Drakes, and that’s Wall Lord Zail’s son. He won’t be assassinated.”
“I heard you shot him in the testicles…?”
“He stopped my heart. We will have a meeting, and he’ll have a chance.”
Xitegen relaxed slightly. He nodded.
“The south’s gone a bit wild, Aunt. Why so many Golems?”
“In case the meeting doesn’t go well. I’ll leave a few with you. I hoped the Bloodfeast Raiders would pop up, or that Kraken Eater tribe. I have a mind to kill something.”
“Great Aunt, the inn—”
“Shut up. I can read reports. Your job is to tell me what’s real and what’s not.”
Xitegen sat back, blinking, and then spoke.
“Well, the [Innkeeper] can’t breathe blood, and she’s less random and silly than you might have been told. But far from infallible. They don’t know Golems, Great Aunt. We’re an unknown power to them.”
“You don’t know Golems, brat. Good enough. Now, tell me about the Winter Solstice.”
She listened. Timekeeper listened. Ulva trusted his opinion, for a given value of it. Later, she’d tell him to spin up his heart and listen to his thoughts. And she saw Xitegen glancing at Primera.
Will you continue despite the truth, or change? She felt slightly bad for him. He hadn’t known, and he’d been raised a Terland. But she needed a replacement. She’d died once, and she was not confident she would endure long in this changing era. First, though, before she set her house to rights and took what was needed so Terland might endure—
First, she had to know what her sister had meant. The Wall Lord was just a bonus. But as Xitegen spoke, Ulva sensed how much the south had changed. By the time she got to Invrisil, Ulva feared it might have created something new entirely.
 
——
 
When Wall Lord Ilvriss reported into The Wandering Inn, it was the week of elections.
He didn’t really care, but it explained the holdup and hubbub in the city. They were eating out at a restaurant called Crab’s Claw. It was very good.
A Centaur wearing a fake mustache and [Chef] with a non-fake eyepatch were eating at a far table, sniping the menu. Ilvriss was just enjoying good food.
“You needn’t insist we go out, I feel bad for Chef Calescent, Lyonette.”
“Nonsense, you’re the only person who doesn’t scarf his food, Ilvriss. I have to have a lunch partner!”
Lyonette pointedly glanced at Nanette, who was licking her plate with Mrsha. Ushar went to scold both girls.
“Ladies do not lick their plates clean.”
“They do if the [Chef] is a Gnoll. Don’t be racist, Ushar.”
Yeah! We’re over-quota for the week!
Ilvriss snorted. He had been telling them about his days, much to their interest. Actually, everyone had had a bit of a stir.
“I’m only sorry Lady Damia couldn’t visit, but she’s a bit of a handful. Excitable. Plus, she wants to work with Miner Georgie, and someone has to. But Captain Asrira seems…better.”
He hesitated when he said that. Everyone’s head turned. Lyonette coughed.
“Is she, ah, the Drake who was buying drugs from Palt with a gang of women?”
“…It’s morale-building. I think she’s cut loose a bit.”
Captain Osthia did indeed seem a bit more casual than the upright, uptight Drake he remembered. She was using her wings like an umbrella. By contrast, her ‘squad’ of Sisters seemed a bit more professional. A middle ground between the two wasn’t bad. Ilvriss waved at her, and she gave him a thumbs up and walked over. All the Sisters were immediately drenched and cursed her.
“No interest in joining us, Captain?”
“No, Wall Lord. With respect, the conversation’s above our pay grade, and I have to keep an eye on that lot. They have it in their heads to tangle with the Brothers. Some kind of gang rivalry. Princess.”
She nodded at Lyonette, who nodded back. Osthia strode back over and kicked one of the Sisters in the ass, sending her into a gutter. Laughter, swearing—Ilvriss spoke.
“She seems happier.”
“Despite being in a squad that’s first into combat?”
“Yes. It’s her squad. I think before that it was just her, Lyonette.”
The [Princess] was troubled, but Ser Dalimont understood. Osthia might lose her squad, but she’d already lost her army. Now she had a kind of family again. Grim though it was, dead gods, he got it.
“The Sisters are cool. But can you explain about the Georgie thing, Wall Lord?”
Nanette wanted clarity, so Ilvriss re-activated his privacy rings and delivered the rest of the story. The Wandering Inn was, of course, the place that had put him onto this journey to begin with. Lyonette was a bit dubious, but she agreed he’d done everything right.
“It sounds like it could be a mess, Ilvriss.”
“When I go home. It was the right thing to do, I think. I just hope Miner Georgie sees it that way and gives us a chance.”
“Oh, er, Georgie, of course! One cannot just kill a man like—but giving away all the artifacts!”
Oh, that’s what she meant. Ilvriss gave Lyonette a mildly pitying gaze.
“Here’s someone else who never got out of their Hoarding years, eh, Mrsha?”
It’s a pathetic trait among royalty and Dragons, forsooth, Ilvriss. But we humor her.
The [Princess] reddened and spluttered.
“I just meant it makes no economic sense, let alone—!”
Ilvriss laughed, and Nanette held up a hand.
“I quite approve of all your actions, Wall Lord. I just would like to point out that while you did give away a lot of value to your employees, it was still a nobleman giving ‘down’ to the poor folk who have to work through his company. It seems to me that ideally, the employees and that [Foreman] would take the share they deserve, not need you to come swooping in like a [Knight].”
She gave him a challenging look full of teenage righteousness, and Ilvriss lifted a spoon to his mouth and blew on his soup. He supposed that was a very [Witchy] way to look at things. He took a bite of the prawn soup, which was excellent, then licked his spoon for politeness, and then replied.
“A very fair assessment, Nanette. Welsca will be delighted to know you’ve adopted her pajamas for daily wear, incidentally.”
“Don’t dodge my statements with cries for mercy, Wall Lord.”
“Hardly. I think you’re right, Nanette. There is an inequity built into anyone who wants to get a free and fair start with a level playing field in Salazsar. Most, if not all cities, are like that.”
She blinked as he lifted a foreclaw.
“Alrric and I commented on it, once. I’m aware this is still largesse from the noblesse, but I ask you this: what should I have done otherwise?”
“Well, like I said—”
“No, no, not what Foreman Rolx would do, or my employees. Could I do any better in the circumstances? I am the Wall Lord of the Gemscale Company, and I can only do what is possible within my role. You are free, of course, to talk to my people, but if you have any comments on what I could have done, I’ll certainly listen.”
Nanette hesitated. She opened her mouth, glanced at Mrsha, who shrugged, and then blushed slightly.
“Well…I don’t have that many notes for you. Um, that might have been a bit preachy. I’ll just go and hang out with the Sisters for a second. Mrsha, coming?”
The two got up to learn horrible lessons, and Ilvriss sat back in his chair.
“I got that unrepentant nuisance.”
Lyonette nearly snorted soup out her nose. She laughed as she high-fived Ilvriss, and Nanette spun around to her horror at their blatant celebration of his victory. And Ilvriss just smiled.
“It’s been a relatively good day. No Golems, only a few raids from the [Ranchers], and Nerul says he has a plan and he’ll be en-route shortly. Though, apparently, he has to charter a huge flight of Wyverns because his cargo’s so heavy. And since Fissival hates us, it’s Manus.”
“What could it be?”
“No clue. Maybe it’s enough drinks to drown Marvus Lischelle. But I’m feeling upbeat. Shall we dance, Princess?”
“Oh, you might twist my arm, Ilvriss.”
They were both in a good mood. The two were getting up, chuckling, as Ser Dalimont went to settle the bill when they heard shouting from outside. Boos, jeering—and they turned and saw the election in full swing.
Lism Swifttail and Krshia Silverfang had been spotted, and a crowd had formed in the street. There were members of the press taking [Magic Pictures], someone with a scrying mirror pushing forwards, and Krshia was trying to say something. Tugging on Lism’s arm.
The purple-scaled Drake was red-faced, and he ducked a potato being hucked at him. Ilvriss muttered.
“Ah, is it still bad?”
“Very bad for Krshia. Imani’s been trying to reverse opinions, but—Imani!”
The undercover [Chef] came over. Palt hesitated before scowling his way forwards. Ilvriss stopped him before he could bump Lyonette hard.
“Magus Palt. Lyonette is injured.”
“Er, sorry, Wall Lord. How’s the digging?”
Palt’s expression turned abashed as Ilvriss gave him a cool glance. He shuffled his hooves. Imani and Lyonette had barely noticed. Imani spoke to Ilvriss.
“I’ve tried, but Krshia’s community’s really upset. I can’t really blame them. And Lism—ah, shit.”
Lism was exploding with fury after a week of this. Ilvriss eyed him and groaned.
“Uh oh. I think he’s having a Noass moment.”
It was like young Dramm or any hothead. You could almost see the lid on Lism’s temple burst, and sure enough, he thrust Krshia’s paws away, grabbed a stone that projected his voice out over the crowd and rain, and began shouting.
“That’s it! I’ve had enough!”
Jeering, the crowd shouting insults at him like ‘traitor’ and ‘liar’, and then Lism’s voice drowned them all out. He swung around.
“Yes! I admit it! You’ve got proof! We’re a couple! We—”
He grabbed Krshia’s shoulder and there were more boos, more thrown objects. Lism caught a tomato and blocked three separate Yellats in one move. That was impressive enough to earn him another shout.
“And yes, we worked together! Don’t shout at me; I know I ran on an anti-Antinium, anti-immigration, anti-Krshia campaign! I was the one who came up with all that! But you know what?”
He looked around, breathing in and out.
“—When I got to the Councilmember’s chamber and I realized the idiots wanted us to just ‘keep on like normal’, and they didn’t see a thing wrong with the damn rent, you know what I realized? We had bigger enemies than Krshia! That she and I had worse to deal with than some damn Humans or the Black Tide! We had damn corrupt [Landlords], and we had to lower the rent!”
He threw up two claws on both hands in victory signs.
“So we did that! We worked with the Antinium to build the city out, and we passed all the laws to keep the Guilds from scooping more gold out of honest Liscorians’ pockets! You know why I was okay with that, and why Krshia Silverfang and I got along? Because we realized we could do more together than apart. If we hadn’t done that, 3rd District would never have been built! There wouldn’t be Humans or all these jobs—”
“You hated her! You’ve always hated her!”
One of Lism’s die-hard supporters stood up on something in the crowd to bellow back. Lism’s eyes popped.
“You think I don’t remember that, Swall? Yes, I hated her, but when we got to know each other, you know what? I took a look at this lovely Gnoll here and I fell in love. What kind of red-blooded Liscorian wouldn’t take his shot with a Gnoll like Krshia Silverfang? Come on up so I can show you what a [Liar] looks like!”
He pointed, and suddenly everyone turned to stare at Krshia Silverfang, who looked ready to murder Lism or turn invisible or both. Lism went on, shouting.
“Yes, I slept with my fellow councilmember who was my opponent! And you know what? I’ll do it again!”
Ilvriss coughed as he took a sip of water, and Lyonette put her face in her hands. Lism just kept going.
“All you naysayers who want to vote for that idiot—he’s the old Council. He just wants to remove the rent controls, put the Guilds in power, and stop working with the Humans, the Antinium, and lick the Walled Cities’ boots! You want that? Vote for him! But if you think this city is better than it was a year ago—vote for me. Not because of my policies or platforms—I’ll change them if I don’t think they’re working! Vote for me because I did this!”
He waved his claws around. Then he leapt up on something. Perhaps a literal soap box.
“Vote for my way! The Lism way! The only way this city gets even better! I’m—”
At that point, someone threw an entire fish at him. Lism went argh—fell down, and then it was a scrum. Ilvriss watched, sipping his drink, until he saw enough members of the Watch protecting Krshia and Lism as they were dragged away. Then he turned and shrugged.
“Well, I think that settles that. Best of luck to both of them.”
He got up and put down a tip as Lyonette walked off to dance.
 
——
 
Okay, at some point Lyonette realized that her activities were suspiciously like a date. She had told Rhisveri he was imagining things when he’d accused her of it.
He was sick or something; he’d come into the inn, all bundled up like his sock puppet alter ego, accused her of ‘romancing Ilvriss’, and then wanted to do the dance. She’d asked him why he was hunched over and couldn’t stand straight. He’d teleported himself out, and she’d put it down to poppycock and a fever.
But when you wrote down the things she and Ilvriss had done when he’d ridden back to Liscor, well…it made sense they had food out in the city. They wanted to give Calescent time off.
Naturally, after that, Lyonette had shown Ilvriss some more new shops and attractions in Liscor since he didn’t know them all. They’d then gone back to the inn to practice dancing—purely because they wanted to do the Highstepper—and after that, they’d have dinner, probably together since they would be hungry after dancing for a while.
She’d not thought that was amiss. Except when she’d vouchsafed to Xinthe that she’d be all sweaty, and couldn’t she bathe before dinner? To which, the trying-to-be-helpful Calanferian servant had asked if Lyonette wanted to try some new bathing facilities that Hexel had just finished. And did she want to share the large baths with Wall Lord Ilvriss?
The [Princess] had drawn breath to castigate Xinthe, then it had hit her. She turned, and Wall Lord Ilvriss mistook her look for an inquiry.
“I can do it! Once more time. Damn you, legs! We almost had it. One victory on top of the rest—”
He heaved himself up as Ser Dalimont offered him a stamina potion. Even Colfa, who liked dancing, couldn’t persuade her husband to dance this much—or wanted to train as obsessively.
But Ilvriss was a competitive Drake; he just didn’t have as much of a chance to show it. He was a good leader, and he could only be a person around Lyonette, sometimes.
Just like she was the boss of the inn and with him she could actually enjoy good food and…
Oh no. No, he lost Periss and—is it me?
Lyonette got up, guilty. Her magical boots let her cross the floor nimbly as she had before, and she hesitated.
“If you have somewhere to be, Ilvriss—you’ve had a long week. We could halt, and you could do something else.”
He was adjusting his collar so he could let his chest breathe. She noticed sweat sliding down his body suddenly and resolved to let Mrsha bully Xinthe as much as she wanted.
“Like what? Lyonette, frankly, I have no excuse to visit Liscor while everyone else is in the dig site. If you take this from me, I’ll have to be responsible nonstop.”
“Well, we can’t have that. One try?”
Lyonette bit her lip, and she began to move into the dance almost unconsciously. But she made two mistakes. The first was that she forgot which dance they were doing. She began to do Erin’s mysterious dance, and Ilvriss nearly slammed into her before he adjusted. And Lyonette was so preoccupied with her thoughts, she used a Skill.
[Flawless Attempt].
Just so she could think about the ethics and ramifications and—her body moved, not effortlessly flowing into each step, but flawlessly. Her body thrilled as she moved like a [Dancer], in a way that would have had even the Eternal Court of Calanfer on their feet if a certain Dame Ushar had taken a clandestine recording of Lyonette practicing. However, Ser Dalimont accidentally tripped the [Knight] as she was going for a better vantage point and coincidentally turned the scrying orb off as he helped her up. Lyonette barely noticed, and Ilvriss was all concentration.
Grace. She hadn’t used [Flawless Attempt] on a dance since they’d begun practicing. It didn’t teach you the dance. It was cheating, like the boots, only—more. So she was surprised, then, when she realized Ilvriss was keeping up with her.
He swung around with her, matching her move for move, and her eyes widened.
He has been practicing. The Wall Lord stepped back across the ground, and his eyes were so filled with concentration she felt it. Then she realized they were at the silly part of the dance.
Oh no. Erin’s stupid, ridiculous quack-quack dance. Lyonette did it, blank-faced, feeling her [Flawless Attempt] running out—it wasn’t perfect. She forgot the next part—did she Cossack dance then hop up? She leapt up too early and cursed.
Wait, now we swing each other around by the hands—she was out of place. So Lyonette was going to wait when she saw Ilvriss jump forwards. He abandoned his wavy-noodle arm move mid-wave and reached out.
They grabbed each other’s hands and swung around. Lyonette blinked. She stumbled back and missed a beat.
“Wrong—oops—wait—”
She forgot another move and began stepping left, arms spread. Ilvriss copied her, moving to the right. Arms identical to—
Wait.
He was keeping up with her. Copying her moves? The third time, Lyonette deliberately—
She stopped dancing. Then lifted her chin. Glided forwards in a walk, performing the Aubrel Solde, a classic Terandrian dance. And she saw Ilvriss walking at her. Eyes wide. Posture almost mimicking hers, but when her head and hand rose, he knelt in front of her hand.
She knew he didn’t know this dance. He took her hand, and she gazed down at him and felt it. Something flowed around her.
No—
Him.
Magic. She stepped back, and they were walking together until their hands clasped. They began to walk, reversing, turning, tracing the steps of a dance he did not know. She saw his eyes glowing.
“Ilvriss…”
That one breathed word broke the spell. He stumbled, feet growing uncertain, and caught her. But she was laughing.
“Ilvriss! What was—you did it!”
“I did? Didn’t you…? I thought when you were deviating—I did! It was—that’s why Erin put in those stupid dance moves! She knew it wasn’t meant to be part of the dance! She wanted us to deviate or make a mistake and—we did it, Lyonette!”
“No, you did!”
She was wondering and jealous and embarrassed. Ilvriss didn’t understand until she told him she’d been using [Flawless Attempt]. And flawless…wasn’t how you danced like magic.
“But—what did I do? I…my legs are killing me. I was in the flow, and I knew you’d made a mistake.”
“Not on purpose!”
“No! No, I just saw it because we’ve done it so many times, and…I think I can do it again. I just need some rest. Do—do you think I know a magical dance? Me?”
He was completely in denial, but Lyonette had seen it. Felt it.
“You don’t know the Aubrel Solde, do you?”
“The what? I wouldn’t know it if you served it at that restaurant.”
“Well, until I spoke, you did it, Ilvriss. You learned a magical dance! Get up! Get up so I can do it!”
He was stunned until Lyonette tried to drag him upright, begging to try it again. She just had to…
Unfortunately, her boots had run out of magic for half a day, and she collapsed. Ilvriss caught her in his arms, and she froze.
Oh no—
He lowered her onto the ground and dropped her.
“You’re too heavy to keep doing that.”
“Excuse you!”
Ilvriss rolled away as she kicked weakly at him. Laughing. In truth, Lyonette didn’t know quite what the dance did, only that Ilvriss had achieved it. She was envious, smiling, heart lifting, and he was laughing.
“Me. A magical dance. What, am I going to sleep and get the [Wacky Arms Dance]?”
“Shut—shut up, you knew it was amazing!”
“The [Erin Noodle Dance].”
He wheezed as he chuckled. He found Lyonette’s indignation hilarious. She was trying to sit up.
“I’m—damn boots! Don’t you dare name it that, Ilvriss, it was incredible! Do it again! You learned the dance, and I got nothing! If I hear that name when I get it—and I will get it—I’ll have Ser Dalimont stab you. Ser Dalimont, did Ushar record that?”
“Ah, she sadly tripped, Princess—”
“She what? You tripped her? Dalimont! I know she has to report to my mother. Let that Bagrhaven do what she wants. The recording!”
“I got most of it until this fool interrupted us, Princess. I missed the magical part. Ser Dalimont, once again sabotaging the Eternal Throne—”
“I was not informed recording Princess Lyonette was part of my duties!”
“No, because you’d be a pervert. I’m simply a faithful retainer. It’s Xinthe’s fault anyways.”
“Ah, indeed.”
“M-me? But what did I—?”
“It’s always your fault.”
“True, but don’t quibble on faults, Ushar! Someone get Mrsha! And Nanette! No, wait, not a word to them until I can do it or they’ll tease me! Don’t you dare tell them, Ilvriss. Ilvriss?”
The Drake was still laughing. He was holding his side, pointing at the [Princess] arguing with her servants, tears in his eyes. Actually, his laughter was so filled with hilarity it was getting insulting.
Lyonette was smiling, and she was about to start scolding louder since he deserved his victory, when the laughter’s tenor changed. She realized the tears in the corners of Ilvriss’ eyes were moving down his cheeks. And that the laughing…
Had turned into sobbing.
The three members of staff stopped arguing. Xinthe, Dalimont, Ushar, turned, and suddenly they stopped making sounds. Lyonette glanced around, but they had become part of the furniture. She saw the Wall Lord sitting there. Pressing the heels of his palms to his eyes.
“Ilvriss? What’s…wrong?”
He shook his head. Tried to speak. But he was hiccupping now. Tears were running from his eyes, and he sat there, covered in sweat, as she stood unsteadily.
“I—it’s nothing, Lyonette. What a fool…I’m sorry. I’m sorry. I just—it hit me.”
“What did?”
She sat down, a dress like a Faerie Flower puddling around her, and he had a simple maroon vest on. Dressed up for their silly hobby. He’d even put on a clashing pair of polka dot pants just to delight Nanette, light blue dots on dark orange he’d personally commissioned. The Wall Lord spoke.
“I just realized…I don’t want to die.”
The [Princess] froze, and the Wall Lord sat there. Wiping at his face.
“I’ve been having fun. Despite it all. Getting angry and happy and upset, and I thought—it just hit me. I was having fun today. Despite Georgie. I felt like I was actually changing my company. My father and I weren’t arguing much. I did the most un-Drake thing I had to. And I wanted to…”
“What—what makes you think you have to die?”
“My mission.”
That was all Ilvriss said, and Lyonette—froze. She knew what that was. He’d told her, in Oteslia.
The Necromancer.
But it was such a big, overwhelming secret he’d been working against that she hadn’t done more than think of it in her nightmares. Ilvriss, though, had been working nonstop the moment he’d found out. Even this dig was all for…
She glanced at Dalimont and Ushar, and they dragged Xinthe away. They stood at the doors as Lyonette whispered.
“I know what you mean, Ilvriss. But that doesn’t mean…”
“Yes. Yes, it does, Lyonette. I’m no idiot. I knew what I was signing up for. I was resolved to do it. Periss…but even with the Faerie Flower drink, some days I struggle to remember every detail about her. And I’m not angry anymore, Lyonette.”
“It’s hard to hold onto your anger, Ilvriss. Even with Nokha, I’d kill her, but I don’t lie awake at night wishing…”
Lyonette began, but Ilvriss just peered up wordlessly. Tears ran down his cheeks, stained slightly with glistening wetness. Shining in the faint light of the ballroom. She sat with him as he whispered.
“I know. But I don’t feel that hatred. And I looked at you, and it was so funny…I realized I was afraid. I’m just a coward. I’m afraid I won’t be able to fight him, Lyonette.”
“Don’t say that. You’ve done so much. Even when the Terlands sent Golems…”
She hugged him, chest constricting. Ilvriss just sniffed.
“I’m not a hero. Xesci seems to think I am, or could be. But I’m…just a Drake. Erin gave me a sword from her [Garden of Sanctuary]. And it’s just a sword. I can’t get it to flare with lightning.”
“It could be a bad sword. She’s not a good judge of…”
He plucked the sword from his belt and tossed it down.
“Not just that. I’m a Wall Lord of Salazsar, and I only got this far with General Sserys literally telling me where to dig. I’m no Zel Shivertail. I wasn’t worthy of standing with him. I’m going to fail. Fail Periss. Fail everyone. I’m more treacherous than Dramm is. I just…I’m scared, Lyonette.”
She said nothing. Just hugged him tightly.
“You’ve never, ever run away, Ilvriss. That’s what Mrsha says about Elia, you know. That’s why she likes her. And what brought me around. Elia doesn’t run. You haven’t, either. Even when I was in danger or…not when it matters.”
“I ran from the Hunter-Killer.”
He was trying to be contrary. She laughed at him.
“You silly prevaricator, you know what I mean. You don’t have to die, Ilvriss. This is…this is The Wandering Inn. Miracles happen.”
“Sometimes, they don’t. Then, Erin pays the price. I don’t know if I can do it, Lyonette. I don’t know if I’m worth what she is.”
Lyonette took one of Ilvriss’ neck spines and pushed down until he was blinking up at her.
“Don’t say that. Don’t put her on a pedestal! Or we’ll never reach her. Erin can’t be alone, Ilvriss. Too many people just buy into her legend. You never have. And neither have I. We have to catch her or she’ll be all by herself and think she has to save the world alone. And sacrifice herself to do it. We’ll catch her.”
“With magical dances? Digging holes in cowpats?”
“We’ll do it because we rise to every challenge. You remember what she was like. For every flag she waved, there were a hundred times we had to poke her into making cakes, or she was just playing chess and hanging out. Erin is the perfect, the ultimate example. If she can hit Level 50, so can anyone. You won’t die. I refuse to let you. Stop…stop crying.”
Lyonette felt tears sting her own eyes. She hugged Ilvriss, and his tears did stop. She felt his breathing stop jerking around in his chest. Then she realized she was holding him as they nearly fell back on the ballroom floor. Only his tail was keeping them upright.
“I’m sorry, Lyonette. I…I don’t know what came over me.”
“Mortality?”
He laughed, and she blinked down at him. Lyonette hesitated, and saw Ilvriss gazing up at her. She pressed her lips together, craned her head downwards—
He dumped her out of his lap. She leapt up—well, struggled up.
“I—”
“No, that was entirely inappropriate—”
“No, it was just—I can’t—”
“Let’s say no more of it, Ilvriss! We should go and—”
They stumbled towards the doors, red-faced. Neither one said a word as they blew past Ushar and Dalimont, who stood to attention like statues.
“Bath! Xinthe, we need to clean up!”
“Agreed. And I’m very embarrassed, but thank you.”
Ilvriss smiled at Lyonette, and she cleared her throat. Then Xinthe was leading and helping Lyonette towards the new bathing rooms that Hexel had made. It even, apparently, had showers designed specifically for Antinium.
Breathe in, out, mention the magical dance to the girls. Figure out how he did that and what it does—unpack the rest of that later. She could almost sense Ilvriss doing the same thing. Lyonette flung herself into the bathing room and let Xinthe help her strip her sweaty garments off. She hopped in the huge bathing pool area, which would be like the public baths in Liscor—and sighed as the hot water cleansed her skin.
“Wait, do I need to shower first, Xinthe?”
Lyonette only realized after a moment that she’d utterly broken protocol by entering dirty, but the servant called out.
“There’s no need to clean yourself first! The baths have a self-cleaning spell installed! Architect Hexel doesn’t trust children or your average bather.”
“Phew, what a relief. That’s Salazsar-standard, but you never know. The amount of mess Brilm makes—he gets in the tub with food. The monster. He does have his good qualities. Thank you, Miss Xinthe. I—”
Wall Lord Ilvriss strode over to the tub, utterly naked, and was levering himself down, groaning. Only then did he spot Lyonette, who was submerged up to her cheekbones in the water. He froze. Lyonette’s head emerged like a swamp-thing as Dame Ushar cracked the door open, and Xinthe hesitated.
“Er, I thought you said—”
Ilvriss exploded out of the water as Lyonette ducked underwater, then came up to scream.
“Ushar! Get Mrsha!”
But she was glad when she heard Ilvriss laughing, even ruefully.
 
——
