“The nose plugs don’t work, Marvus—”
“Hells!”
 
——
 
The lead pack not terrorizing the Golvas was sneaking its way across the Cowpat Plains. The grinning monsters with evil in their souls emerged from the tall grass into recently-grazed short grass, only to meet a small army of Lischelle [Ranchers] and the other family.
Their response? One dodged an arrow loosed at maximum range. The rest raised their tails, aimed up—and the wind blew the cloud of visible, purple-brown particulates straight towards the [Ranchers].
Demonsoul Skunks had once been used in war and were considered a war-crime. This was while War Manticores were an acceptable use of monsters in battle.
Demonsoul Skunks had once come to Chandrar and plagued Stitch-folk nations. Even if you removed your actual nose, you smelled them. They were rumored to stain the soul. They fouled fabric-flesh forever.
Demonsoul Skunks ate regular skunks whenever they found them. They were classified as ‘evil’ in some monster bestiaries while most monsters were normally classified as neutral, animalistic, interested in self-survival.
Marvus Lischelle was in full retreat when he saw the Drake hanging in the sky. He peered up, eyes watering, and Captain Osthia Blackwing hovered well above the cloud of fumes. Then he turned and saw Wall Lord Ilvriss.
The Wall Lord was marching at the head of a group of a hundred Drake [Soldiers] carrying a standard with Salazsar’s purple banner aloft. They bore some kind of pale yellow breastplate, though, and had weirdly conical helmets.
“The hell’s he doing? Is he mad?”
Marvus owed the Drake no favors, but he began to ride that way to tell the Drake to run. There was killing a man and being skunked until your descendants died. However, the Wall Lord halted a mile out of the skunk’s range.
They kept crawling forwards, pursuing the [Ranchers]. Marvus hadn’t a clue how to take them on. Traps? They’d maybe fall for them, but bear traps? And they were after live prey. Maybe Big Burlap could kill ‘em if he could stand the stench, but he’d be a goner…
Then he saw the Drakes and Gnolls come to a halt behind the Wall Lord. He pointed a sword which sparked for a moment, despite the clear skies, and they all slammed down the weapons they were carrying.
Spears. Fancy damn spears with gemstone tips. Yellow—topaz? Marvus didn’t own much jewelry aside from Ilvriss’ gift, and it spoke to him of Drake richness. An unimaginable city of gemstones, apparently.
Once, the Drakes slammed their spears down. Then twice in quick succession. A rhythmic motion, and he saw something—spark—between the hundred speartips. Like…Marvus had an image of a thunderstorm and lightning flashing down.
Electricity?
A fourth time the Erchirite Spears slammed their spears down, and Ilvriss’ sword arced with the same current. Marvus saw the Drakes halt for one second, then hoist their spears skywards with a shout. The lightning flashed up into the sky, and he flinched.
“What th—”
Thwoom.
The flash behind him lit up the Cowpat Plains as the lightning struck. Marvus felt the pressure on his back, whirled—and saw a hole in the ground. And not a lot of Demonsoul Skunks.
No—two of them were visible. One dragged itself away with blackened limbs, wandering around blearily. The other was twitching on the ground. They didn’t seem to understand what had happened. The first skunk looked around and then up. It raised its tail weakly, tried to shuffle off—
Osthia Blackwing twirled in the air as she flew over the skunk and spat an orb of acid down. It hit the Demonsoul Skunk, and it began to writhe, screeching. She fired a bow and hit the second skunk in the back and winged her way upwards.
“Dad. Was that—”
Burlise was open-mouthed. Marvus turned back, and the Wall Lord lowered his sword. He lifted a hand. Then, in the ringing silence, Marvus heard his voice.
“Erchirite Spears—advance!”
The company of the Gemstone Regiments of Salazsar advanced over the Cowpat Plains. A second group of [Riders] followed them, and Marvus Lischelle saw a [Mage] point a wand at the remains of the Demonsoul Skunks.
A [Fireball] flashed across the plains—and the Demonsoul Skunks exploded as the [Fireball] hit their musk and detonated the particulate.
Demonsoul Skunks’ musk was flammable.
Demonsoul Skunk parts were highly useful in some alchemical creations, but most [Alchemists] refused to work with them, even Saliss.
Demonsoul Skunks were not immune to lightning bolts.
 
——
 
The Erchirite Spears were having a great time. Ilvriss only had two active companies of the battalion he’d paid for from Salazsar, and they hadn’t done much. Getting punched by [Ranchers] was not what they were good at.
Firing long-range lightning at unsuspecting targets like the wrath of the heavens? He watched as one of their [Captains] breathed some lightning over his topaz-tipped spear, juicing it up for the next attack.
“Wall Lord, permission to drain our reserves?”
“Permission granted, Captain. I want those monsters slagged. Welsca, offer our [Mages] as dispellers for whatever part of the skunks’ musk is magical. Consult with Wistram about anti-scent spells.”
“Are you sure, Wall Lord? They’ve been nothing but a pain in our tails—”
One of his people complained, and Ilvriss feigned sniffing the air. He covered his noseholes—Ancestors, it reeked even from here!
“You want to keep smelling them? This place reeks bad enough. Oh, and someone cast a [Rain] spell to take down the risk of fire and smell. Company, advance. Also—would the audience remove themselves?”
He turned and glared at the little Gnoll girl eating popcorn and the rest of the watchers using the [World’s Eye Theatre]. However, the Erchirite Spears seemed very pleased as they began to slam the spears down as they marched forwards, charging up another attack.
“Oh come on, Ilvriss! This is great!”
“At least turn the music off, K—Randy. What is that?”
“Uh…just a really topical song. It’s called ‘Thunderstruck’.”
Ilvriss sighed. More Earther jokes. Then he saw the [Captain] glancing at him.
“Something to say, Captain Altaz? You get used to them.”
“Er, no, Wall Lord. But we’re fine with an audience. We show off our abilities all the time. And you can’t help having music, can you?”
Ilvriss stared at him, then around at the Erchirite Spears, then rubbed at his forehead.
“…Can you copy that onto a magic crystal, K—Randy?”
“Sure can, dude. Everyone needs a theme song.”
“Theirs is the Salazsarian National Anthem!”
“Sure, but this is better. Plus, someone said the Singer of Terandria’s dead, which would be—dude, that’d be horrible. Let’s not think about that. Hey Mrsha, turn it up!”
Ilvriss had to admit that when you cranked it loud enough, it sounded good. Especially when the Erchirite Spears timed throwing a lightning bolt to the drop of the song. He noticed Marvus Lischelle watching him and waved at the man.
Just because Ilvriss had good soldiers, it didn’t mean he wanted to use them. And the Erchirite Spears were only two hundred strong. He’d paid for a battalion, at great expense, but the rest of the soldiers were in reserve at the moment. He didn’t want to alarm anyone. Ilvriss watched them turn another group of bewildered Demonsoul Skunks into ash, and then someone breathed.
“Wall Lord Ilvriss…does Salazsar sell those spears?”
“Occasionally, to adventurers or anyone with a Class III weapons permit. This only really works en-masse and with training. Why?”
“C-can I buy some?”
Ilvriss turned and saw Lady Damia standing there, open-mouthed. He was about to say ‘absolutely not’, but then he just shrugged.
“Want to try using one? Go catch Captain Altaz. They let you try them at public demonstrations. I wanted to be one when I was young. Personally, I like the Lapis Shields for their adaptab—and she’s gone.”
He chuckled as Lady Damia went shooting after the Erchirite Spears. Then Ilvriss felt his scales crawling. He felt at the back of his neck, heard the music stop—and saw Mrsha drop her bucket of popcorn. She fumbled for her wand, dropped it, picked it up, and drew in the air. But in that way of horror and this kind of thing, he had to read her words first.
Ilvriss. Behind you.
Kevin managed a strangled sound, and Ilvriss turned, drawing his sword. He saw Damia spin and the Erchirite Spears whirl—the Rubirel Guard around him crossed halberds too late.
The giant Golem with a single eyeball stalk rising from its weird, thin body, standing on two telescoping legs stood a bit taller. Its hands were nothing more than long, thin fingers it was using to keep balanced. It had de-cloaked, and Ilvriss’ heart was thundering.
Not now. Why n—
“ᴡᴀʟʟ ʟᴏʀᴅ ɪʟᴠʀɪꜱꜱ ɢᴇᴍꜱᴄᴀʟᴇ. ʟᴀᴅʏ ᴜʟᴠᴀ ᴛᴇʀʟᴀɴᴅ ɪꜱ ᴀʀʀɪᴠɪɴɢ ᴀᴛ ɪɴᴠʀɪꜱɪʟ. ᴘʀᴇᴘᴀʀᴇ ʏᴏᴜʀꜱᴇʟꜰ.”
It spoke, voice flat and loud and—his scales crawled, and Ilvriss felt it all coming together. He managed a nod as the Rubirel Guard called for reinforcements.
“For war or peace?”
The Golem didn’t respond. Its legs extended, and then it stepped over him. It was forty feet tall now, and began striding north. Each step taking it impossibly far, like a half-Giant.
That—that was no normal Golem. The [World’s Eye Theatre] hadn’t spotted it. Even Ilvriss’ aura and his new precautionary spells—the Drake wondered if the Erchirite Spears could down the Golem if they knew it was there. Then he focused on what it had said.
Terlands had come. One of the Five Families had begun to move in force, and Lady Ulva Terland…
“Well, she’s known to hold a grudge. Miss Mrsha, would you kindly inform Lyonette that it’s time to panic or find a miracle? And ask my Uncle if he’s got anything, please.”
Then he straightened his back, adjusted his suit, and sighed.
It was time.
 
——
 
Lyonette du Marquin received a message from Nerul, terse and brief.
“I will try to make it. Engaging them now in negotiations. Your preference how you want to dance, but it’d better be a truce first. They’ve come for war.”
She sat there, then had a thought. She appeared in front of Ilvriss as he was galloping back to camp.
“Ilvriss?”
“Ahh! Don’t do that!”
“Sorry! Nerul is contacting them, but I’m told there’s dozens of Terlands, and Xitegen himself is riding with Ulva!”
“Is that positive? Negative? He knows you.”
“I think it’s positive? Ilvriss—how many cows did you steal from the Lischelles?”
“Three hundred? I expect them to be gone the next time Marvus comes by. Why? Do you have a cow-based superweapon or something?”
Lyonette favored Ilvriss with a smile.
“Nothing so fancy. I just had a thought. We’re in [Rancher] lands. The Lischelles can’t see the Terlands as anything but a threat, and we’ve tweaked their noses.”
“And shot their bulls. I don’t know how mad Marvus is, but I hope killing the skunks will warm him up.”
“Precisely. So…I propose we meet them on the most appropriate ground. I’m going to write an invitation. I’ll have Nanette and Mrsha deliver them personally. I want you to throw a cookout.”
“What?”
Ilvriss blinked at her, but then he nodded before she could explain.
“Of course. But will it offend the Terlands?”
“We can’t entertain them like they’ll want, which is Golems. And neither of us are northern Izrilians.”
“Fair point. But will they accept?”
“We’ll see. However, I think we have a chance. Lord Xitegen is with them, Ilvriss.”
“Right, and he doesn’t like a lot of what the inn stands for, and he trusts Drakes as much as he should—”
“Ilvriss, it’s Xitegen. He’s not going to miss a barbecue.”
Behind Lyonette, Mrsha slapped her forehead. That was her mother for you, assuming some people ran on their stomachs if they were even a gram over the weight Lyonette considered decent. On such things, the fate of everything rested.
 
——
 
“They want a what?”
Ulva Terland sat up in the carriage she was heartily sick of as Invrisil drew into view. Timekeeper spoke.
“As I believe that is a rhe-torical question, Lady Ulva, the location is out-side of the Cowpat Plains. Due to the smell. They have invited the local [Ran-cher] families. Landed nobility.”
“Upstarts? I heard they ran off the idiots who abandoned their lands. Well, well. We might as well kill multiple Crelers with one stone. Let’s appraise them. Any objections, Xitegen? Is it a trap?”
“I surely hope not, Great Aunt, because if that means fresh Lischelle meat, I will be there.”
She rolled her eyes.
“Is that some new breed of cattle? I don’t pay attention to food trends.”
“You’ve never had—? Great Aunt, are you serious?”
“I don’t pay attention to what we buy! We have local [Ranchers] in the north who cater to the nobility. Is this lot better?”
Xitegen kissed his fingers. Then pressed his fingers to the sides of his mouth and ran them down. After a few more nonverbal motions, Ulva shrugged.
“Tell the family to bring an appetite. And the poison-tester Golems. Those girls whom you said tried to deliver the invitation, Timekeeper—was one a Gnoll with white fur?”
“Yes, Lady Ter-land. They were quite alarmed when they were intercepted.”
“How does that work? You just broke the Skill or something?”
“They were re-layed into a holding matrix, Lady Ulva. It is disorienting to li-ving beings—”
“Beat your heart faster, I’m sick of your speech. Do you still have them?”
Timekeeper’s rate of speech accelerated, and the staccato quality didn’t vanish, but he sounded distinctly faster.
“Yes, Lady Terland. I believe they are screaming about the sight.”
“Hmm. Tell them we accept. The [Princess] will be there. And tell her to bring the Gnoll child. What’s the other one?”
“She claims to be a witch.”
“Bah. Busybodies. She’s optional.”
Ulva sat back, and her own heart beat faster. She was coming to reckon with this. She just had to wait…she noticed Xitegen watching her.
“What? Expecting them to win me over with the magical power of this inn and friendship, boy?”
“If anyone could resist the inn’s charms, it would be you, Great Aunt. It will be entertaining either way.”
She laughed at that. And the [Bereaved Matriarch of House Terland] turned and smiled once. At the rising star of House Terland. The boy whose levels exceeded almost any other [Lord]’s. Heir and successor and, perhaps, the leader needed for a new age.
She was just half of the last generation, old and bitter, and re-awakened. She bared her teeth at him as his smile faded.
“Hah. What level are you again?”
“Er, over Level 40, Great Aunt? I shouldn’t like to say unless I go higher, in which case, I will be throwing a celebration.”
“The House shall. Level 40? Good. We never have enough [Lords] and [Ladies] above Level 40. You’re sturdy enough now, you’ll live. Primera? Throw him out the carriage.”
“Er, Aunt, wh—”
The carriage door opened. There was a scuffle. Ulva leaned against the window and saw Xitegen Terland spin past her. The carriage continued racing on at top speed.
The door shut. The rest of the family stopped trying to get Ulva’s attention.
And a pair of girls who’d finally been allowed to see Ulva for a second swallowed hard.
Ulva Terland cometh.
 
——
 
“We have to attend, of course. That—we’re going to show them up.”
Aronia declared after five minutes of pacing. Marvus half-rose.
“Well, of course we have to do that. But the Terlands. What if they…?”
“We put on our Zenze best, Marvus, and we bow and act polite, but don’t promise or give anything away. Damn that Drake. We—I need to do some reading. Who knows the north? Who’s been up to First Landing or has any notion of the Terlands?”
“Only Colfa’s been that far north long. There’s Cousin Fibri who goes around selling—”
“Call him. Call everyone and the Golvas. No fighting, Marvus. We have to get ready!”
 
——
 
“L-Lady Ulva? In the south? She never leaves First Landing!”
Desinee El was stunned. Legitimately. This felt like a fever dream. She and Lady Damia exchanged a look.
They understood what it meant for Ulva Terland to come south. Everyone was used to Magnolia Reinhart, but she was young compared to Ulva. And…
“We have to do something. She’s going to kill Ilvriss!”
“We? I need to talk to Deilan. Excuse me—”
Damia grabbed Desinee’s waist as the older woman tried to struggle away from her.
“No, wait, you have to help! You were part of the Winter Solstice, right?”
“Let go of me, young [Lady]! I just came here to sell Kaalblades, and I was nearly eaten by a bull, and now the Terlands are marching on—do I know you? I have nightmares about the Winter Solstice!”
They were arguing as Wall Lord Ilvriss stood with Captain Osthia.
“Undead mostly destroyed?”
“Yessir. Just in time for the Golems to mince us all. Should I bring all our troops?”
“I very much doubt all our forces would be able to handle the Golems. Take the Rubirel Guard, and you’ll be there with your Sisters? It can’t come to a fight, Osthia.”
“Yessir, because we’ll lose, sir.”
“Exactly, good woman. Where’s Xesci? I don’t think she should be—”
“The message said to bring her, sir.”
“Right, well, let’s ask if she wants to actually come. It might not be the worst—Welsca, what’s wrong?”
The [Adjutant] came running, face dead white. Ilvriss turned as Damia and Desinee fought behind him, and Welsca appeared ready to vomit. But she had Clan Leader Plisce with her, and she delivered a single report with a piercing whisper-shout.
“Wall Lord! Brisket!”
“…Brisket?”
Everyone turned to stare at her, and Welsca gasped.
“Brisket…takes sixteen hours to prepare!”
Ilvriss checked the position of the sun. Then he turned.
“Send for Chef Calescent and Imani, and find someone who can speed-smoke…is that a Skill? And get me the best meat we have—”
He turned. The ‘meat’ mooed at him from one of the overfull pastures. Ilvriss pointed silently.
“Er, Lady Damia? Project for you.”
 
——
 
“You’d better be recording. Yes, yes, I know they’ll murder anyone who gets close, but please. A recording!”
Calidus Reinhart was begging the [Assassins] he’d sent. He just…he thought it’d be hilarious.
 
——
 
And so did the half-Elf. When he heard about it, he actually did a merry jig.
“Perfect! The stage is set! Now, what to wear, what to wear…”
He went running back home to put on his finest. He owed the [Princess] nothing less, after all. Terlands? He remembered their Golems, oh yes. He wondered how they’d changed.
If anyone remembered him…it would be them. After running a hundred miles, the half-Elf slowed.
“Wait. Aren’t the Terlands on Terandria? What are they doing down here?”
 
——
 
Lord Gorthes Reinhart had no plans on going to a barbecue. He just kept chasing the damn Unmarked Coach around—he’d run three teams of warhorses ragged trying to intercept the bastard [Driver] on her path.
He was, at this moment, apologizing for running someone over, a first. But then, Lord Xitegen had smashed into his coach after being hurled from his.
Gorthes was rather impressed the swearing [Lord] was actually alive.
 
——
 
“There will be no Goblins at the cookout. No objections. I’m sorry, everyone, but Ulva Terland and Lord Xitegen are, um, very biased.”
“…Do we get leftovers?”
“Yes!”
“Okay, then. That good with us, Miss Lyonette. You want us to set up, though?”
Mrsha listened to the shouting in the background as Calescent ripped a cabinet door open and reached into it with trembling claws.
He brought out his patented, Redfang-approved spice kit for barbecues with shaking claws. No one barbecued around The Wandering Inn. And his guests would be the House of Terland including the matriarch? He took a deep breath. In and out.
It was time, once more, to level.
Elia licked her lips as she peered into the kitchen. Lyonette was giving orders until Peggy asked a sly question.
“You putting on a nice dress maybe? Lord Ilvriss will be there and lots of fancy nobles. You and Miss Colfa?”
“I—well, it is Ulva Terland. She’s practically royalty in stature. Where’s Colfa? We should get our finest.”
Peggy smirked at a scowling Asgra, Nanette, and other members. She and Xinthe were Team Ilvriss, and the servant instantly went rushing to remind Lyonette of all the best shops in Invrisil and Pallass that could accommodate her. Lyonette ran off.
Mrsha?
Mrsha solemnly, and after great deliberation, adjusted her kilt. She put on a blue one with black stripes. Then she added a red bowtie and studied herself in the mirror.
“Mrsha! Looking good!”
Nanette gave her some finger guns and a wink as she went to join Lyonette’s dress prep. Mrsha eyed Nanette, then took the bowtie off.
 
——
 
They were all going to be there. It was like watching a bunch of wagons accelerating towards each other, [Drivers] activating Skills, and not one budging. A glorious disaster.
Nerul felt it. His class was screaming at him that there was more he didn’t know, but this upcoming meeting had so many high-level players and potential for permanent changes that it would be one of the top five negotiations in his life.
And he wasn’t sure he’d even make it. 
He was riding a Wistram Carriage towards Pallass, leaning out the window and shouting for people to get out of his way. Nerul was using Skills and swearing at every small obstacle.
He was late.
But he had no choice. He’d had to fight through a mountain of paper. He’d had to invent paperwork to fill out. And of course, even if he’d done all that—he’d needed to get gemstones custom-cut and fitted. Then have them inscribed with magic runes that had to be manually bored through the gemstones, enchanted with nigh on microscopic runes…
Not easy, even if you knew exactly what to do. Fortunately, once they’d finished an arm, the rest had gone faster. He turned his head.
“We’re not going to make it to Pallass on time. We have to begin—now. I’ve got a scrying orb, and we should be able to gain an audience. The Terlands have disembarked their carriages in Invrisil. Now’s the time to negotiate, if at all. I don’t know if they’re going to try and kill Ilvriss the moment they meet him. Do you…have any odds on the likelihood of that?”
The being sitting across from Nerul was still carving her other arm with a diamond-edged razor. She had taken over sculpting her body once they’d given her an arm. And claimed she’d have to re-do her arm since they’d done it so poorly. It would still, apparently, be substandard to the limbs she had possessed.
Still, she was so dangerous that the city had wanted to send a full escort of Rubirel Guards with Nerul. He’d refused because they’d slow the carriage, and because…they wouldn’t keep him from being killed if he was wrong.
Yweine’s head rose. Her face was that of a Drake.
Odd, for it to terrify him so much and unsettle him. But she had been made to serve the Walled Families.
Of course she’d been made to look like a Drake. Her face had mostly been intact, if damaged, but extremities had been lost; now the new gemstone stood out, appearing cheap compared to the fine, ancient materials she had been made with.
Nevertheless, she had legs, one arm, and a tail, which flicked every now and then next to her. So much like a real Drake—save for the translucent ‘skin’ of gems. Her scales were green, running to blue in places.
Like her neck spines, which were turquoise. But they were different. Instead of sticking out across the back of her neck, they drooped. Coming down the sides of her head like…hair. Like bangs, framing her features.
Some Drakes had neck spines that looked like that, but this…was not the Yweine from the records of her. She’d changed her appearance. Even the outfit she wore was different. She would be nude without clothing, but instead of an old Salazsarian serving outfit, she had requested armor.
Armor for the one Golem to leave the vaults. In service to Salazsar until the terms of her agreement were completed. How much could she be trusted?
She can be trusted as long as it serves her and the other Golems. Until such time as the contract no longer benefits her.
In a way, it made it all delightfully simple. Nerul listened to his racing heartbeat, like drums. Or was that [Dangersense]? But then Yweine spoke.
“I do not have access to the combined intelligence of the others, Diplomat. We are now within distant simulation-extrapolations. To allow for optimal thought, I have discarded potential outcome-projections. I am only personally aware of the Terlands from over a hundred years hence. Naturally, I have supplemented this data from reports and historical texts and will continue to do so, but these sources of data are inherently untrustworthy.”
“Fair. Well…how do you think this will go?”
“I would like your plan to succeed, Diplomat Nerul. It allows for the most efficient path to freedom for my people.”
She didn’t blink. Didn’t stop carving the runes in her arm even though she seemed to be studying him. Her hands adjusted for the slight rattle of the carriage moving.
Wistram, witness this. Nerul almost hoped they were watching and soiling their robes. But he spent only a moment knowing that he had changed things in the world. He took small pride in that. Then…he triggered the scrying orb, spoke, and waited.
It took time, of course, to make contact with the Terlands, even with Salazsar’s authority. But they paid attention. Nerul was put on hold as the Mage’s Guild in Invrisil realized who he was, then stopped wasting his time. Then he spoke to a Terland [Lady] Alorelle.
Haughty, recoiling at the mere sight of him, and clearly being tasked with checking on missives. A problem. Nerul waved at her.
“Hello, Lady Alorelle Terland! I’d like to request an immediate audience with Matriarch Ulva Terland—I am representing Wall Lord Ilvriss and Salazsar!”
“Lady Ulva is resting. She shall consider your request, Drake—”
Nerul sighed. He had angled the scrying orb so Yweine was behind him. The [Lady] was so focused on putting him down that she didn’t even notice Yweine.
Well, few Golems had such detailed visages. And he doubted they had many Drake Golems in House Terland. He pulled the scrying orb closer and saw her recoil.
“Excuse me, Lady Alorelle, but that was not a request. [Pay Attention]. It is, of course, Lady Ulva Terland’s right to refuse the meeting. Not yours.”
“How dare you! Salazsar does not order House Terland around. How—who is—who is that behind you?”
At last, Alorelle finally noticed what Nerul wanted, and he sighed again. He actually had to use a Skill to direct her attention…he liked semi-sharp people more than dunces or geniuses.
Alorelle’s eyes focused on Yweine, and the Golem turned her head. Her very eyes were made of gemstones, but each section different, to mimic the whites of an eye, the iris and pupil—when she opened her mouth, her diamond teeth moved with her jaw, just like a real Drake’s. She pulled at the armor and revealed a bared breast.
This time, Alorelle nearly leapt out of her seat—until the glow made her freeze. And there she saw it.
A heart. A literal heart of gemstones, pulsing with light. It radiated power as Nerul saw Yweine’s lips move upwards in a smile that was devoid of any true pleasure. When she spoke, it was with thought. A sentience so many were afraid to give Golems, because true sentience?
“I am Zelkyr’s creation, Lady Alorelle Terland. Tell Lady Ulva that once, one of the three daughters that Archmage Zelkyr ever made ran away. Just once. She begged the head of House Terland to take her in before the Archmage found her. Your histories would record that as proof of who I am. Hurry. The [Diplomat] threatens my existence.”
The [Lady] sat there a second and laughed. Nervously.
She licked her lips, and Nerul wondered how she had ever married a Named-rank adventurer and stayed so…
It must be an unhappy marriage, just as rumored. Poor Eldertuin.
“This is a trick. A joke. You can’t be—”
Yweine leaned forwards, and her voice boomed.
“RUN, LADY ALORELLE.”
The [Lady] ran. Someone dropped the scrying orb. Nerul sat back and saw a white-faced [Mage] scrambling to pick it up.
“Er—er—Lady Alorelle has left the building, Diplomat. Should I, um…?”
“I’m sure we’ll receive a direct call. Please forwards my scrying orb’s identifier to House Terland if they inquire.”
His heart was beating so hard. Nerul smiled at the [Mage]. The man licked his lips.
“Yes, Diplomat, of course. May I ask—?”
“No. Goodbye.”
Nerul turned the scrying orb off. It was a very good one. Salazsar-made, not the kind of thing you could hack and scry through if you were Wistram.
“Well, that did that. How good are you, Yweine? The best? Is that why they elected you to go with me?”
“I was considered the most appropriate for a range of outcomes.”
“Combat being one of them? Or both combat and thought?”
She went back to carving her arm, then put it aside.
“Wistram is attempting to take control of your scrying orb.”
Her eyes focused on it, and Nerul saw an incoming [Scrying] spell request. It even said ‘Wistram Academy, Urgent’. He went to refuse, and Yweine caught his hand so fast he jerked.
“If you refuse, it will activate the spell, and they will begin recording.”
“Ah. That’s unpleasant. Let me just write a—you know what? [Emergency Communications: Dragonspeaker Luciva].”
Nerul screamed for a few seconds, then sat back. Someone else’s problem, lovely. He watched the request pulse a few times.
“Well, Lady Ulva’s going to call us, and I think I have to deal with this before I accept her call. What do I do to avoid being recorded?”
A smile, again, without mirth.
“Accept it.”
He rolled his eyes.
“Damned [Mages]. Someone was definitely patting themselves on the back when they came up with that one. And yanking themselves off under their robes.”
“Yes.”
He hit the ‘yes’ grumpily, and Yweine sat there as a rather harried-looking man appeared on the scrying orb.
“Diplomat Nerul, I am Archmage Eldavin. Did I just receive the correct wording on that [Message] spell? Damned filtering systems sometimes—”
He spotted Yweine and froze. Nerul supposed the truth was going to leak after the Terlands—if it hadn’t already. Hence why she hadn’t bothered to lean out of the way. He smiled.
“Hello, Archmage Eldavin? Delighted, delighted. Please stop trying to hack my scrying orb. I’d love to talk, but if I’ve not been killed by tomorrow, I should have some time for you.”
“Is that a—you idiots kept—now, see here! Firstly—”
Nerul turned the scrying orb off and, after some fiddling, blocked Wistram Academy. He sat back. Then he saw the scrying orb light up and an obnoxious ring tone play.
Lady Ulva, House Terland.
“…Anything I’ve missed? Are you on our side or is the next few minutes going to be you holding up my head and negotiating safe passage north?”
He turned, and the Golem smiled at him.
“Diplomat Nerul, we have struck an agreement. Your Skills enforce such matters.”
“Yes. And you don’t love Salazsar or my nephew.”
He met her eyes, unable to read her face. Yweine’s head tilted coquettishly. Then she leaned forwards.
“Diplomat. To reassure you, I remind you that willingly or not, I am Zelkyr’s creation. Sons and daughters he had no regard for nor knew existed, even when we told him.”
“Yes. And this makes me think you’re on our side how?”
Her cold eyes seemed to even sparkle.
“We are still what we were raised to be. Drakes. Golem Drakes. Do you think we trust House Terland unconditionally? There are no Zelkyr-Golems in House Terland.”
Nerul thought about that.
“He didn’t like Humans. But after he vanished…over a century and no Zelkyr-Golems? Hm. You’d imagine…one of the Truestone Golems sought sanctuary there. So other Golems would have, even if they didn’t know that. What happened to them?”
The Golem sat back.
“They took us apart to make their Golems.”
The [Diplomat] studied her and then, abruptly, relaxed. He knew she had told him that to make him feel better, but it was enough.
“Ah. Well, let’s see how this goes.”
He sighed and touched the scrying orb. Then he stood and bowed.
“Lady Ulva Terland. I am Diplomat Nerul Gemscale. On behalf of Salazsar, greetings. Prosperity be yours eternal. I apologize for the setting; I should have liked to meet you in person, but I had great fear that my nephew, Wall Lord Ilvriss, would be dead before I got there. May I have a moment of your time?”
When his head rose, he saw Lady Ulva Terland sitting on a floating dais, face cold, eyes appraising him. Everyone else in the room, some kind of mansion the Terlands owned no doubt, was staring at Yweine. She had put aside her arm and stood so light shining through one of the carriage window slits caught her gemstone body and illuminated it.
But Ulva just spared Yweine one glance and focused on Nerul. He felt her aura push him across the world.
Ancestors shit on me, she’s well and truly out of her depression. She spoke.
“You have won it, Diplomat. I am House Terland. Where Golems march, we follow. Who stands there with you?”
Diplomat Nerul gestured to the side, still half-bowed as if he were addressing royalty.
“May I have the dubious honor of presenting Golem Yweine? She was in service to Salazsar during the era of Archmage Zelkyr.”
He felt at his vest pockets, preparing a little mirror as the Terlands exclaimed, gasped, and Ulva’s eyes narrowed. Here came the dance, and it was going to be…straightforwards. Knife out.
For the throat.
He smiled as his eyes flicked up. Ulva Terland just…shrugged.
“So?”
Ancestors, he liked her for that.
 
——
 
“Ulva. Ulva, I think that’s really—”
Old Nouzcrat was trembling so bad that when he leaned over he nearly fell on her. Timekeeper steadied him, and Ulva sighed.
“We’ll find out. Be silent. All of you.”
Her family was tittering around her like a flock of frightened birds. That idiot, Alorelle, had come back shouting what she’d seen. Which meant they’d insisted on being part of this meeting.
Just as that Drake intended. She locked eyes with Diplomat Nerul. He was apparently Salazsar’s best. She didn’t doubt it, if this was the trump card he’d brought out. She couldn’t read his face; she didn’t have to. She just had to know herself and watch to see how he tried to move her.
Ulva gestured, and Nouzcrat was moved aside, gently, but another fool took his place.
“Great Aunt—if this is a Zelkyr-Golem, then Salazsar might have more.”
She actually turned her head to stare at Ramone Terland.
“That is obvious. Water is wet. This is an attempt to save Wall Lord Ilvriss’ sorry hide. You are forbidden from speech. Timekeeper, remove the next fool who says something that wastes my time.”
“Yes, Lady Terland.”
The voices grew quieter, but Lord Xitegen walked forwards. He had a lot of bandages on his body from being tossed from the coach. She felt vaguely bad about that, but it had been a good lesson to him about something. Physics, if nothing else.
“May I offer, respectfully, Great Aunt, that we need proof? Which is an obvious statement I am making for the benefit of the family? Before we jump to throw our hands up in the air.”
Her eyes flicked to Alorelle and the other Terlands in such a frenzy. Ulva nodded, grimacing.
“Timekeeper, is that a Zelkyr-made Golem?”
Her servant responded instantly as Nerul blinked. He began to speak, and she drew a finger through the air—he shut up. Smart man.
“It is not possible to judge at this distance, Lady Ulva.”
“Increase your heartbeat until you have a conclusion if it is possible.”
The whirring in his body intensified. The Golem’s voice grew faster, again, and more precise. Something else entered his voice. A touch of…
Emotion? Xitegen twisted, and Ulva’s lips curled. This was the power of a Terland. The true power she did not activate often. If Petria were alive, and they both used the Skill—then Xitegen would have seen stone truly wake. Emotions were enough, even a hint, to still the Terlands.
“If…she is a copy, she is an adept one. Her body is recently repaired, Lady Ulva. Fracture points are consistent with magical gemstone fault lines. There are five markings visible on her body, one just below her left cheek, that bear Zelkyr’s signature icon.”
Ulva squinted, and Timekeeper continued.
“It is not visible with the Human eye. Her posture and reaction time are in excess of mine without your Skill. Every 0.027 seconds, her body moves in a default resting pattern that cycles through six different resting animations. Each scale of her original body appears to articulate with movement. These qualities indicate she is beyond the manufacture of modern Golem artificing. It is possible she is illusory. However, the refraction of light across her body would require a Level 50 [Illusionist] to copy given the speed the carriage is moving at. Furthermore, she is attempting to communicate with me, Lady Ulva, by using the refraction of light along her neck spines. I present better-than-equal odds she is authentic based on available data.”
At this last comment, even Nerul turned to glance at Yweine. If she had been subtly altering the light across her neck spines, it was not visible now. Likely, it had not been to the naked eye, even before Timekeeper had said that.
Ulva just chuckled as she held out a hand and a drink was placed into it. She sipped.
“She forgets loyalty was the first thing we installed in all Golems, Timekeeper? Don’t answer that. There’s your proof, Xitegen. A real, Zelkyr-Golem. Sentient-class, no less. One that is not under Salazsar’s control. You kept pieces when they rebelled, didn’t you? Naughty Drakes.”
She wondered how many they had and if any other cities had these Golems. Nerul smiled.
“We’re all rascals this side of the High Passes, Lady Ulva. If I may—”
“Your nephew stopped my heart, and his agent took my sister’s face. He will answer for both those affronts.”
The Terlands stirred, some in horror, not having known either part. Nerul’s eyes flickered—then he replied so fast she was impressed.
“You did try to murder him first.”
“He was warned.”
“Lady Ulva, is this the time for naked hostility between us? With the Antinium? With the New Lands?”
He spread his arms as best he could in the carriage. Ulva snapped back, amused.
“The Antinium are your problem. You’re the ones who’ve lost the tribes. As for hostility, Manus sent their agents north.”
“I hate to mention that Lord Tyrion—”
She leaned forwards, and he shut up as her aura made the Golem Hearts around the room flash. There was an echoing flash from inside Yweine’s body, and the Golem just—twitched.
Just for a second. She didn’t react otherwise, but Ulva’s lips curled up. The Golem didn’t know Ulva’s aura could do that to her. 
“That was Veltras. You seem to forget how this works, Diplomat, so I shall spell it out for you. Veltras went against the Goblin Lord. Veltras made a plan to siege Liscor. Reinhart stopped him. Manus struck all the north because that’s what Manus saw. It’s well they never went further than around Invrisil, for they would have angered the others. Veltras made war against Ailendamus, with support, blessings, aye. But just that. Now, Terland has come down to you. And we are not…Veltras. You deal with us. If the five of us come down south because it is time, then you’ll have an actual war. Now, address House Terland, and be quick about it.”
She sat back, and Diplomat Nerul bowed his head slowly.
“I humbly beg your forgiveness, Lady Ulva. I forgot my lessons—”
“Stop kowtowing. Speak, Salazsar.”
His eyes rose, and they flashed. Nerul straightened, and she had to admit he looked more fit than she’d assume for a [Diplomat]. Thighs reminiscent of Xitegen’s.
“Very well. I point out merely that Salazsar has only come north to dig. In lands we used to own. Without malice, without blades. You sent a Golem against us, and you were, unfortunately, a casualty of defeating it. One does not stick their hand in a beehive and complain overlong, I feel. Can we agree to civil discussions?”
He went from effusive to direct as a Golem shoving you in a second. The family muttered—Ulva just bared her teeth.
“You’re only here for the novelty of it. I have a bone to pick with the [Princess] and Wall Lord. I make no promises.”
At this point, someone broke in.
“Great Aunt! What about the Golem? She’s Zelkyr-made! She’s—ulp—”
Timekeeper had picked up the idiot and was ejecting them, but the hubbub grew so loud that Ulva turned her lead.
“Silence! It’s one damn Golem, and that’s why he brought her!”
“Great Aunt, she’s a work of art! She’s the only Sentient-class Zelkyr Golem remaining and—”
“And? I’ve seen better.”
Gasps. Yweine’s face didn’t change, but Ulva grinned.
“Didn’t like that, did you, Golem? I’m not my family. Zelkyr was gifted at form and sentience, and he made Truestone Golems. He wasn’t the only artificer Terland has known. There are better in our vaults.”
She refused to take any bait, but Yweine’s reply was that too-swift wit that happened when you gave Golems too much intelligence. As if that made better servants. It didn’t make anyone happier, master or creation.
“Your experience is evident, Lady Ulva Terland. I have consulted records, and your advanced age for a Human indicates you would have been a girl when Archmage Zelkyr’s authority was still somewhat intact, despite his absence. You would also have been present for the apparent death and reanimation of Archmage Perril Chandler, the Archmage of Death. May I confirm these statements?”
Ulva felt her skin crawl. She snapped back.
“He is called Az’kerash now. He was executed before I was born. I knew him only as the Necromancer, and I saw him destroying his way south. Nothing more than a monster. What is your point?”
Yweine nodded once.
“In that case, Lady Terland, I would remind you that Zelkyr’s knowledge, techniques, and stores of materials have not all been found. Moreover, my creation is not one of a High Magic era. It is, therefore, reproducible and above current Terland capabilities by several factors.”
Not bad. Ulva could nod at that, but she thought the argument played better with everyone else. She drummed her fingers on the armrest of her Dais of Health.
“If you won’t give it to Salazsar, or they haven’t used it, you don’t have it. I recognize your make and model. You’re a serving Golem. I believe you were Sentient-class. So what? Illivere has the ‘Oracle of Elbe’. I met it once as a girl. Petria said it was the first time she’d met a Golem dumber than she was.”
Heads swung back her way, and some were admiring. Even Nerul’s—they’d never seen someone go up against a Sentient-class Golem. Never met them. Ulva sighed. Children.
“We possess information about Archmage Zelkyr, Lady Ulva. We are a collective.”
This time, Yweine made a mistake, and Ulva spat her drink back into her cup. Threw her head back and laughed mockingly.
“Now I know you’re just a bunch of odd misfits, not some super Sentient-class Golems. Ten Clay Golems are a collective. Give them a wide enough set of parameters and enough time and they’ll get up to something. What do you have to tell us the others didn’t? That Zelkyr was a worthless fool, even for a Drake? Diplomat, I’m tempted to let children talk to children.”
He adjusted his suit as Yweine leaned back, and Ulva could tell she’d annoyed the Golem.
“Delightful as it would be to cross swords with one of Terland’s Virtues, Lady Ulva, I’m afraid I’m but a hatchling in front of you.”
“Most are. But your Golem has nothing for us that it hasn’t given Salazsar—”
Yweine spoke too fast, too loud, a crack in her normal, steady tone.
“We…are imprisoned in Salazsar. We have given them nothing. There are twenty-six of us. The [Diplomat] has signed an agreement for our cooperation.”
Ulva’s eyes narrowed. Nerul half-turned, then went still. He made a rueful expression, and she watched his tail, but it didn’t twitch.
Well now, that changed some things. Her family was getting uppity again. Ulva turned.
“Timekeeper, have one of the spellcasting Golems [Mass Silence] everyone but Xitegen and I.”
“Done, Lady Terland.”
That worked. Xitegen stepped forwards.
“Diplomat, as entertaining as this is, couldn’t this wait for the delightful-sounding cookout? I’ve never been to one, and my Great Aunt is not a woman to change her mind until she herself wishes to.”
“I’m simply here to try to effect a peaceful resolution to this regrettable conflict, Lord Xitegen. That is my class.”
“Or to deliver the redsoaked flag of war. What else could you possibly tell me to stay my hand? If I find the Wall Lord obtrusive, I will remove him. If I find reason, I may give him a chance to run. He is in the north, and there are lines you do not cross.”
Nerul’s jaw clenched for the first time as Ulva pressed at him, and he held his ground.
“Magnolia Reinhart went south.”
“Magnolia Reinhart was invited.”
They locked gazes until Nerul backed up a step in the coach. Sat down.
“Very well. I’ve said my piece, and I do hope to make it to the cookout. I’ve brought some famed Salazsarian pork—the best I’ve ever had. If we come to blows, we come to blows, Lady Ulva. Just so you know, in that regrettable circumstance, Lord Eschowar is back.”
Ulva went cold for a second, and she remembered him. She whispered.
“Enjoying that, are you?”
Nerul glanced at Yweine. Then smiled.
“As much as any man when a giant wakes. And I was born for a more peaceful age, despite all these disagreeable wars. Now, Lady Ulva, we know where the other stands. We know whom the other has brought—”
He nodded at Timekeeper and Yweine, who regarded each other steadily.
“—And we know what we stand to lose.”
“There are more Golems in the vaults. They’re meant to die before Terlands. Had I the choice, I would trade one for each Wall Lord in the south.”
Actually, she wouldn’t because that would be a damn waste of Golems; there were always new Drakes to fight. But it sounded hostile and imperial, and Ulva saw Nerul grimace ever-so-slightly. Here is my position, and now show me how you’ll move me from it. She waited, and he exhaled.
“What I meant, Lady Ulva, was what we had to lose. And in our case, Salazsar has fallen out of love with making Golems. It would be regrettable, but that is my offer.”
Her eyes flickered. Did he mean…? Xitegen focused on Nerul, Yweine, and then glanced at Ulva and around the room.
“He’ll have the rest of the Golems destroyed if we kill Wall Lord Ilvriss.”
The [Lady] lost her temper in a second.
“Timekeeper, eject Xitegen through a damn window! Clear the room!”
Silent pandemonium. Timekeeper lumbered at Xitegen, who just…ran for it since he’d learned his lesson. The rest of the Terlands? They were still [Silenced], so it was like an avalanche of [Mimes], throwing their hands up, screaming, running towards her to beseech her—
“Get back. Stop snivelling! Golems, clear the room!”
Ulva cursed and rose, and Golems herded her family away from her. She spun, saw Nerul smiling, and then Yweine’s desperate expression locked on hers. She made it behind Nerul, just for a second, and mouthed at Ulva.
…please…give…?
Ulva didn’t read lips. She hoped like Rhir’s hells that Timekeeper had seen it and would tell her what had been said. Then she turned the scrying orb off and began to shout. And part of her admired the move.
Damn the Drake! He had never been after her, but the family. He’d even gotten Xitegen; the boy still loved Golems.
Destroy every last one of Zelkyr’s remaining Golems in the City of Gems. Mutually assured destruction, only Nerul had pointed the crossbow at his own city. Very clever.
She just thought he’d made a huge tactical error. Yweine was a Sentient-class Golem. Those loved their own hides. When the last wailing Terland was finished being pushed out of the room, throwing a tantrum and screaming at Ulva, she massaged her temples.
“Timekeeper, what did Yweine say?”
“Lady Ulva, her message to me was a plea for assistance and questions. If you wish the full transcript, I shall provide it. If you are referring to her final message to you, it is: ‘Lady Ulva, don’t make war. Please give us sanctuary.’”
Sanctuary. Ulva’s right eye snapped open.
“She wants to leave. He must have promised her something. She wants to leave, and the condition is that I avoid killing the Wall Lord. Interesting.”
She sat back in her chair. She didn’t like all this damn pushing. From Nerul, from even her dead sister—yes, she’d been overjoyed to see Petria.
But she had some nerve to tell Ulva to forgive Drakes. The [Matriarch] of House Terland sat there.
“This changes nothing. I’ll weigh it along with everything else, but I will meet that Drake. If you sense any double-crossing, just kill them, Timekeeper.”
“Yes, Lady Terland.”
“…How good is that Golem?”
He took time to respond; his heart was beating slower now that he wasn’t as needed.
“Unknown, Lady Terland. If she has freed herself from her restrictions, she is more adaptive than her counter-parts I have battled. She would possess greater spell-abilities than I. But be substantively less sturdy.”
“Put it in terms I understand. Modern-day Gold-rank or Named-rank?”
“…At least high-Gold, Lady Ulva. Likely better.”
“Hmph. Final question, Timekeeper, then we’ll go spank some children.”
“Yes, Lady Terland?”
She leaned over.
“What is a cookout? Some kind of country meeting? What do they serve?”
 
——
 
When the scrying orb turned off, Nerul sat back then immediately got a dozen [Message] spells from the Terlands who had been having opinions throughout the call. Then more. He’d answer them…
“I can answer some of the [Message] spells in your tone, Diplomat. I merely require a sample of your correspondence.”
Yweine spoke, seemingly cool and unfazed by the showdown with one of the most powerful women in the world. Nerul eyed her, then he began to chuckle. And laugh.
“Yweine, that was—I think we got her! What do you think?”
He leapt up and began to pace the tiny carriage, thinking. The Golem sat.
“I believe Lady Ulva was unconvinced until the end, but she accepted the falsehood that you are threatening the lives of my fellow Golems.”
And that—that was the point. Nerul wiped at his brow, which was perspiring as if he was lifting weights.
“Ancestors, let’s hope so.”
Because that was the lie. The only one he’d told in a meeting of truths. Nerul’s plan had been simple enough in conception; complicated only made too many fault points.
Ulva Terland was used to Golems. She wouldn’t be impressed by even Yweine, he suspected. The entire Golem body might be able to throw Ulva Terland, but he’d accounted for her being able to go toe-to-toe with Yweine.
So he’d made himself the weak point. The villain. The conditions of his pact with the Golems of Zelkyr were simple: they helped him and didn’t cross him, he didn’t destroy them.
However, the Terlands loved Golems by and large. Even if Ulva didn’t, the best way to make them more valuable to her was for her to think they had no loyalty to Salazsar. Hence, Yweine claiming their fates rested on Ilvriss not being killed.
Which they didn’t. Nerul had strongly intimated to the Golems that Ilvriss’ death was an unacceptable outcome, but threatening the Golems with life-or-death over that outcome had struck him as a good way to get them trying to escape or turncoat.
All he needed was the lie.
All he needed was the Terlands to think it was what was going to happen.
“We’ve thrown them out of hate and into thinking, Yweine. That’s the outcome of all this work. All we have to do is stop an angry mob of Terlands from descending on this cookout. Ulva Terland is hard to shift; high levels always are. But if her family starts listening, thinking, then I’ve set up Ilvriss and Lyonette for a leg-sweep. Let’s hope they learned that in dance practice. Now…we just have to reach there. Excuse me if I lie down and become a nervous wreck.”
“Not at all, Diplomat. I believe you have achieved an optimal outcome. I shall take over the steering of this carriage. I believe Wistram is attempting to do so to arrange a meeting with the Archmage of Memory.”
So saying, Yweine punched a hand into the side of the magical carriage, and it shifted. Nerul realized they’d been going away from the road. He grunted, then lay down.
Intelligent Golems on one side, army of them on the other. My nephew in between with a smoked brisket. There was nothing left for it now, he just caught some sleep with the practice of a true negotiator.
When he got out of the carriage, it would be time for the cookout. And then they’d see. Nerul sighed.
After a moment, he pulled out a packet of carefully sealed Salazsarian bacon and began to eat it, raw. Because he was damn hungry.
“Salazsarian bacon. No one talks about that until they’ve had it. Then—Ancestors, that’s the stuff. They don’t even sell this unless it’s a big event. Can’t beat it.”
“This has been true since Zelkyr’s era, Diplomat.”
“Ancestors, really? We really are the best city.”
Then he put his head down and passed out.
 
[Chief Diplomat of Bijou Splendor Level 49!]
[Skill – Grand Diplomacy: Move the Hearts of All That Think Obtained!]
 
[Title – He Who Negotiates With Golems Obtained!]
[Title Reward – Brooch of Golem Speech Granted!]
 
 
 
 
Author’s Note:
Did you know earwigs bite? I mention this because an earwig bit me the other day. I thought they were just annoyingly gross, but now it’s war.
I realize that first I mention the mouse issue (haven’t seen any more signs of them so far), and now I’m making it out like I’m under assault by a plague. It’s more like a few per week in the summer, but I don’t…like…intruders in my house. Especially ones that bite.
Now, mandatory pirateaba crisis over. Let’s address a few interesting things.
 
Firstly, a minor note I did not explain in-chapter fully is Prince Dafranail, the one who got drop-kicked and who’s going around backhanding peasants. You may think he’s an asshole.
But also his Skill isn’t just for show. [See it My Way] is a version of Nerul’s Skill that acts on entire cities. Arguably less powerful since that was Nerul’s Level 40 capstone, and Dafranail is lower-level, but it’s like a plague of thought.
If you’re not someone like Deilan El or the El family who instantly resists and takes umbrage with Dafranail, and he gets away with his actions even with mild censure, people start to come around to his point of view. It’s a pernicious power that royalty can get. But it does not confer immunity to having your teeth mashed into the side of a metal machine, so I just wished to elaborate since I don’t always talk about Skills. But like the Grand Design (Second Edition), it’s fun. For the sake of the plot, I don’t do it as much. Maybe I should, for a few chapters.
 
Second is Advance Miner Georgie. He’s a funny guy and not a great dude. There were some concerns in my stream-chat about representation with him being, well, sort of an asshole, but I have asked representatives of the transmasc community to read and comment and taken feedback on board.
Every experience is different, and opinions may vary, but Georgie is, well, the result of a lot of factors that make him act the way he does. Including himself, but he’s acting like other [Miners] in their culture. He’s far from perfect but that’s part of the point; ‘representation fails when people are cookie-cutter perfect.’ I’m quoting that, there.
I’m definitely interested in opinions, but I just wanted to note that I did try to listen and pay attention, like I did with the Saliss-Onieva chapters and other times.
 
Whew, that’s it. I’m gonna go back to paranoidly searching for earwigs and look forward to one last huge chapter. I’m not splitting these up, large as they are, but I may need a break after I round this out because I’m burning my backlog and energy to write this much. But I think it’s a fun arc and hope you enjoy the ending. One more chapter. It’s gonna be big. Like indoor pl—I have never stopped thinking of that quote for at least two decades. I don’t know why.
—pirateaba
